of $E_1, \ldots, E_r$; two orthonormal bases of the same $m_k$-dimensional
space differ precisely by right multiplication by an element of $O(m_k)$.

2. This is the Courant--Fischer min-max theorem
(\citealp{Horn_Johnson_2013}, Section 4.2).
The convexity of $\lambda_1$ and the concavity of $\lambda_p$ follow
because $\bm{S} \mapsto \bm{v}^T\bm{S}\bm{v}$ is linear for each fixed
$\bm{v}$, and a pointwise supremum, respectively infimum, of linear
functions is convex, respectively concave.

3. Put $\bm{M} := \bm{K}^T\bm{U}$, which again satisfies
$\bm{M}^T\bm{M} = \bm{I}_q$, and let $c_j := \sum_{k=1}^q M_{jk}^2$ be the
squared norm of its $j$-th row, that is the $j$-th diagonal entry of the
rank $q$ orthogonal projector $\bm{M}\bm{M}^T$;
hence $c_j \in [0,1]$ and $\sum_{j=1}^p c_j = \tr(\bm{M}\bm{M}^T) = q$.
By \eqref{eq:spectral_decomposition},
$\tr(\bm{U}^T\bm{S}\bm{U}) = \tr\bracket{\bm{M}^T\text{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_p)\bm{M}}
= \sum_{j=1}^p \lambda_j c_j$.
Using $c_j - 1 \leqslant 0$ and $\lambda_j \geqslant \lambda_q$ for
$j \leqslant q$, and $c_j \geqslant 0$ and
$\lambda_j \leqslant \lambda_{q+1}$ for $j > q$, we get
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{j=1}^p \lambda_j c_j - \sum_{j=1}^q \lambda_j
  = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j(c_j-1) + \sum_{j>q}\lambda_j c_j
  \leqslant \lambda_q \sum_{j\leqslant q}(c_j-1) + \lambda_{q+1}\sum_{j>q}c_j
  = -\bracket{\lambda_q - \lambda_{q+1}}\sum_{j>q}c_j
  \leqslant 0,
\end{equation*}
where the last equality used
$\sum_{j\leqslant q}(c_j-1) = -\sum_{j>q}c_j$.
This is \eqref{eq:ky_fan}.
If $\lambda_q > \lambda_{q+1}$, the displayed chain shows that equality
forces $c_j = 0$ for every $j > q$, that is, the last $p-q$ rows of
$\bm{M}$ vanish, that is, the column space of $\bm{U} = \bm{K}\bm{M}$ is
contained in $\text{span}(\bm{k}_1,\ldots,\bm{k}_q)$, hence equal to it by
equality of dimensions.
Conversely, if the two spaces coincide then
$\bm{U} = (\bm{k}_1,\ldots,\bm{k}_q)\bm{R}$ for some $\bm{R} \in O(q)$ and
$\tr(\bm{U}^T\bm{S}\bm{U})
= \tr\bracket{\bm{R}^T\text{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q)\bm{R}}
= \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j$.

4. Write $\bm{E} := \bm{T}-\bm{S}$.
For every unit vector $\bm{v}$ we have
$\bm{v}^T\bm{T}\bm{v} \leqslant \bm{v}^T\bm{S}\bm{v} + \lambda_1(\bm{E})$,
so taking minima over a $k$-dimensional subspace and then the maximum over
such subspaces in \eqref{eq:courant_fischer} gives
$\lambda_k(\bm{T}) \leqslant \lambda_k(\bm{S}) + \lambda_1(\bm{E})$;
exchanging the roles of $\bm{S}$ and $\bm{T}$ gives
$\lambda_k(\bm{S}) \leqslant \lambda_k(\bm{T}) - \lambda_p(\bm{E})$.
Hence
$\abs{\lambda_k(\bm{T})-\lambda_k(\bm{S})}
\leqslant \max\bracket{\abs{\lambda_1(\bm{E})}, \abs{\lambda_p(\bm{E})}}
= \|\bm{E}\|$,
the spectral norm of a symmetric matrix being the largest modulus of its
eigenvalues.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:pca_variational}
Part 3 of Lemma~\ref{thm:spectral_facts} is the reason the word
\textit{principal} appears in the name of the model.
For any $\bm{U} \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times q}$ with
$\bm{U}^T\bm{U} = \bm{I}_q$ the matrix $\bm{U}\bm{U}^T$ is the orthogonal
projector onto the column space of $\bm{U}$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reconstruction_identity}
  \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \|\bm{X}_i - \bm{U}\bm{U}^T\bm{X}_i\|^2
  = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{X}_i^T\bracket{\bm{I}_p - \bm{U}\bm{U}^T}\bm{X}_i
  = \tr(\bm{S}_n) - \tr\bracket{\bm{U}^T\bm{S}_n\bm{U}} :
\end{equation}
minimizing the mean squared reconstruction error over $q$-dimensional
subspaces is the same as maximizing the retained empirical variance
$\tr(\bm{U}^T\bm{S}_n\bm{U})$, and by \eqref{eq:ky_fan} the optimum is
$\sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)$, attained exactly at the span of
the $q$ leading eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ as soon as
$\lambda_q(\bm{S}_n) > \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_n)$.
Note also that part 1 already contains, in embryo, the non-identifiability
that forced the quotient construction of Section~\ref{sec:basic_setup}:
what the data determine is the leading eigen\textit{space}, whereas an
orthonormal basis $\bm{K}_q$ of it is determined only up to right
multiplication by an element of $O(q)$.
This is exactly the ambiguity $\hat{\bm{W}} = \bm{K}_q(\bm{\Lambda}_q -
\hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{R}$ recorded in
Proposition~\ref{thm:TB}, and it disappears on $M$.
Finally, when a group of eigenvalues is separated from the rest, the
projector onto the corresponding invariant subspace is not merely well
defined but real analytic in $\bm{S}$; this is taken up in
Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz} on the set $\mathcal{O}$ of \eqref{eq:O_def}, which
by part 4 above is open.
\end{remark}

We now turn to the properties of $\bm{S}_n$ as a random matrix under the
sampling scheme fixed at the beginning of this section.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:S_n_properties}
Let $\bm{X}_1, \bm{X}_2, \ldots$ be independent and identically
distributed $N_p(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ random vectors and let
$\bm{S}_n$ be as in \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_def}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\bm{S}_n \in \text{Sym}(p)$ and $\bm{S}_n \succeq \bm{0}$, with
  $\rank(\bm{S}_n) \leqslant \min(n,p)$;
  in particular $\bm{S}_n \succ \bm{0}$ is impossible when $n < p$.
  \item $n\bm{S}_n$ follows the Wishart law $W_p(n, \bm{\Sigma}_0)$, and
  for $n \geqslant p$ its distribution has a density with respect to the
  Lebesgue measure of $\text{Sym}(p)$
  (\citealp{Gupta_Nagar_2000}, Chapter 3).
  \item $\bm{S}_n$ is an unbiased estimator of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$, and for
  all $\bm{B}, \bm{C} \in \text{Sym}(p)$ and all
  $i,j,k,l \in \{1,\ldots,p\}$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:S_n_covariance}
    \begin{gathered}
      \mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{S}_n} = \bm{\Sigma}_0,
      \qquad
      \text{Cov}\bracket{\tr(\bm{B}\bm{S}_n), \tr(\bm{C}\bm{S}_n)}
      = \frac{2}{n}\tr\bracket{\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{C}\bm{\Sigma}_0},
      \\
      \text{Cov}\bracket{(\bm{S}_n)_{ij}, (\bm{S}_n)_{kl}}
      = \frac{1}{n}\bracket{(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{ik}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{jl}
      + (\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{il}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{jk}}.
    \end{gathered}
  \end{equation}
  \item $\bm{S}_n$ is a sufficient statistic for $\mathcal{P}$: the joint
  density of $(\bm{X}_1,\ldots,\bm{X}_n)$ at $[\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M$ is
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:joint_density}
    \begin{gathered}
      \prod_{i=1}^n p(\bm{x}_i;[\bm{W},\sigma^2])
      = (2\pi)^{-\frac{np}{2}}
      \bracket{\det \bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-\frac{n}{2}}
      \exp\bracket{-\frac{n}{2}
      \tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-1}\bm{s}_n}},
      \\
      \bm{s}_n := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{x}_i\bm{x}_i^T .
    \end{gathered}
  \end{equation}
  \item $\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$ almost surely, and
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:S_n_CLT}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0} \rightsquigarrow \bm{G}
    \qquad \text{in } \text{Sym}(p),
  \end{equation}
  where $\bm{G}$ is the centred Gaussian random element of
  $\text{Sym}(p)$ with
  $\text{Cov}\bracket{\langle \bm{B},\bm{G}\rangle,\langle\bm{C},\bm{G}\rangle}
  = 2\tr(\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{C}\bm{\Sigma}_0)$
  for $\bm{B},\bm{C} \in \text{Sym}(p)$.
  In particular $\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\| = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$.
  \item Consequently
  $\max_{1\leqslant k\leqslant p}
  \abs{\lambda_k(\bm{S}_n) - \lambda_k(\bm{\Sigma}_0)} \to 0$
  almost surely, and this quantity is
  $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$.
  The convergence of the eigenvalues is not monotone in any useful sense,
  but it is biased outwards in the mean:
  $\mathbb{E}\squared{\lambda_1(\bm{S}_n)} \geqslant \lambda_1(\bm{\Sigma}_0)$
  and
  $\mathbb{E}\squared{\lambda_p(\bm{S}_n)} \leqslant \lambda_p(\bm{\Sigma}_0)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
1. Symmetry is clear and
$\bm{v}^T\bm{S}_n\bm{v} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (\bm{X}_i^T\bm{v})^2
\geqslant 0$;
the column space of $\bm{S}_n$ is contained in the span of
$\bm{X}_1,\ldots,\bm{X}_n$, whence the bound on the rank.

2. By definition, a sum of $n$ independent
$\bm{X}_i\bm{X}_i^T$ with $\bm{X}_i \sim N_p(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ is
$W_p(n,\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ distributed, and this law is non-singular, with the
usual density on the cone of positive definite matrices, as soon as
$n \geqslant p$ and $\bm{\Sigma}_0 \succ \bm{0}$;
the latter holds by Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} below.
The absolute continuity of the law of $\bm{S}_n$ is re-derived by a more
elementary route, avoiding any Wishart computation, in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{thm:generic_S}.

3. Since $\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{X}_1} = \bm{0}$ we have
$\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{X}_1\bm{X}_1^T} = \bm{\Sigma}_0$, and averaging
gives the first identity of \eqref{eq:S_n_covariance}.
For the second, note that
$\tr(\bm{B}\bm{S}_n) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{X}_i^T\bm{B}\bm{X}_i$
is an average of independent quadratic forms, so that, by Theorem 7.7.2
of \cite{Gupta_Nagar_2000} already used in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM},
\begin{equation*}
  \text{Cov}\bracket{\tr(\bm{B}\bm{S}_n),\tr(\bm{C}\bm{S}_n)}
  = \frac{1}{n}\text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{X}_1^T\bm{B}\bm{X}_1,
  \bm{X}_1^T\bm{C}\bm{X}_1}
  = \frac{2}{n}\tr\bracket{\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{C}\bm{\Sigma}_0}.
\end{equation*}
The entrywise formula is the special case
$\bm{B} = \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{e}_i\bm{e}_j^T + \bm{e}_j\bm{e}_i^T}$
and
$\bm{C} = \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{e}_k\bm{e}_l^T + \bm{e}_l\bm{e}_k^T}$,
for which $\tr(\bm{B}\bm{S}_n) = (\bm{S}_n)_{ij}$,
$\tr(\bm{C}\bm{S}_n) = (\bm{S}_n)_{kl}$ and, expanding the four resulting
terms and using the symmetry of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$,
$\tr(\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{C}\bm{\Sigma}_0)
= \frac{1}{2}\bracket{(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{ik}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{jl}
+ (\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{il}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)_{jk}}$.

4. Immediate from \eqref{eq:p_def} and the identity
$\bm{x}^T\bm{B}\bm{x} = \tr\bracket{\bm{B}\bm{x}\bm{x}^T}$, which turns
$\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{x}_i^T\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-1}\bm{x}_i$
into $n\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-1}\bm{s}_n}$;
sufficiency then follows from the Fisher--Neyman factorization criterion,
the right hand side of \eqref{eq:joint_density} depending on the sample
only through $\bm{s}_n$.

5. The entries of $\bm{X}_1\bm{X}_1^T$ are products of two jointly
Gaussian variables, hence integrable with finite second moments, so the
strong law of large numbers applied to each of the finitely many entries
gives $\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$ almost surely, and the multivariate
central limit theorem (\citealp{van_der_Vaart_1998}, Chapter 2) applied to
the independent and identically distributed random elements
$\bm{X}_i\bm{X}_i^T$ of the $\frac{p(p+1)}{2}$-dimensional Euclidean space
$\text{Sym}(p)$ gives \eqref{eq:S_n_CLT}, the covariance of the limit
being that of $\bm{X}_1\bm{X}_1^T$, computed in 3.
The last assertion follows because all norms on $\text{Sym}(p)$ are
equivalent and a weakly convergent sequence is uniformly tight.

6. The first two claims follow from \eqref{eq:weyl_lipschitz} and 5.
For the last two, $\lambda_1$ is convex and $\lambda_p$ is concave on
$\text{Sym}(p)$ by part 2 of Lemma~\ref{thm:spectral_facts}, so Jensen's
inequality and $\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{S}_n} = \bm{\Sigma}_0$ give
$\mathbb{E}\squared{\lambda_1(\bm{S}_n)}
\geqslant \lambda_1\bracket{\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{S}_n}}
= \lambda_1(\bm{\Sigma}_0)$
and the reverse inequality for $\lambda_p$;
the expectations are finite because
$\abs{\lambda_k(\bm{S}_n)} \leqslant \tr(\bm{S}_n)$, which is integrable.
\end{proof}

It remains to record the spectrum of the matrix that $\bm{S}_n$
approximates.
The point of the next lemma is that $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ has exactly the
\textit{spiked} shape --- $q$ eigenvalues above a flat floor of height
$\sigma_0^2$ --- which the model postulates, and that the corresponding spectral
gap is strictly positive; by Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties} the sample
covariance matrix inherits both features for $n$ large.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}
Let $\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$ be a singular value
decomposition of $\bm{W}_0$, with $\bm{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times q}$
having orthonormal columns,
$\bm{D}_0 = \text{diag}(\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0),\ldots,\sigma_q(\bm{W}_0))$
and $\bm{V}_0 \in O(q)$, and let
$\bm{P}_0 := \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$ be the orthogonal projector onto the
column space of $\bm{W}_0$.
Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization}
  \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \bm{U}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0^2 + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q}\bm{U}_0^T
  + \sigma_0^2\bracket{\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0},
  \qquad
  \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  = \bm{U}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0^2 + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q}^{-1}\bm{U}_0^T
  + \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}\bracket{\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0},
\end{equation}
so that the eigenvalues of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ are
$\lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_0^2$ for
$j = 1, \ldots, q$ and $\lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_0^2$ for
$j = q+1,\ldots,p$.
Consequently $\bm{\Sigma}_0 \succeq \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_p \succ \bm{0}$,
$\|\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\| = \sigma_0^{-2}$,
$\det \bm{\Sigma}_0 = \sigma_0^{2(p-q)}\prod_{j=1}^q(\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2+\sigma_0^2)$,
the eigenspace of the $q$ largest eigenvalues is the column space of
$\bm{W}_0$, and

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_0_gap}
  \lambda_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)
  = \sigma_{\min}(\bm{W}_0)^2 =: \delta_0 > 0 .
\end{equation}
Moreover, almost surely there is a (random) $N$ such that for all
$n \geqslant N$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:S_n_eventual_gap}
  \bm{S}_n \succeq \frac{\sigma_0^2}{2}\bm{I}_p \succ \bm{0}
  \qquad \text{and} \qquad
  \lambda_q(\bm{S}_n) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_n) \geqslant \frac{\delta_0}{2}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0^2\bm{U}_0^T$ and
$\bm{I}_p = \bm{P}_0 + (\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)$, the first identity of
\eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} follows from
$\bm{\Sigma}_0 = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_p$;
completing the columns of $\bm{U}_0$ to a basis of $\mathbb{R}^p$ by an
orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of the column space of
$\bm{W}_0$ exhibits \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} as a spectral
decomposition \eqref{eq:spectral_decomposition}, whence the list of
eigenvalues, the identification of the eigenspace of the $q$ largest ones
with the column space of $\bm{W}_0$, and the formula for the determinant.
Since $\rank(\bm{W}_0) = q$ we have $\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0) > 0$ for all
$j \leqslant q$, so all $q$ leading eigenvalues are strictly larger than
$\sigma_0^2$, which is therefore the smallest eigenvalue;
this gives $\bm{\Sigma}_0 \succeq \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_p$,
$\|\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\| = \lambda_p(\bm{\Sigma}_0)^{-1} = \sigma_0^{-2}$ and
\eqref{eq:Sigma_0_gap}.
The second identity of \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} is checked by
multiplying the two expressions and using
$\bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_0 = \bm{I}_q$ together with
$\bm{U}_0^T(\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0) = \bm{0}$.

For \eqref{eq:S_n_eventual_gap}, let
$\epsilon := \frac{1}{4}\min(\sigma_0^2, \delta_0) > 0$.
By part 5 of Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties} we have almost surely
$\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\| \leqslant \epsilon$ for all large $n$,
and then \eqref{eq:weyl_lipschitz} gives
$\lambda_p(\bm{S}_n) \geqslant \lambda_p(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - \epsilon
= \sigma_0^2 - \epsilon \geqslant \frac{\sigma_0^2}{2}$
as well as
$\lambda_q(\bm{S}_n) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_n)
\geqslant \lambda_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - 2\epsilon
\geqslant \frac{\delta_0}{2}$.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The maximum likelihood estimator}

The log-likelihood of the model \eqref{eq:quotient_model}
is the function on $M$ given by

\begin{equation}\label{eq:loglik}
  \mathbb{L}_n([\bm{W},\sigma^2]) := \sum_{i=1}^n \log p(\bm{X}_i;[\bm{W},\sigma^2])
  = -\frac{np}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{n}{2}\log \det \bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])
  - \frac{n}{2}\tr \bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-1}\bm{S}_n};
\end{equation}
it depends on the sample only through $\bm{S}_n$ and it is genuinely
a function on the quotient $M$, since the right hand side depends on
$(\bm{W},\sigma^2)$ only through $\bm{\Sigma}$.
This is precisely the reason why the model had to be quotiented
in the first place: $\mathbb{L}_n$ is exactly constant along the
fibers of $\pi$, so no maximizer of $\mathbb{L}_n$ on $\Theta$ can ever be
unique, whereas on $M$ it can.
The following classical computation identifies the maximizer.
We write $\lambda_1(\bm{S}) \geqslant \ldots \geqslant \lambda_p(\bm{S})$
for the eigenvalues of $\bm{S} \in \text{Sym}(p)$, the space of real symmetric
$p \times p$ matrices, repeated according to multiplicity, and
we let

\begin{equation}\label{eq:O_def}
  \mathcal{O} := \curly{\bm{S} \in \text{Sym}(p) : \lambda_q(\bm{S}) > \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S})},
\end{equation}
which is an open subset of $\text{Sym}(p)$ because the eigenvalues depend
continuously on $\bm{S}$.
For $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ we write $\bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$ for the orthogonal
projector of $\mathbb{R}^p$ onto the sum of the eigenspaces of
$\lambda_1(\bm{S}), \ldots, \lambda_q(\bm{S})$; this is well defined
precisely because of the spectral gap in \eqref{eq:O_def}.

The next lemma collects the identities that let one pass between the
projector form and the eigenvector form of the maximizer below.
They are less innocent than they look: the second one uses that the
columns of $\bm{K}_q$ are eigenvectors and not merely an orthonormal
basis of the range of $\bm{P}_q$, as Remark~\ref{rmk:projector_algebra}
shows.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:projector_algebra}
Let $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$, put $\lambda_j := \lambda_j(\bm{S})$,
$\bm{P}_q := \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$ and
$\bm{\Lambda}_q := \text{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q)$, and let
$\bm{K}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ be a matrix whose columns are
orthonormal eigenvectors of $\bm{S}$ associated with
$\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_q$ in this order, that is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:K_q_def}
  \bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q = \bm{I}_q
  \qquad \text{and} \qquad
  \bm{S}\bm{K}_q = \bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q .
\end{equation}
Then the column space of $\bm{K}_q$ is the range of $\bm{P}_q$, the
projector $\bm{P}_q$ commutes with $\bm{S}$, and

\begin{equation}\label{eq:projector_algebra}
  \bm{P}_q = \bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T,
  \qquad
  \bm{S}\bm{P}_q = \bm{P}_q\bm{S} = \bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q
  = \bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{K}_q^T,
  \qquad
  \bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q - c\,\bm{P}_q
  = \bm{K}_q\bracket{\bm{\Lambda}_q - c\,\bm{I}_q}\bm{K}_q^T
\end{equation}
for every $c \in \mathbb{R}$.
Finally, a matrix $\bm{K}_q'$ satisfies \eqref{eq:K_q_def} if and only if
$\bm{K}_q' = \bm{K}_q\bm{R}$ for some $\bm{R} \in O(q)$ commuting with
$\bm{\Lambda}_q$, that is block diagonal along the blocks of equal values
in $(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q)$;
in particular none of the matrices in \eqref{eq:projector_algebra}
depends on the choice of $\bm{K}_q$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $E$ denote the sum of the eigenspaces of
$\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_q$, so that $\bm{P}_q$ is by definition the
orthogonal projector onto $E$.
Because $\lambda_q > \lambda_{q+1}$, no eigenvalue $\lambda_j$ with
$j > q$ is equal to any of $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q$, so the total
multiplicity of the latter is exactly $q$ and $\dim E = q$.
The columns of $\bm{K}_q$ are eigenvectors associated with those
eigenvalues, hence lie in $E$, and they are orthonormal, hence span a
$q$-dimensional subspace of $E$; therefore the column space of
$\bm{K}_q$ is $E$.
Since $\bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q = \bm{I}_q$, the matrix $\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T$ is
symmetric and idempotent with range $E$, that is
$\bm{P}_q = \bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T$, which is the first identity.

For the second, $\bm{S}\bm{P}_q = \bm{S}\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T
= \bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{K}_q^T$ by \eqref{eq:K_q_def};
this matrix is symmetric, so
$\bm{P}_q\bm{S} = \bracket{\bm{S}\bm{P}_q}^T = \bm{S}\bm{P}_q$, which is
the asserted commutation, and
$\bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q = \bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{K}_q^T
= \bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{K}_q^T$ again by
$\bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q = \bm{I}_q$.
Subtracting $c\,\bm{P}_q = c\,\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T$ gives the third
identity.

Suppose now that $\bm{K}_q'$ also satisfies \eqref{eq:K_q_def}.
By the first part both matrices have column space $E$, so
$\bm{K}_q' = \bm{K}_q\bm{R}$ with $\bm{R} := \bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q'$, and
$\bm{R}^T\bm{R} = \bm{K}_q'^T\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T\bm{K}_q'
= \bm{K}_q'^T\bm{P}_q\bm{K}_q' = \bm{I}_q$
because the columns of $\bm{K}_q'$ lie in $E$; thus $\bm{R} \in O(q)$.
Applying $\bm{S}$ gives
$\bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{R} = \bm{S}\bm{K}_q\bm{R} = \bm{S}\bm{K}_q'
= \bm{K}_q'\bm{\Lambda}_q = \bm{K}_q\bm{R}\bm{\Lambda}_q$, and
multiplying on the left by $\bm{K}_q^T$ yields
$\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{R} = \bm{R}\bm{\Lambda}_q$;
comparing entries, $(\lambda_i - \lambda_j)R_{ij} = 0$, so $R_{ij} = 0$
whenever $\lambda_i \neq \lambda_j$, which is the asserted block
structure.
Conversely such an $\bm{R}$ clearly returns a matrix satisfying
\eqref{eq:K_q_def}.
Independence of the choice is then immediate, since by
\eqref{eq:projector_algebra} every matrix occurring there is expressed
through $\bm{S}$ and $\bm{P}_q$ alone.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:projector_algebra}
The hypothesis that the columns of $\bm{K}_q$ be eigenvectors, and not
merely an orthonormal basis of the range of $\bm{P}_q$, cannot be
dropped from the second identity of \eqref{eq:projector_algebra}, even
though it is irrelevant for the first.
If $\bm{U} = \bm{K}_q\bm{R}$ with $\bm{R} \in O(q)$ arbitrary then
$\bm{U}\bm{U}^T = \bm{P}_q$ still, whereas
$\bm{U}\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{U}^T = \bm{K}_q\bm{R}\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{R}^T\bm{K}_q^T$
equals $\bm{K}_q\bm{\Lambda}_q\bm{K}_q^T$ only when $\bm{R}$ commutes
with $\bm{\Lambda}_q$.
For instance, for $p = 3$, $q = 2$ and
$\bm{S} = \text{diag}(2,1,\frac{1}{2}) \in \mathcal{O}$, the vectors
$\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,1,0)^T$ and $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,-1,0)^T$ form an
orthonormal basis of the range of $\bm{P}_2 = \text{diag}(1,1,0)$, but the
corresponding matrix $\bm{U}\bm{\Lambda}_2\bm{U}^T$ has upper left
$2 \times 2$ block $\begin{pmatrix} \frac{3}{2} & \frac{1}{2} \\
\frac{1}{2} & \frac{3}{2}\end{pmatrix}$, whereas
$\bm{P}_2\bm{S}\bm{P}_2$ has upper left block
$\text{diag}(2,1)$.
This is also the reason why the ordering requirement in
\eqref{eq:K_q_def} matters: the eigenvalues must be attached to the
eigenvectors that carry them.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Tipping--Bishop]\label{thm:TB}
Let $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ be positive definite and put
$\lambda_j := \lambda_j(\bm{S})$ and $\bm{P}_q := \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$.
Then the function
\begin{equation*}
  [\bm{W},\sigma^2] \longmapsto
  -\frac{p}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{1}{2}\log \det \bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])
  - \frac{1}{2}\tr \bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])}^{-1}\bm{S}}
\end{equation*}
attains its maximum over $M$ at one and only one point
$[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2] \in M$, determined by

\begin{equation}\label{eq:TB_formula}
  \hat{\sigma}^2 = \frac{1}{p-q}\sum_{j=q+1}^p \lambda_j,
  \qquad
  \hat{\bm{W}}\hat{\bm{W}}^T = \bm{P}_q \bm{S} \bm{P}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\, \bm{P}_q.
\end{equation}
Concretely, $\hat{\bm{W}} = \bm{K}_q(\bm{\Lambda}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{R}$
for an arbitrary $\bm{R} \in O(q)$, where the columns of
$\bm{K}_q \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ are orthonormal eigenvectors of
$\bm{S}$ associated with $\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_q$
and $\bm{\Lambda}_q = \text{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is Section 3.2 together with Appendix A of \cite{Tipping_Bishop_1999}:
equations (7) and (8) there give the stationary points and equations
(17)--(20) of their Appendix A.2 show that the global maximum is attained
exactly when the $q$ \textit{largest} eigenvalues of $\bm{S}$ are retained.
Since $\lambda_q > \lambda_{q+1}$ the set of retained eigenvalues,
and hence the projector $\bm{P}_q$, is unambiguous, so the maximizer
is a single point of $M$.
The two descriptions agree by Lemma~\ref{thm:projector_algebra}:
the last identity of \eqref{eq:projector_algebra} with
$c = \hat{\sigma}^2$ gives
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{P}_q
  = \bm{K}_q\bracket{\bm{\Lambda}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q}\bm{K}_q^T
  = \bracket{\bm{K}_q\bracket{\bm{\Lambda}_q-\hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{R}}
  \bracket{\bm{K}_q\bracket{\bm{\Lambda}_q-\hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{R}}^T
  = \hat{\bm{W}}\hat{\bm{W}}^T
\end{equation*}
for every $\bm{R} \in O(q)$.
Finally $[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]$ is really a point of $M$:
$\hat{\sigma}^2 > 0$ because $\bm{S}$ is positive definite, and
$\rank(\hat{\bm{W}}) = q$ because
$\hat{\sigma}^2 \leqslant \lambda_{q+1} < \lambda_q$
forces every diagonal entry of $\bm{\Lambda}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_q$
to be strictly positive.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{def:MLE}
Whenever $\bm{S}_n$ is positive definite and lies in $\mathcal{O}$,
we let $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n, \hat{\sigma}_n^2] \in M$ denote the maximum likelihood
estimator, that is the unique maximizer of \eqref{eq:loglik} over $M$
supplied by Proposition~\ref{thm:TB} applied to $\bm{S} = \bm{S}_n$.
On the complementary event we set $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n, \hat{\sigma}_n^2] := [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$;
Lemma~\ref{thm:generic_S} below shows that this convention is invoked
only on a null set as soon as $n \geqslant p$.
\end{definition}

\subsection*{The fitted covariance matrix and the exact fit equation}

It is worth recording what the maximum likelihood estimator does to the
covariance matrix rather than to the parameter, if only because
\eqref{eq:loglik} immediately suggests a competitor: instead of
maximizing the likelihood, why not simply ask the model to reproduce the
sample covariance matrix, that is, why not solve

\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact_fit_equation}
  \bm{S} = \bm{W}\bm{W}^T + \sigma^2\bm{I}_p,
  \qquad [\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M ?
\end{equation}
The next two propositions answer this: the maximum likelihood fit is the
sample covariance matrix with its trailing eigenvalues flattened,
\eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} is almost surely unsolvable, and its least
squares relaxation returns the maximum likelihood estimator again.

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:fitted_covariance}
Let $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ be positive definite, let
$[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]$ be the maximizer supplied by
Proposition~\ref{thm:TB} and let

\begin{equation}\label{eq:fitted_def}
  \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} := \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]}
  = \hat{\bm{W}}\hat{\bm{W}}^T + \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_p
\end{equation}
be the \textit{fitted covariance matrix}.
Write $\lambda_j := \lambda_j(\bm{S})$, let $\bm{k}_1,\ldots,\bm{k}_p$ be
orthonormal eigenvectors of $\bm{S}$ ordered as in
\eqref{eq:spectral_decomposition} and put $\bm{P}_q := \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$.
Then
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ has the same eigenvectors as $\bm{S}$, keeps
  its $q$ largest eigenvalues and replaces the remaining $p-q$ by their
  average $\hat{\sigma}^2$:
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:fitted_covariance}
    \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} = \sum_{j=1}^q \lambda_j\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T
    + \hat{\sigma}^2\sum_{j=q+1}^p \bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T,
    \qquad
    \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} - \bm{S}
    = \sum_{j=q+1}^p \bracket{\hat{\sigma}^2 - \lambda_j}\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T .
  \end{equation}
  \item $\tr \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} = \tr \bm{S}$ and
  $\bm{P}_q\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{P}_q = \bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q$;
  moreover $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ is positive definite, lies in
  $\mathcal{O}$, satisfies $\bm{P}_q(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}) = \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$,
  and the map $\bm{S} \mapsto \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ is idempotent.
  \item The fit is exact if and only if the trailing spectrum of $\bm{S}$
  is flat:
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:exact_fit_criterion}
    \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} = \bm{S}
    \iff \lambda_{q+1} = \lambda_{q+2} = \ldots = \lambda_p
    \iff \bm{S} \in \bm{\Sigma}(M),
  \end{equation}
  that is, if and only if \eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} is solvable, in
  which case $[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]$ is its unique solution in $M$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
1. Lemma~\ref{thm:projector_algebra} applied to
$\bm{K}_q := (\bm{k}_1,\ldots,\bm{k}_q)$, which satisfies
\eqref{eq:K_q_def} by \eqref{eq:spectral_decomposition}, gives
$\bm{P}_q = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T$ and
$\bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T$,
so \eqref{eq:TB_formula} gives
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
  = \bracket{\bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{P}_q}
  + \hat{\sigma}^2\bracket{\bm{P}_q + (\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_q)}
  = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T
  + \hat{\sigma}^2\sum_{j>q}\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T ,
\end{equation*}
and subtracting $\bm{S} = \sum_{j=1}^p\lambda_j\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T$ gives
the second identity of \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance}.

2. Taking traces in \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance} and using
$\hat{\sigma}^2 = \frac{1}{p-q}\sum_{j>q}\lambda_j$ gives
$\tr\hat{\bm{\Sigma}} = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j
+ (p-q)\hat{\sigma}^2 = \tr\bm{S}$, and
$\bm{P}_q\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{P}_q = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\lambda_j
\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T = \bm{P}_q\bm{S}\bm{P}_q$ because the $\bm{k}_j$ are
orthonormal.
The eigenvalues of $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ are
$\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_q$ and $\hat{\sigma}^2$ with multiplicity
$p-q$; they are all strictly positive, and
$\hat{\sigma}^2 \leqslant \lambda_{q+1} < \lambda_q$ shows both that
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}} \in \mathcal{O}$ and that its leading $q$-dimensional
eigenspace is $\text{span}(\bm{k}_1,\ldots,\bm{k}_q)$, that is
$\bm{P}_q(\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}) = \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})$.
Idempotence follows: the trailing eigenvalues of $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ are
already all equal to $\hat{\sigma}^2$, so applying
\eqref{eq:fitted_covariance} to $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ reproduces it.

3. By the second identity of \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance} the difference
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}} - \bm{S}$ is symmetric with eigenvalues
$\hat{\sigma}^2 - \lambda_j$, $j > q$, in the directions
$\bm{k}_{q+1},\ldots,\bm{k}_p$, so it vanishes if and only if
$\lambda_j = \hat{\sigma}^2$ for every $j > q$, that is if and only if
the trailing eigenvalues are all equal, in which case their common value
is their average $\hat{\sigma}^2$.
If this holds then $\bm{S} = \hat{\bm{\Sigma}} \in \bm{\Sigma}(M)$;
conversely, if $\bm{S} = \bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])$ for some
$[\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M$, then Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} applied
to $(\bm{W},\sigma^2)$ in place of $(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$ shows that the
$p-q$ smallest eigenvalues of $\bm{S}$ all equal $\sigma^2$.
Uniqueness of the solution is uniqueness of the maximizer in
Proposition~\ref{thm:TB}, since any solution of
\eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} satisfies
$\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2]) = \bm{S} = \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$
and $\bm{\Sigma}$ is injective on $M$ by construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:exact_fit}
Two consequences of \eqref{eq:exact_fit_criterion} deserve to be
separated.
If $q = p-1$ the trailing spectrum consists of the single eigenvalue
$\lambda_p$, the flatness condition is vacuous, and the fit is always
exact: $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}} = \bm{S}$, and \eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} is
always solvable.
This is not an accident, since
$\dim M = p(p-1)+1-\frac{(p-1)(p-2)}{2} = \frac{p(p+1)}{2}
= \dim \text{Sym}(p)$
when $q = p-1$: the model is then saturated and imposes no constraint on
$\bm{\Sigma}$ beyond positive definiteness and a simple smallest
eigenvalue.
If $q < p-1$, by contrast, an exact fit forces $\bm{S}$ to have a
repeated eigenvalue.
Matrices with a repeated eigenvalue form a Lebesgue null subset of
$\text{Sym}(p)$, being the zero set of the discriminant of the
characteristic polynomial as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}, and
the law of $\bm{S}_n$ is absolutely continuous for $n \geqslant p$ by
Lemma~\ref{thm:generic_S};
hence almost surely
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n := \bm{\Sigma}([\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2])
\neq \bm{S}_n$ and \eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} has no solution at all.
The two matrices are nevertheless close: by \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance},
\eqref{eq:weyl_lipschitz} and part 5 of Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties},
\begin{equation*}
  \|\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{S}_n\|
  = \max_{j>q}\abs{\hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)}
  \leqslant 2\max_{j>q}\abs{\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n) - \sigma_0^2}
  \leqslant 2\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|
  = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}},
\end{equation*}
the middle inequality holding because $\hat{\sigma}_n^2$ is an average of
the $\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)$, $j > q$.
The discrepancy disappears in the limit precisely because the population
matrix does have a flat trailing spectrum,
$\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \ldots = \lambda_p(\bm{\Sigma}_0)
= \sigma_0^2$, by Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}.
\end{remark}

Since \eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} is almost surely unsolvable, the
natural way to rescue the estimator it was meant to define is to require
it only approximately, that is to replace it by the minimum distance,
or least squares, estimator

\begin{equation}\label{eq:least_squares}
  [\tilde{\bm{W}}_n, \tilde{\sigma}_n^2]
  := \argmin_{[\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M}
  \big\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{W}\bm{W}^T - \sigma^2\bm{I}_p\big\|_F^2 .
\end{equation}
This turns out to produce nothing new.

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:least_squares}
Let $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ be positive definite and keep the notation of
Proposition~\ref{thm:fitted_covariance}.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:least_squares_value}
  \min_{[\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M}
  \big\|\bm{S} - \bm{W}\bm{W}^T - \sigma^2\bm{I}_p\big\|_F^2
  = \sum_{j=q+1}^p \bracket{\lambda_j - \hat{\sigma}^2}^2,
\end{equation}
the minimum being attained at $[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]$, and every
minimizer has $\sigma^2 = \hat{\sigma}^2$.
In particular the least squares estimator \eqref{eq:least_squares} may be
taken to be the maximum likelihood estimator of
Definition~\ref{def:MLE}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\bm{B} := \bm{W}\bm{W}^T$, which ranges exactly over the positive
semi-definite matrices of rank $q$ as $[\bm{W},\sigma^2]$ ranges over $M$
with $\sigma^2 > 0$ fixed, put
$\bm{C} := \bm{S}-\sigma^2\bm{I}_p$ and $\bm{R} := \bm{C}-\bm{B}$, and
note $\lambda_j(\bm{C}) = \lambda_j - \sigma^2$.
We first bound $\|\bm{R}\|_F^2$ from below for fixed $\sigma^2$.
Since $\bm{B} \succeq \bm{0}$ we have
$\bm{v}^T\bm{R}\bm{v} \leqslant \bm{v}^T\bm{C}\bm{v}$ for every $\bm{v}$,
so \eqref{eq:courant_fischer} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ls_monotone}
  \lambda_j(\bm{R}) \leqslant \lambda_j(\bm{C})
  \qquad \text{for } j = 1,\ldots,p .
\end{equation}
Moreover $K := \ker \bm{B}$ has dimension at least $p-q$, and for a
subspace $V$ with $\dim V = j+q$ the intersection $V \cap K$ has
dimension at least $j$; choosing $W \subseteq V\cap K$ with
$\dim W = j$ and using $\bm{v}^T\bm{B}\bm{v} = 0$ on $K$,
\begin{equation*}
  \min_{\bm{v}\in V,\ \bm{v}^T\bm{v}=1}\bm{v}^T\bm{C}\bm{v}
  \leqslant \min_{\bm{v}\in W,\ \bm{v}^T\bm{v}=1}\bm{v}^T\bm{C}\bm{v}
  = \min_{\bm{v}\in W,\ \bm{v}^T\bm{v}=1}\bm{v}^T\bm{R}\bm{v}
  \leqslant \lambda_j(\bm{R}),
\end{equation*}
and taking the maximum over such $V$ in \eqref{eq:courant_fischer},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ls_rank}
  \lambda_{j+q}(\bm{C}) \leqslant \lambda_j(\bm{R})
  \qquad \text{for } j = 1, \ldots, p-q .
\end{equation}
Let now $n_{\sigma} := \#\curly{j : \lambda_j \geqslant \sigma^2}$.
For $1 \leqslant j \leqslant n_{\sigma}-q$ we have
$\lambda_{j+q}(\bm{C}) \geqslant 0$, so \eqref{eq:ls_rank} gives
$\lambda_j(\bm{R})^2 \geqslant (\lambda_{j+q}-\sigma^2)^2$, while for
$j > n_{\sigma}$ we have $\lambda_j(\bm{C}) < 0$, so
\eqref{eq:ls_monotone} gives
$\lambda_j(\bm{R})^2 \geqslant (\sigma^2-\lambda_j)^2$.
These two ranges of indices are disjoint, whence
\begin{equation*}
  \|\bm{R}\|_F^2 = \sum_{j=1}^p \lambda_j(\bm{R})^2
  \geqslant \sum_{j=q+1}^{n_{\sigma}}\bracket{\lambda_j-\sigma^2}^2
  + \sum_{j>n_{\sigma}}\bracket{\sigma^2-\lambda_j}^2
  = \sum_{j\leqslant q}\squared{\bracket{\sigma^2-\lambda_j}_+}^2
  + \sum_{j>q}\bracket{\lambda_j-\sigma^2}^2 =: L(\sigma^2),
\end{equation*}
where $(x)_+ := \max(x,0)$, the first sum being empty when
$n_{\sigma} \leqslant q$; the last equality is checked separately in the
cases $n_{\sigma} \geqslant q$ and $n_{\sigma} < q$.
The function $L$ is strictly convex and continuously differentiable on
$\mathbb{R}$, with
$L'(x) = 2\sum_{j\leqslant q}(x-\lambda_j)_+
- 2\sum_{j>q}(\lambda_j-x)$.
Since $\hat{\sigma}^2 \leqslant \lambda_{q+1} \leqslant \lambda_j$ for
$j \leqslant q$ the first sum vanishes at $x = \hat{\sigma}^2$, and the
second vanishes there because $\hat{\sigma}^2$ is the average of
$\lambda_{q+1},\ldots,\lambda_p$; hence $\hat{\sigma}^2$ is the unique
minimizer of $L$, with
$L(\hat{\sigma}^2) = \sum_{j>q}(\lambda_j-\hat{\sigma}^2)^2$.
Therefore every $[\bm{W},\sigma^2] \in M$ satisfies
$\|\bm{S}-\bm{W}\bm{W}^T-\sigma^2\bm{I}_p\|_F^2 \geqslant L(\sigma^2)
\geqslant L(\hat{\sigma}^2)$, with the second inequality strict unless
$\sigma^2 = \hat{\sigma}^2$.
Finally the bound is attained at $[\hat{\bm{W}},\hat{\sigma}^2]$: by
\eqref{eq:fitted_covariance},
$\bm{S} - \hat{\bm{W}}\hat{\bm{W}}^T - \hat{\sigma}^2\bm{I}_p
= \bm{S}-\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}
= \sum_{j>q}(\lambda_j-\hat{\sigma}^2)\bm{k}_j\bm{k}_j^T$,
whose squared Frobenius norm is exactly
$\sum_{j>q}(\lambda_j-\hat{\sigma}^2)^2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The coincidence in Proposition~\ref{thm:least_squares} is a small piece of
good luck rather than a general principle.
Maximizing \eqref{eq:loglik} is, up to constants not depending on the
parameter, the same as minimizing over $M$ the Stein loss
$\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_n}
- \log\det\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\bm{S}_n} - p$ with
$\bm{\Sigma} = \bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W},\sigma^2])$, which is a very different
discrepancy from the squared Frobenius distance minimized in
\eqref{eq:least_squares}; that the two are minimized at the same point
here reflects the special structure of \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance},
namely that the optimal fit acts only on the eigenvalues of $\bm{S}_n$
and leaves its eigenvectors alone.
The practical conclusion is that the exact fit equation
\eqref{eq:exact_fit_equation} defines no new estimator: it is either
unsolvable, which is the generic case, or solved by the maximum
likelihood estimator, and its least squares relaxation is the maximum
likelihood estimator as well.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Genericity of the sample covariance matrix}

Both the well-posedness of the estimation error and the consistency
of the maximum likelihood estimator rest on the behaviour of the
spectral projector $\bm{P}_q$, which we now record.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:riesz}
\,
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\mathcal{O}$ is open and connected in $\text{Sym}(p)$.
  \item $\bm{P}_q : \mathcal{O} \to \text{Sym}(p)$ is real analytic.
  \item Let $\bm{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ have orthonormal columns
  and define $\phi : \mathcal{O} \to \mathbb{R}$ by
  $\phi(\bm{S}) := \det \bracket{\bm{U}_0^T \bm{P}_q(\bm{S})\bm{U}_0}$.
  Then $\phi$ is real analytic and $\phi^{-1}(\{0\})$ is a Lebesgue null
  subset of $\text{Sym}(p) \cong \mathbb{R}^{\frac{p(p+1)}{2}}$.
  \item Let $\bm{S}_* \in \mathcal{O}$ and put
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:gap_spread}
    \delta_* := \lambda_q(\bm{S}_*) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*) > 0,
    \qquad
    \rho_* := \lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*) \geqslant \delta_* .
  \end{equation}
  Then every $\bm{S} \in \text{Sym}(p)$ with
  $\|\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*\| \leqslant \frac{\delta_*}{8}$ belongs to
  $\mathcal{O}$ and satisfies
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:projector_lipschitz}
    \|\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}) - \bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_*)\|
    \leqslant \frac{16\rho_*}{\delta_*^2}\,\|\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*\| .
  \end{equation}
  In particular $\bm{P}_q$ is Lipschitz on a neighbourhood of every point
  of $\mathcal{O}$, with a constant controlled by the spectral gap at that
  point alone.
  \item $\bm{P}_q$ is continuous on $\mathcal{O}$; concretely, if
  $\bm{S}_* \in \mathcal{O}$ and $\bm{S}_n \to \bm{S}_*$ in
  $\text{Sym}(p)$, then $\bm{S}_n \in \mathcal{O}$ for all large $n$ and
  $\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n) \to \bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_*)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
1. Openness was already observed.
For connectedness, fix $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ and diagonalize
$\bm{S} = \bm{K}\text{diag}(\bm{\lambda})\bm{K}^T$ with $\bm{K} \in O(p)$
and $\bm{\lambda} = (\lambda_1(\bm{S}),\ldots,\lambda_p(\bm{S}))$
non-increasing; replacing one column of $\bm{K}$ by its negative
if necessary, which does not change $\bm{S}$, we may assume
$\det(\bm{K}) = 1$.
Let $\bm{\mu} := (2,\ldots,2,1,\ldots,1)$ with the value $2$ repeated
$q$ times, and consider first the path
$t \mapsto \bm{K}\text{diag}((1-t)\bm{\lambda}+t\bm{\mu})\bm{K}^T$
for $t \in [0,1]$.
The vector $(1-t)\bm{\lambda}+t\bm{\mu}$ is non-increasing, being a convex
combination of non-increasing vectors, so it lists the ordered eigenvalues,
and the gap at position $q$ equals
$(1-t)(\lambda_q(\bm{S})-\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S})) + t > 0$;
the path therefore stays inside $\mathcal{O}$ and joins $\bm{S}$
to $\bm{K}\text{diag}(\bm{\mu})\bm{K}^T$.
Next, $SO(p)$ is path connected, so there is a path $u \mapsto \bm{K}(u)$
in $SO(p)$ from $\bm{K}$ to $\bm{I}_p$; the induced path
$u \mapsto \bm{K}(u)\text{diag}(\bm{\mu})\bm{K}(u)^T$
has constant spectrum $\bm{\mu}$, hence constant gap $1 > 0$ at position $q$,
stays in $\mathcal{O}$, and ends at $\text{diag}(\bm{\mu})$.
Every point of $\mathcal{O}$ is thus joined to the fixed point
$\text{diag}(\bm{\mu})$ by a path inside $\mathcal{O}$.

2. Fix $\bm{S}_* \in \mathcal{O}$, choose $c \in \mathbb{R}$ with
$\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*) < c < \lambda_{q}(\bm{S}_*)$
and let $\Gamma \subset \mathbb{C}$ be the positively oriented circle
of centre $\frac{1}{2}(\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) + c)$
and radius $\frac{1}{2}\bracket{\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*)}$,
which meets the real axis in the interval with endpoints
$\frac{1}{2}\bracket{c + \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*)}
\in \bracket{\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*), c}$ and
$\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) + \frac{1}{2}\bracket{c - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*)}
> \lambda_1(\bm{S}_*)$,
so that $\Gamma$ encircles $\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*), \ldots, \lambda_q(\bm{S}_*)$
and no other eigenvalue of $\bm{S}_*$.
Put $\delta := \min_{z \in \Gamma}\text{dist}(z, \text{spec}(\bm{S}_*)) > 0$.
If $\bm{S} \in \text{Sym}(p)$ satisfies $\|\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*\|_2 < \delta$
then $z\bm{I}_p - \bm{S}$ is invertible for every $z \in \Gamma$,
Weyl's inequality keeps exactly $q$ eigenvalues of $\bm{S}$ inside $\Gamma$,
and diagonalizing $\bm{S}$ shows, by the residue theorem applied to each
eigenvalue separately, that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:riesz_formula}
  \bm{P}_q(\bm{S}) = \frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\Gamma}(z\bm{I}_p - \bm{S})^{-1}\mathrm{d}z.
\end{equation}
Expanding the resolvent in a Neumann series,
\begin{equation*}
  (z\bm{I}_p - \bm{S})^{-1}
  = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\squared{(z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S}_*)^{-1}(\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*)}^k
  (z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S}_*)^{-1},
\end{equation*}
a series which converges absolutely and uniformly in $z \in \Gamma$
whenever $\|\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*\|_2 < \delta$, since
$\|(z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S}_*)^{-1}\|_2 \leqslant \delta^{-1}$ there.
Each summand is a homogeneous polynomial of degree $k$ in the entries
of $\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*$ whose coefficients are continuous in $z$,
so integrating term by term over the compact contour $\Gamma$
exhibits $\bm{P}_q$ as a convergent power series in the entries of
$\bm{S}-\bm{S}_*$ on that ball.

The argument just given is the standard Riesz projection argument of
analytic perturbation theory and is not new: it is carried out in
\cite{Kato_1995}, Chapter II, \S 1.4, where the projection associated
with the eigenvalues enclosed by $\Gamma$ is defined by the contour
integral \eqref{eq:riesz_formula} and expanded, by term by term
integration of the Neumann series of \S 1.3 there, into a convergent
power series in the perturbation, and in \S 2.1 of the same chapter for
a group of eigenvalues, which is the case needed here;
\S 6.1 specializes the discussion to symmetric operators.
The use of the contour integral in perturbation theory goes back to
Sz.-Nagy and to Kato himself, replacing the earlier method of Rellich,
as the footnote to \S 1.4 of \cite{Kato_1995} records.
We have reproduced the proof because in the real symmetric
finite-dimensional case it is short, and because part 4 below needs the
resulting bound with an explicit constant.

3. Real analyticity of $\phi$ is immediate from 2, the determinant being
a polynomial.
Moreover $\phi$ does not vanish identically: taking
$\bm{S}_\star := \bm{I}_p + \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$, whose eigenvalues are
$2$ with multiplicity $q$, with eigenspace the column space of $\bm{U}_0$,
and $1$ with multiplicity $p-q$, we get $\bm{S}_\star \in \mathcal{O}$,
$\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_\star) = \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$ and
$\phi(\bm{S}_\star) = \det(\bm{I}_q) = 1$.
By 1 the domain $\mathcal{O}$ is open and connected, so the zero set of the
non-trivial real analytic function $\phi$ is Lebesgue null in
$\text{Sym}(p)$ by \cite{Mityagin_2020}.
Finally $\text{Sym}(p)\setminus \mathcal{O}$ is itself Lebesgue null:
it is contained in the zero set of the discriminant of the characteristic
polynomial of $\bm{S}$, which is a polynomial in the entries of $\bm{S}$
and does not vanish at $\text{diag}(1,2,\ldots,p)$.

4. Continuity is already contained in 2; what has to be produced here is
the explicit constant, and for that we specialize the contour of 2 to the
midpoint $c := \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\lambda_q(\bm{S}_*)+\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*)}$
of the spectral gap.
Then $\Gamma$ is the circle of centre
$a := \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) + c}$ and radius
$r := \frac{\rho_*}{2}$, which meets the real axis at
$a - r = \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*) + \frac{\delta_*}{4}$ and
$a + r = \lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) + \frac{\delta_*}{4}$.
A real number $\lambda$ is at distance $\big|r - \abs{\lambda-a}\big|$
from $\Gamma$, and $\lambda \mapsto r - \abs{\lambda - a}$ is concave, so
on the interval $[\lambda_q(\bm{S}_*), \lambda_1(\bm{S}_*)]$, which
contains $\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*),\ldots,\lambda_q(\bm{S}_*)$, it is minimized
at an endpoint, where it takes the values
$a + r - \lambda_1(\bm{S}_*) = \frac{\delta_*}{4}$ and
$\lambda_q(\bm{S}_*) - (a-r) = \frac{3\delta_*}{4}$;
and every eigenvalue $\lambda_j(\bm{S}_*)$ with $j > q$ lies to the left
of $a-r$ at distance at least
$(a-r) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S}_*) = \frac{\delta_*}{4}$.
Hence the quantity $\delta$ of part 2 satisfies
$\delta \geqslant \frac{\delta_*}{4}$.

Let now $\bm{E} := \bm{S}-\bm{S}_*$ with
$\|\bm{E}\| \leqslant \frac{\delta_*}{8} \leqslant \frac{\delta}{2}$.
By \eqref{eq:weyl_lipschitz},
$\lambda_q(\bm{S}) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{S})
\geqslant \delta_* - 2\|\bm{E}\| \geqslant \frac{3\delta_*}{4} > 0$,
so $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$, and every eigenvalue of $\bm{S}$ is within
$\|\bm{E}\|$ of an eigenvalue of $\bm{S}_*$, so that
$\text{dist}(z, \text{spec}(\bm{S})) \geqslant \delta - \|\bm{E}\|
\geqslant \frac{\delta}{2}$ for $z \in \Gamma$.
Both resolvents are therefore defined on $\Gamma$ and
\eqref{eq:riesz_formula} holds for $\bm{S}$ as well as for $\bm{S}_*$.
Diagonalizing shows that a real symmetric matrix has
$\|(z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S})^{-1}\| = \text{dist}(z,\text{spec}(\bm{S}))^{-1}$
for every $z \notin \text{spec}(\bm{S})$, so subtracting the two copies of
\eqref{eq:riesz_formula}, inserting the resolvent identity
\begin{equation*}
  (z\bm{I}_p - \bm{S})^{-1} - (z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S}_*)^{-1}
  = (z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S})^{-1}\bm{E}\,(z\bm{I}_p-\bm{S}_*)^{-1},
\end{equation*}
and estimating the integrand along the contour, of length $2\pi r$, gives
\begin{equation*}
  \|\bm{P}_q(\bm{S})-\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_*)\|
  \leqslant \frac{1}{2\pi}\,(2\pi r)\,\frac{2}{\delta}\cdot\frac{1}{\delta}
  \,\|\bm{E}\|
  = \frac{2r}{\delta^2}\|\bm{E}\|
  \leqslant \frac{16\rho_*}{\delta_*^2}\|\bm{E}\|,
\end{equation*}
using $r = \frac{\rho_*}{2}$ and $\delta \geqslant \frac{\delta_*}{4}$.

5. This is contained in 2, and also in 4, but it needs neither and may be
quoted directly: it is Theorem 5.1 of \cite{Kato_1995}, Chapter II,
\S 5.1, a section entitled ``Continuity of the eigenvalues and the total
projection'', which asserts that when the operator depends continuously
on its argument its eigenvalues are continuous and so is the total
projection attached to the group of eigenvalues encircled by a fixed
contour; the discussion immediately preceding that theorem states the
conclusion in the form used here, for a sequence of operators converging
to a limit.
One applies it to each of the distinct eigenvalues of $\bm{S}_*$ among
$\lambda_1(\bm{S}_*),\ldots,\lambda_q(\bm{S}_*)$, finitely many, whose
total projections sum to $\bm{P}_q$;
that $\bm{S}_n$ eventually lies in $\mathcal{O}$, so that $\bm{P}_q$ may
be evaluated at it at all, is \eqref{eq:weyl_lipschitz} once more.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The factor $\rho_*$ in \eqref{eq:projector_lipschitz} is an artefact of
estimating a circular contour that must enclose the whole leading part of
the spectrum; the sharp form of the estimate, the Davis--Kahan
$\sin\Theta$ theorem, replaces $\frac{16\rho_*}{\delta_*^2}$ by
$\frac{2}{\delta_*}$.
We do not need the sharp constant anywhere: only the fact that the
Lipschitz constant is finite and depends on $\bm{S}_*$ solely through its
spectrum.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:generic_S}
Suppose $n \geqslant p$.
Then, almost surely, $\bm{S}_n$ is positive definite,
$\bm{S}_n \in \mathcal{O}$, and the $q \times q$ matrix
$\bm{W}_0^T \hat{\bm{W}}_n$ is invertible.
In particular the orthogonal matrix

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q_n_def}
  \bm{Q}_n := \bm{Q}(\bm{W}_0, \hat{\bm{W}}_n)
  = \bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T\bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T\hat{\bm{W}}_n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}
  \hat{\bm{W}}_n^T \bm{W}_0 \in O(q)
\end{equation}
of Proposition~\ref{thm:logarithmic_map} is almost surely well defined.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first check that the law of $\bm{S}_n$ is absolutely continuous with
respect to the Lebesgue measure of $\text{Sym}(p)$.
Let $\bm{Y}_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times p}$ be the matrix whose rows are
$\bm{X}_1^T, \ldots, \bm{X}_p^T$ and let $\bm{Y}_2$ collect the remaining
$n-p$ observations, so that
$\bm{S}_n = \frac{1}{n}\bracket{\bm{Y}_1^T\bm{Y}_1 + \bm{Y}_2^T\bm{Y}_2}$
and $\bm{Y}_1$ is independent of $\bm{Y}_2$ with a strictly positive
Lebesgue density on $\mathbb{R}^{p \times p}$.
Condition on $\bm{Y}_2$ and consider
$T(\bm{Y}) := \frac{1}{n}(\bm{Y}^T\bm{Y} + \bm{Y}_2^T\bm{Y}_2)$
on the open set of invertible $\bm{Y} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times p}$,
whose complement is Lebesgue null.
Its differential at $\bm{Y}$ is
$\bm{H} \mapsto \frac{1}{n}(\bm{H}^T\bm{Y} + \bm{Y}^T\bm{H})$,
which is onto $\text{Sym}(p)$: given $\bm{B} \in \text{Sym}(p)$,
the choice $\bm{H} = \frac{n}{2}\bm{Y}^{-T}\bm{B}$ returns $\bm{B}$.
Thus $T$ is a smooth submersion, and by the rank theorem
(\citealp{Lee_2013}, Theorem 4.12) every point has a neighbourhood on which
$T$ is, up to diffeomorphisms, a linear projection
$\mathbb{R}^{p^2} \to \mathbb{R}^{\frac{p(p+1)}{2}}$.
Since preimages of Lebesgue null sets under such projections are
Lebesgue null by Fubini's theorem, and diffeomorphisms preserve
Lebesgue null sets, $T^{-1}(N)$ is Lebesgue null for every
Lebesgue null $N \subseteq \text{Sym}(p)$, after covering the domain
by countably many such neighbourhoods.
Hence the conditional law of $\bm{S}_n$ given $\bm{Y}_2$ charges no
Lebesgue null set, and the same holds for its mixture over $\bm{Y}_2$.

By Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}, the set of $\bm{S} \in \text{Sym}(p)$ that
either lie outside $\mathcal{O}$ or satisfy $\phi(\bm{S}) = 0$
is Lebesgue null; here we take $\bm{U}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ to be
any matrix with orthonormal columns spanning the column space of $\bm{W}_0$,
which has dimension $q$ because $\rank(\bm{W}_0) = q$.
It follows that almost surely
$\bm{S}_n \in \mathcal{O}$ and $\phi(\bm{S}_n) \neq 0$.
Positive definiteness, by contrast, is not a generic property in
$\text{Sym}(p)$ and must come from the structure of $\bm{S}_n$ instead:
the singular matrices form a Lebesgue null subset of
$\mathbb{R}^{p \times p}$, so $\bm{Y}_1$ is almost surely invertible and
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{S}_n = \frac{1}{n}\bm{Y}_1^T\bm{Y}_1 + \frac{1}{n}\bm{Y}_2^T\bm{Y}_2
  \succeq \frac{1}{n}\bm{Y}_1^T\bm{Y}_1 \succ \bm{0}
\end{equation*}
almost surely.

It remains to see that $\phi(\bm{S}_n)\neq 0$ is exactly the invertibility
of $\bm{W}_0^T\hat{\bm{W}}_n$.
Write $\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{T}$ with $\bm{T} \in \mathbb{R}^{q\times q}$
invertible, which is possible since the columns of $\bm{U}_0$
form a basis of the column space of $\bm{W}_0$, and use the
representative $\hat{\bm{W}}_n = \bm{K}_q(\bm{\Lambda}_q-\hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{I}_q)^{\frac{1}{2}}$
of Proposition~\ref{thm:TB}; note that invertibility of
$\bm{W}_0^T\hat{\bm{W}}_n$ is unchanged if $\hat{\bm{W}}_n$ is replaced by
$\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{R}$ with $\bm{R} \in O(q)$, so it is a property of the
class $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$.
Since $\bm{\Lambda}_q - \hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{I}_q$ is invertible, we get
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{W}_0^T\hat{\bm{W}}_n \text{ is invertible}
  \iff \bm{U}_0^T\bm{K}_q \text{ is invertible}
  \iff \det\bracket{\bm{U}_0^T\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T\bm{U}_0} \neq 0
  \iff \phi(\bm{S}_n) \neq 0,
\end{equation*}
where we used $\det(\bm{U}_0^T\bm{K}_q)^2
= \det(\bm{U}_0^T\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T\bm{U}_0)$
and $\bm{K}_q\bm{K}_q^T = \bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{Consistency and the aligned estimation error}

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:consistency}
Almost surely, $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2] \to [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$ in $M$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\bm{P}_0$ for the orthogonal projector onto the column space of
$\bm{W}_0$, so that $\bm{P}_0\bm{W}_0 = \bm{W}_0$, and abbreviate

\begin{equation}\label{eq:P_n_def}
  \bm{P}_n := \bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n),
\end{equation}
the orthogonal projector of $\mathbb{R}^p$ onto the sum of the eigenspaces
of the $q$ largest eigenvalues of $\bm{S}_n$, which is defined as soon as
$\bm{S}_n \in \mathcal{O}$.
By Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} the eigenvalues of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ are
$\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_0^2$ for $j = 1,\ldots,q$ and $\sigma_0^2$
with multiplicity $p-q$, the eigenspace of the $q$ largest ones is the
column space of $\bm{W}_0$, and the gap of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ at position $q$
is $\delta_0 = \sigma_{\min}(\bm{W}_0)^2 > 0$ by \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_gap};
its spread, in the sense of \eqref{eq:gap_spread}, is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_0_spread}
  \rho_0 := \lambda_1(\bm{\Sigma}_0)-\lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0)
  = \sigma_1(\bm{W}_0)^2 .
\end{equation}
In particular $\delta_0 > 0$ says that $\bm{\Sigma}_0 \in \mathcal{O}$, so
that $\bm{P}_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ is defined, and we claim that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:P_q_Sigma_0}
  \bm{P}_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \bm{P}_0 .
\end{equation}
Indeed, let $\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$ be the singular value
decomposition used in Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}, so that the
columns of $\bm{U}_0$ are orthonormal and span the column space of
$\bm{W}_0$, whence $\bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$, and
$\bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0^2\bm{U}_0^T$.
Then
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{U}_0
  = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T\bm{U}_0 + \sigma_0^2\bm{U}_0
  = \bm{U}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0^2 + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q}
  = \bm{U}_0\bm{\Lambda}_q,
  \qquad
  \bm{\Lambda}_q := \text{diag}\bracket{\lambda_1(\bm{\Sigma}_0),\ldots,\lambda_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0)},
\end{equation*}
the identification of the diagonal entries being that of
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}, and they are listed in non-increasing
order because the singular values $\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)$ are.
Thus $\bm{U}_0$ satisfies \eqref{eq:K_q_def} for $\bm{S} = \bm{\Sigma}_0$,
and the first identity of \eqref{eq:projector_algebra} in
Lemma~\ref{thm:projector_algebra} gives
$\bm{P}_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T = \bm{P}_0$, which is
\eqref{eq:P_q_Sigma_0}.
The inclusion of the column space of $\bm{W}_0$ in the range of
$\bm{P}_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ is the easy half of this; what the gap
$\delta_0 > 0$ contributes, through Lemma~\ref{thm:projector_algebra}, is
that the range is not larger, no eigenvalue of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ below the
cut being equal to one above it.
Equivalently, every $\bm{v}$ orthogonal to the column space of $\bm{W}_0$
satisfies $\bm{W}_0^T\bm{v} = \bm{0}$ and hence
$\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{v} = \bm{W}_0\bracket{\bm{W}_0^T\bm{v}} + \sigma_0^2\bm{v}
= \sigma_0^2\bm{v}$, so the orthogonal complement of that column space is
exactly the eigenspace of the smallest eigenvalue $\sigma_0^2$.

By part 5 of Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties},
$\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$ almost surely; we fix from now on a
realization lying in that almost sure event, so that the whole argument
below is a deterministic statement about a convergent sequence of
symmetric matrices.

We first make precise in what sense $\bm{P}_n \to \bm{P}_0$.
Individual eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ need not converge to anything, as
Remark~\ref{rmk:pca_variational} recalls: they are determined only up to
sign, and up to a rotation inside an eigenspace when eigenvalues collide.
What converges is the projector, and it does so because it is a locally
Lipschitz function of the matrix near $\bm{\Sigma}_0$, the Lipschitz
constant being governed by the spectral gap $\delta_0$.
Precisely, choose $N$ with
$\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\| \leqslant \frac{\delta_0}{8}$ for all
$n \geqslant N$, which is possible since
$\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$ and $\delta_0 > 0$.
Part 4 of Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}, applied with $\bm{S}_* = \bm{\Sigma}_0$
and $\bm{S} = \bm{S}_n$, then shows that $\bm{S}_n \in \mathcal{O}$ for
every $n \geqslant N$, so that $\bm{P}_n$ is well defined, and that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:P_n_convergence}
  \|\bm{P}_n - \bm{P}_0\|
  = \|\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n) - \bm{P}_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0)\|
  \leqslant \frac{16\rho_0}{\delta_0^2}\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|
  = \frac{16\,\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\sigma_{\text{min}}(\bm{W}_0)^4}
  \|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|
  \longrightarrow 0 .
\end{equation}
The constant depends only on the true parameter, through the singular
values of $\bm{W}_0$; combined with part 5 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties} the same inequality gives the rate
$\|\bm{P}_n - \bm{P}_0\| = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$, which we do
not need here but which is the quantitative form of the statement.
For the convergence \eqref{eq:P_n_convergence} alone, without the
constant, no argument is needed at all: it is part 5 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}, quoted there from Theorem 5.1 of
\cite{Kato_1995}.

By \eqref{eq:S_n_eventual_gap} the matrix $\bm{S}_n$ is also eventually
positive definite, so Proposition~\ref{thm:TB} applies to it for all large
$n$.
Since $\tr\bracket{(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_n)\bm{S}_n}
= \tr(\bm{S}_n) - \tr(\bm{P}_n\bm{S}_n)
= \sum_{j>q}\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)$
by Lemma~\ref{thm:projector_algebra}, formula \eqref{eq:TB_formula} reads
$\hat{\sigma}_n^2
= \frac{1}{p-q}\tr\bracket{(\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_n)\bm{S}_n}$, and the trace
being a continuous bilinear function of its two arguments we get, using
$\bm{P}_n \to \bm{P}_0$ and $\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$,
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\sigma}_n^2 = \frac{1}{p-q}\tr\bracket{(\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_n)\bm{S}_n}
  \longrightarrow \frac{1}{p-q}\tr\bracket{(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)\bm{\Sigma}_0}
  = \frac{\sigma_0^2}{p-q}\tr(\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0) = \sigma_0^2 ,
\end{equation*}
where the third equality holds because
$(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)\bm{\Sigma}_0 = \sigma_0^2(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)$, itself
a consequence of $\bm{\Sigma}_0 = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_p$
and $(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)\bm{W}_0 = \bm{0}$, and the fourth because
$\bm{P}_0$ is an orthogonal projector of rank $q$, so that
$\tr(\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0) = p - \tr(\bm{P}_0) = p-q$.
In the same way
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{W}}_n\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T
  = \bm{P}_n\bm{S}_n\bm{P}_n - \hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{P}_n
  \longrightarrow \bm{P}_0\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{P}_0 - \sigma_0^2\bm{P}_0
  = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T,
\end{equation*}
using $\bm{P}_0\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{P}_0 = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T + \sigma_0^2\bm{P}_0$.

It remains to convert this into convergence in $M$.
We claim that if $\bm{B}_n \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times q}$ satisfy
$\bm{B}_n\bm{B}_n^T \to \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T$, then
$\min_{\bm{Q}\in O(q)}\|\bm{B}_n\bm{Q}-\bm{W}_0\|_F \to 0$.
Indeed $\|\bm{B}_n\|_F^2 = \tr(\bm{B}_n\bm{B}_n^T)$ is convergent, hence
bounded.
If the claim failed there would be $\epsilon > 0$ and a subsequence
along which $\min_{\bm{Q}}\|\bm{B}_n\bm{Q}-\bm{W}_0\|_F \geqslant \epsilon$;
by boundedness we may extract a further subsequence with
$\bm{B}_n \to \bm{B}$, and then $\bm{B}\bm{B}^T = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T$.
This matrix has rank $q$, so $\rank(\bm{B}) = q$ and
$(\bm{B},\sigma_0^2) \sim (\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$;
by the description of the equivalence classes recalled in
Section~\ref{sec:basic_setup} there is $\bm{Q}_0 \in O(q)$ with
$\bm{B} = \bm{W}_0\bm{Q}_0$.
Consequently
$\min_{\bm{Q}}\|\bm{B}_n\bm{Q}-\bm{W}_0\|_F
\leqslant \|\bm{B}_n\bm{Q}_0^T - \bm{B}\bm{Q}_0^T\|_F
= \|\bm{B}_n-\bm{B}\|_F \to 0$
along that subsequence, a contradiction.

Applying the claim to $\bm{B}_n = \hat{\bm{W}}_n$ we obtain, almost surely,
matrices $\bm{Q}_n' \in O(q)$ with
$\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n' \to \bm{W}_0$; combined with $\hat{\sigma}_n^2 \to \sigma_0^2$
this says $(\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n', \hat{\sigma}_n^2) \to (\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$ in
$\Theta$, and the continuity of $\pi$ gives
\begin{equation*}
  [\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]
  = \pi\bracket{(\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n', \hat{\sigma}_n^2)}
  \longrightarrow \pi\bracket{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)} = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]
\end{equation*}
in $M$, as required.
\end{proof}

The proof just given passes through the spectral projector, and therefore
through the perturbation theory of Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}.
It is worth recording that this is avoidable: once
Proposition~\ref{thm:fitted_covariance} is available, the whole spectral
input reduces to Weyl's inequality, which we quote from Chapter 2 of
\cite{Chen_et_al_2021}, a chapter devoted to exactly the situation at
hand --- there, in the notation of its equation (2.1), $\bm{M}$ is
``an observed or estimated data matrix such as the sample covariance
matrix'' and $\bm{M}^{\star}$ is ``the target matrix such as the
population covariance matrix''.

\begin{proof}[Alternative proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency}]
Fix a realization in the almost sure event
$\curly{\bm{S}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0}$ of part 5 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties} and set
$\bm{E}_n := \bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0$, so that $\|\bm{E}_n\| \to 0$;
this is \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_def} read as the perturbation
$\bm{S}_n = \bm{\Sigma}_0 + \bm{E}_n$ of \cite{Chen_et_al_2021},
equation (2.1).
Write $\lambda_j := \lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)$.

\textbf{Step 1: the eigenvalues.}
Lemma 2.2 of \cite{Chen_et_al_2021}, Weyl's inequality for eigenvalues,
states that $\abs{\lambda_j(\bm{A}) - \lambda_j(\bm{A}+\bm{E})}
\leqslant \|\bm{E}\|$ for real symmetric $\bm{A}$ and $\bm{E}$ and every
$j$; with $\bm{A} = \bm{\Sigma}_0$ and $\bm{E} = \bm{E}_n$ this gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:weyl_S_n}
  \max_{1 \leqslant j \leqslant p}
  \abs{\lambda_j - \lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0)} \leqslant \|\bm{E}_n\|
  \longrightarrow 0 .
\end{equation}
By Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} we have
$\lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_0^2$ for every $j > q$,
$\lambda_p(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_0^2 > 0$ and
$\lambda_q(\bm{\Sigma}_0) - \lambda_{q+1}(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \delta_0 > 0$,
so \eqref{eq:weyl_S_n} shows that for all large $n$ the matrix
$\bm{S}_n$ is positive definite and lies in $\mathcal{O}$, whence
Proposition~\ref{thm:TB} applies to it, and that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigma_hat_weyl}
  \hat{\sigma}_n^2 = \frac{1}{p-q}\sum_{j>q}\lambda_j
  \longrightarrow \frac{1}{p-q}\,(p-q)\,\sigma_0^2 = \sigma_0^2,
  \qquad
  \max_{j>q}\abs{\lambda_j - \hat{\sigma}_n^2}
  \leqslant 2\max_{j>q}\abs{\lambda_j - \sigma_0^2}
  \leqslant 2\|\bm{E}_n\|,
\end{equation}
the middle inequality because $\hat{\sigma}_n^2$ is an average of the
$\lambda_j$ with $j > q$, each of which is within $\|\bm{E}_n\|$ of
$\sigma_0^2$.

\textbf{Step 2: the loading matrix.}
Let $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n := \bm{\Sigma}([\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2])
= \hat{\bm{W}}_n\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T + \hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{I}_p$ be the fitted
covariance matrix \eqref{eq:fitted_def}.
By the second identity of \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance} the difference
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{S}_n$ is symmetric with eigenvalues
$\hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \lambda_j$, $j > q$, so
$\|\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{S}_n\|
= \max_{j>q}\abs{\hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \lambda_j}
\leqslant 2\|\bm{E}_n\| \to 0$
by \eqref{eq:sigma_hat_weyl}.
Consequently $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n \to \bm{\Sigma}_0$, and therefore
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{W}}_n\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T
  = \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{I}_p
  \longrightarrow \bm{\Sigma}_0 - \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_p
  = \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T .
\end{equation*}
No spectral projector, and hence no continuity or analyticity statement
about $\bm{P}_q$, has been used.

\textbf{Step 3: convergence in $M$.}
From $\hat{\sigma}_n^2 \to \sigma_0^2$ and
$\hat{\bm{W}}_n\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T \to \bm{W}_0\bm{W}_0^T$ the conclusion
follows exactly as in the last part of the proof above, which is a
statement about the quotient and involves no spectral theory.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
If the spectral projector itself is wanted, and not merely the products
in which it appears, the same chapter of \cite{Chen_et_al_2021} supplies
it in quantitative form: the Davis--Kahan $\sin \Theta$ theorem, Theorem
2.7 there, and its Corollary 2.8, bound the distance between the
eigenspaces of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ and of $\bm{S}_n$ spanned by the $q$
leading eigenvectors by a multiple of $\|\bm{E}_n\|$ divided by the
eigengap, under a condition on $\|\bm{E}_n\|$ relative to that gap.
Since $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ and, eventually, $\bm{S}_n$ are positive definite,
ordering their eigenvalues by magnitude is the same as ordering them by
value, so Corollary 2.8 applies to $\bm{P}_n$ and $\bm{P}_0$ as they are
defined here and is an alternative to parts 4 and 5 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency} recovers, for the independent and
identically distributed Gaussian sampling scheme, the strong consistency
result of \cite{Datta_Chakrabarty_2023} and \cite{Datta_2026}, Corollary 1,
which is proved there by a Wald-type argument valid under considerably
weaker assumptions.
The proof above is included because it is short once
Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz} is available, and because it makes the section
self-contained.
\end{remark}

We can now fix once and for all the normal chart in which the whole
analysis will be carried out.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:chart}
There exists $\rho \in \bracket{0, \min(\sigma_{\text{min}}(\bm{W}_0), \sigma_0^2)}$
such that, writing
$B_{\rho} := \{\bm{u} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M : \abs{\bm{u}} < \rho\}
\subset \mathcal{E}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$,
the map $\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ restricted to $B_{\rho}$
is a diffeomorphism onto an open subset
$\mathcal{U}_0 := \text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(B_{\rho}) \subseteq M$,
and such that $\mathcal{U}_0 \subseteq U$, where $U$ is the neighbourhood
supplied by Proposition~\ref{thm:logarithmic_map} at $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} the exponential map is defined on
$\mathcal{E}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$, and it is a local diffeomorphism at
$\bm{0}$, so there is an open neighbourhood $\mathcal{N}$ of $\bm{0}$
inside $\mathcal{E}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ that is mapped diffeomorphically onto
an open subset of $M$.
Since $\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ is continuous and
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{0}) = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in U$,
we may pick $\rho > 0$ so small that
$B_{\rho} \subseteq \mathcal{N}$ and
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(B_{\rho}) \subseteq U$.
A restriction of a diffeomorphism to an open subset is again a
diffeomorphism onto its (open) image.
\end{proof}

We fix such a $\rho$ for the rest of the section, and define the
\textit{aligned estimation error}

\begin{equation}\label{eq:e_n_def}
  \bm{e}_n := \bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0,\ \hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \sigma_0^2}
  \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q} \times \mathbb{R},
  \qquad
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n := \begin{cases}
    \text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]}
    & \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n, \\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
where $\bm{Q}_n$ is as in \eqref{eq:Q_n_def} and
$\mathcal{A}_n := \curly{[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2] \in \mathcal{U}_0}$.

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:error_welldefined}
For $n \geqslant p$ we have $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$;
on $\mathcal{A}_n$ the vector $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ belongs to $B_{\rho}$
and its horizontal lift at $(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$ is exactly $\bm{e}_n$,
that is $\iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) = \bm{e}_n$;
and $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$ almost surely.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{thm:generic_S} the matrix $\bm{Q}_n$ is almost surely well
defined for $n \geqslant p$, so $\bm{e}_n$ is.
By Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency} and the fact that $\mathcal{U}_0$ is
an open neighbourhood of $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$, almost surely
$[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2] \in \mathcal{U}_0$ for all large $n$;
in particular $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$.
On $\mathcal{A}_n$ we have $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \in B_{\rho}$ by
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart}, and since
$\mathcal{U}_0 \subseteq U$ the formula of
Proposition~\ref{thm:logarithmic_map} applies and reads
\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n
  = d\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}\bracket{\bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0,\ \hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \sigma_0^2}}
  = d\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}(\bm{e}_n).
\end{equation*}
As observed in the proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:logarithmic_map},
$\bm{e}_n$ is a horizontal vector at $(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$, so
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) = \bm{e}_n$ by \eqref{eq:lift_def}.
Finally let $\epsilon \in (0,\rho)$.
Then $\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(B_{\epsilon})$ is an open neighbourhood
of $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$, so almost surely
$[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$ lies in it for large $n$, and injectivity of
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ on $B_{\rho}$ then forces
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} < \epsilon$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Assumption III.2 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} postulates that the estimator
almost surely avoids the cut locus of the true parameter, so that the
logarithmic map may be applied to it, and
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} is what plays that role here.
We cannot verify that assumption literally, because $M$ is incomplete and
the discussion following Proposition~\ref{thm:logarithmic_map} shows that
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ genuinely fails to be globally defined.
What Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} provides instead is the only
thing the asymptotic theory ever uses: an event of probability tending to
one on which the intrinsic error is well defined, together with the
explicit Procrustes description \eqref{eq:e_n_def} of it.
Since modifying a sequence of random vectors on a sequence of events of
vanishing probability affects neither its limit in probability nor its
limit in distribution, all statements below are insensitive to the
convention adopted off $\mathcal{A}_n$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Asymptotic linearity}

We can now state the definition, which is Definition 3.10 of the
companion draft and, in the language of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026},
the hypothesis of their Lemma III.3.

\begin{definition}\label{def:asymptotic_linearity}
Let $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$ be a sequence of estimators for which the
intrinsic estimation error $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ of \eqref{eq:e_n_def} is
defined on events of probability tending to one.
The sequence is said to be \textit{asymptotically linear at $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$
with influence operator} $\text{IF}$
if $\text{IF} : \mathbb{R}^p \to T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$ is measurable,
centred and square integrable, that is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_moments}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)} = \bm{0}
  \qquad \text{and} \qquad
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\abs{\text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)}^2} < \infty,
\end{equation}
and if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:asymptotic_linearity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n \text{IF}(\bm{X}_i) + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
the remainder being $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ with respect to the norm
$\abs{\, \cdot \,}$ of $T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
Both sides of \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} live in the single fixed
vector space $T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$, so the expression is meaningful even
though $M$ is curved; this is exactly the device by which
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} transport the classical theory to manifolds.
Note also that $\text{IF}$ is required to take values in the
\textit{tangent} space, whereas the score \eqref{eq:score} is a linear
functional, that is an element of the \textit{cotangent} space;
the inverse Fisher operator \eqref{eq:G_inverse_def} is precisely
what converts one into the other.
\end{remark}

The following theorem is the main result of this section.
It is the analogue of Theorem 5.3 of the companion draft, and it
instantiates Corollary V.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} for the
probabilistic principal component analysis model.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:MLE_CLT}
Let $1 \leqslant q < p$ and let $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in M$.
Then the maximum likelihood estimator $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$ of
Definition~\ref{def:MLE} is asymptotically linear at $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$
in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity},
with influence operator
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_def}
  \text{IF}(\bm{x}) := G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{s(\bm{x};[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])}
  \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M .
\end{equation}
That is,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_linear}
  \sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]}
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n
  G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{s(\bm{X}_i;[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
and consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_CLT}
  \sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]}
  \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0}, G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}}
  \qquad \text{on } T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M,
\end{equation}
the Gaussian measure on $T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$ whose covariance operator is
the inverse Fisher information operator.
Equivalently, in terms of the Procrustes aligned error \eqref{eq:e_n_def},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0,\ \hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \sigma_0^2}
  \rightsquigarrow
  \iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}\bracket{N\bracket{\bm{0}, G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}}}
\end{equation}
as random elements of $\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times \mathbb{R}$;
the limit is a centred Gaussian law carried by the
$d$-dimensional subspace $H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $G := G_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$, $\iota := \iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$
and $s(\bm{x}) := s(\bm{x};[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])$ throughout.
The strategy is the one of Step 2 of the companion draft:
in the normal chart supplied by Lemma~\ref{thm:chart} the family
$\mathcal{P}$ becomes an ordinary parametric family indexed by an open
subset of a Euclidean space, and $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ becomes an ordinary
$M$-estimator, so that Theorem 5.23 of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}
applies once its four hypotheses have been verified.
The essential structural point, which makes every verification a finite
computation, is that by Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} the
exponential map is \textit{linear} in the horizontal lift, so no
approximation of $\text{Exp}$ is ever needed.

\textbf{Step 1: the model in normal coordinates.}
Fix a $g_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$-orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ of
$T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$ and identify $T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$ with
$\mathbb{R}^d$ through it; under this identification $B_{\rho}$ is the
open Euclidean ball of radius $\rho$ and $\abs{\, \cdot \,}$ is the
Euclidean norm.
For $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ put $(\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}, b_{\bm{u}}) := \iota(\bm{u})$
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_u_def}
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} := \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})}
  = (\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})^T
  + (\sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})\bm{I}_p,
\end{equation}
using Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map} and \eqref{eq:Sigma_Phi_def}.
Because $\iota$ is linear, expanding \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_def} gives the
\textit{exact} identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} = \bm{\Sigma}_0
  + \bracket{\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T + b_{\bm{u}}\bm{I}_p}
  + \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T
  = \bm{\Sigma}_0 + D_{\bm{u}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])
  + \bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}^T,
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional}: as a function of $\bm{u}$, the covariance
matrix is a quadratic polynomial with no remainder whatsoever.
Set, for $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ and $\bm{x} \in \mathbb{R}^p$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:m_def}
  m(\bm{u};\bm{x}) := \log p\bracket{\bm{x}; \text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})}
  = -\frac{p}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{1}{2}\log\det \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}
  - \frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^T\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{x},
\end{equation}
which in the notation \eqref{eq:l_def} is $l_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})(\bm{x})$,
and write $\mathbb{M}_n(\bm{u}) := \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n m(\bm{u};\bm{X}_i)$
and $M(\bm{u}) := \mathbb{E}\squared{m(\bm{u};\bm{X}_1)}$.
By \eqref{eq:loglik} and Lemma~\ref{thm:chart},
on the event $\mathcal{A}_n$ the vector $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ almost surely
maximizes $\mathbb{M}_n$ over $B_{\rho}$, since by
Lemma~\ref{thm:generic_S} and Definition~\ref{def:MLE} the point
$[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$ almost surely maximizes
$\mathbb{L}_n$ over all of $M$, and there it lies in the chart.
On the remaining null event $\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{0}$, because
$[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2] = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$ by
Definition~\ref{def:MLE}.

\textbf{Step 2: uniform spectral bounds.}
Let $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$.
Since $\iota$ is an isometry, $\|\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\|_F \leqslant \rho$
and $\abs{b_{\bm{u}}} \leqslant \rho$; as $\rho < \sigma_0^2$, formula
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_def} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:spectral_bounds}
  c_1 \bm{I}_p \preceq \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} \preceq c_2 \bm{I}_p,
  \qquad
  c_1 := \sigma_0^2 - \rho > 0,
  \qquad
  c_2 := \bracket{\sigma_{\text{max}}(\bm{W}_0)+\rho}^2 + \sigma_0^2 + \rho,
\end{equation}
the lower bound because $(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})^T
\succeq \bm{0}$, and the upper bound because
$\sigma_{\text{max}}(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}) \leqslant
\sigma_{\text{max}}(\bm{W}_0) + \|\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\|_F$.
In particular $\|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\|_2 \leqslant c_1^{-1}$
and $\|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}\|_F \leqslant \sqrt{p}\,c_2$
uniformly on $B_{\rho}$.

\textbf{Step 3: smoothness and the derivative of the criterion.}
On the open cone of positive definite matrices the maps
$\bm{\Sigma}\mapsto \bm{\Sigma}^{-1}$ and $\bm{\Sigma}\mapsto\log\det\bm{\Sigma}$
are real analytic, and $\bm{u}\mapsto\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}$ is a polynomial by
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}; hence for every fixed $\bm{x}$ the function
$\bm{u} \mapsto m(\bm{u};\bm{x})$ is $C^{\infty}$ on $B_{\rho}$.
Its differential is computed exactly as in \eqref{eq:l_derivative}: for
$\bm{h} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:m_derivative}
  \nabla m(\bm{u};\bm{x})[\bm{h}]
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\curly{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}
  \squared{\bm{x}\bm{x}^T - \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}}\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}
