  \bracket{D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})}}},
\end{equation}
where, by \eqref{eq:Sigma_derivative},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_derivative_u}
  D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})}
  = \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})^T
  + (\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}})\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p,
\end{equation}
which is linear in $\bm{h}$ and affine in $\bm{u}$.
Taking $\bm{u} = \bm{0}$ in \eqref{eq:m_derivative} and comparing with
\eqref{eq:score} shows
\begin{equation}\label{eq:score_is_gradient}
  \nabla m(\bm{0};\bm{x})[\bm{h}] = s(\bm{x})(\bm{h}),
\end{equation}
so that the gradient of the criterion at the truth is the score.

\textbf{Step 4: a square integrable Lipschitz envelope.}
Let $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ and let $\bm{h}$ be a unit vector.
From \eqref{eq:Sigma_derivative_u} and
$\|\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{C}^T\|_F \leqslant \|\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\|_F\|\bm{C}\|_2$,
\begin{equation*}
  \left\|D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u})}\right\|_F
  \leqslant 2\bracket{\sigma_{\text{max}}(\bm{W}_0)+\rho} + \sqrt{p}
  =: c_3 .
\end{equation*}
Combining this with $\abs{\tr(\bm{Z}\bm{Y})}\leqslant \|\bm{Z}\|_F\|\bm{Y}\|_F$
and the bounds of Step 2, \eqref{eq:m_derivative} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:envelope}
  \sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}=1}\abs{\nabla m(\bm{u};\bm{x})[\bm{h}]}
  \leqslant \frac{c_3}{2c_1^{2}}\bracket{\|\bm{x}\|^2 + \sqrt{p}\,c_2}
  =: \dot{m}(\bm{x})
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u} \in B_{\rho}.
\end{equation}
Since $\bm{X}_1$ is Gaussian, $\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{X}_1\|^4] < \infty$
and therefore $\mathbb{E}[\dot{m}(\bm{X}_1)^2] < \infty$.
As $B_{\rho}$ is convex and $m(\, \cdot \,;\bm{x})$ is $C^1$ there,
the mean value theorem yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lipschitz}
  \abs{m(\bm{u}_1;\bm{x}) - m(\bm{u}_2;\bm{x})}
  \leqslant \dot{m}(\bm{x})\abs{\bm{u}_1-\bm{u}_2}
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u}_1, \bm{u}_2 \in B_{\rho}.
\end{equation}

\textbf{Step 5: the population criterion.}
Since $\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_1\bm{X}_1^T] = \bm{\Sigma}_0$, \eqref{eq:m_def} gives
the closed form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M_def}
  M(\bm{u}) = -\frac{p}{2}\log(2\pi) - \frac{1}{2}\log \det \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}
  - \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_0},
\end{equation}
which, by the argument of Step 3, is $C^{\infty}$ on $B_{\rho}$;
in particular no differentiation under the integral sign is needed
anywhere below.
Subtracting $M(\bm{0})$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:KL}
  M(\bm{0}) - M(\bm{u})
  = \frac{1}{2}\squared{\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_0} - p
  + \log\frac{\det\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}}{\det\bm{\Sigma}_0}}
  = \frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^p \bracket{\mu_j - 1 - \log\mu_j},
\end{equation}
where $\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_p > 0$ are the eigenvalues of the positive definite
matrix $\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-\frac{1}{2}}$,
which is similar to $\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_0$.
The elementary inequality $\mu - 1 - \log \mu \geqslant 0$, with equality
only at $\mu = 1$, shows that $M(\bm{0}) - M(\bm{u}) \geqslant 0$ with
equality if and only if all $\mu_j = 1$, that is if and only if
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} = \bm{\Sigma}_0$.
By the injectivity of $\bm{\Sigma}$ established in
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} this happens exactly when
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{u}) = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$, and by
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart} exactly when $\bm{u} = \bm{0}$.
Hence $\bm{0}$ is the unique maximizer of $M$ over $B_{\rho}$.

We now compute the second order Taylor expansion of $M$ at $\bm{0}$.
Fix $\bm{h} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$, write
$(\bm{U},b) := \iota(\bm{h})$, and let
$\bm{\Sigma}(t) := \bm{\Sigma}_{t\bm{h}}$.
By \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ray}
  \bm{\Sigma}(t) = \bm{\Sigma}_0 + t\bm{\Delta} + t^2 \bm{U}\bm{U}^T,
  \qquad
  \bm{\Delta} := D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])
  = \bm{U}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}^T + b\bm{I}_p,
\end{equation}
so $\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}(0) = \bm{\Delta}$ and
$\ddot{\bm{\Sigma}}(0) = 2\bm{U}\bm{U}^T$ exactly.
Using $\frac{d}{dt}\log\det\bm{\Sigma} = \tr(\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}})$
and $\frac{d}{dt}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1} = -\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}$,
the two terms of \eqref{eq:M_def} contribute
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\squared{-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\bm{\Sigma}(t)}
  = -\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{\Delta}},
  \qquad
  \frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\squared{-\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}(t)^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_0}}
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{\Delta}},
\end{equation*}
whose sum vanishes, so $\nabla M(\bm{0}) = 0$, as it must since $\bm{0}$ is
an interior maximizer.
Differentiating once more,
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\bigg|_{t=0}\squared{-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\bm{\Sigma}(t)}
  = -\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\ddot{\bm{\Sigma}}(0)}
  + \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{\Delta}}^2}
\end{equation*}
and, since
$\frac{d}{dt}\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}}
= \bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\ddot{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}
- 2\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}$,
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\bigg|_{t=0}\squared{-\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}(t)^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_0}}
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\ddot{\bm{\Sigma}}(0)}
  - \tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{\Delta}}^2}.
\end{equation*}
The terms in $\ddot{\bm{\Sigma}}(0)$ cancel identically and we are left with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hessian}
  \frac{d^2}{dt^2}\bigg|_{t=0}M(t\bm{h})
  = -\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])}^2}
  = -G(\bm{h},\bm{h})
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:G_def}.
As $M$ is twice continuously differentiable on $B_{\rho}$, its Hessian at
$\bm{0}$ is the symmetric bilinear form determined by the quadratic form
\eqref{eq:hessian}, namely $-G$, and Taylor's theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M_taylor}
  M(\bm{u}) = M(\bm{0}) - \frac{1}{2}G(\bm{u},\bm{u}) + o\bracket{\abs{\bm{u}}^2}
  \qquad \text{as } \bm{u} \to \bm{0}.
\end{equation}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} the form $G$ is positive definite, so the second
derivative matrix of $M$ at $\bm{0}$ is symmetric and nonsingular.
Incidentally, \eqref{eq:hessian} is the manifold version of the classical
identity between the Fisher information and the expected negative Hessian
of the log-likelihood, recorded as Remark III.4 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026};
here it comes out of \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} by a finite computation
precisely because the exponential map is linear in the horizontal lift.

\textbf{Step 6: application of the $M$-estimation theorem.}
We check the four hypotheses of Theorem 5.23 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998} for the criterion functions
$\{m(\bm{u};\, \cdot \,)\}_{\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}}$ indexed by the open
subset $B_{\rho}$ of $\mathbb{R}^d$, at the point $\bm{u} = \bm{0}$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For each $\bm{u}$ the map $\bm{x}\mapsto m(\bm{u};\bm{x})$ is
  measurable, being continuous, and for each $\bm{x}$ the map
  $\bm{u}\mapsto m(\bm{u};\bm{x})$ is differentiable at $\bm{0}$ with
  derivative $s(\bm{x}) \in L^2$, by Step 3 and \eqref{eq:score_is_gradient};
  square integrability of the score is \eqref{eq:r_L2norm}.
  \item The Lipschitz condition with square integrable envelope is
  \eqref{eq:lipschitz} and \eqref{eq:envelope}.
  \item The map $\bm{u}\mapsto M(\bm{u})$ admits the second order Taylor
  expansion \eqref{eq:M_taylor} at the maximizer $\bm{0}$, with
  nonsingular symmetric second derivative matrix $-G$, by Step 5.
  \item By Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined},
  $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$ in probability, and
  $\mathbb{M}_n(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \geqslant \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{0})$ holds exactly:
  on $\mathcal{A}_n$ because $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ maximizes $\mathbb{M}_n$ over
  $B_{\rho} \ni \bm{0}$ by Step 1, and off $\mathcal{A}_n$ because there
  $\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{0}$.
  In particular
  $\mathbb{M}_n(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \geqslant \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{0}) - o_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})$.
\end{enumerate}
The cited theorem therefore yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vdV_conclusion}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n
  = \bm{G}^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{s}(\bm{X}_i) + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
where, in the orthonormal coordinates of Step 1, $\bm{G}$ is the
$d \times d$ matrix with entries $\bm{G}_{jk} = G(\bm{e}_j,\bm{e}_k)$ and
$\bm{s}(\bm{x}) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ is the vector with entries
$\bm{s}(\bm{x})_j = s(\bm{x})(\bm{e}_j)$.
Now if $\text{IF}(\bm{x}) = \sum_{j}c_j(\bm{x})\bm{e}_j$ is defined by
\eqref{eq:IF_def}, then \eqref{eq:G_inverse_def} reads
$\sum_k \bm{G}_{jk}c_k(\bm{x}) = s(\bm{x})(\bm{e}_j)$ for every $j$,
that is $\bm{c}(\bm{x}) = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{s}(\bm{x})$;
so \eqref{eq:vdV_conclusion} is precisely
\eqref{eq:MLE_linear} and \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity}.

The requirements \eqref{eq:IF_moments} hold: the score has mean zero
and covariance $G$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, that is
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)] = \bm{0}$ and
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)^T] = \bm{G}$,
whence $\mathbb{E}[\bm{c}(\bm{X}_1)] = \bm{0}$ and
$\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{c}(\bm{X}_1)\|^2] = \tr(\bm{G}^{-1}) < \infty$.
The vectors $\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1), \bm{s}(\bm{X}_2), \ldots$ being independent,
identically distributed, centred and square integrable, the multivariate
central limit theorem gives
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n\bm{s}(\bm{X}_i) \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G})$,
so that by \eqref{eq:vdV_conclusion}, Slutsky's lemma and the continuous
mapping theorem,
\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{\bm{0}, \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G}\bm{G}^{-1}} = N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}},
\end{equation*}
which is \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}: in the $g_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$-orthonormal basis
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ the covariance operator $G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$
is represented by the matrix $\bm{G}^{-1}$.

\textbf{Step 7: the Procrustes form.}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined},
$\bm{e}_n = \iota(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ on $\mathcal{A}_n$, and
$\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n)\to 1$.
If $\bm{\xi}_n$ and $\bm{\eta}_n$ agree on events of probability tending
to one and $\bm{\xi}_n \rightsquigarrow \bm{\xi}$, then
$\bm{\eta}_n\rightsquigarrow \bm{\xi}$ as well, since for every bounded
Lipschitz test function $\psi$ one has
$\abs{\mathbb{E}\psi(\bm{\xi}_n)-\mathbb{E}\psi(\bm{\eta}_n)}
\leqslant 2\|\psi\|_{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n^c)\to 0$
by the portmanteau theorem.
Applying this and the continuous mapping theorem to the linear isometry
$\iota$ turns \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} into \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes};
the limit is carried by $\iota(T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M) = H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$,
which by Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} has dimension $d$.
\end{proof}

The proof just given is a proof by $M$-estimation.
It verifies the hypotheses of a general theorem about criterion
functions, and it therefore uses Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} only as a
repository of formulas --- the expression \eqref{eq:score} for the
score, its first two moments \eqref{eq:r_L2norm}, and the positive
definiteness of $G$ --- while Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} is not used at
all.
The substance of Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, namely that the square root
likelihood is differentiable in $L^2(\mathbb{R}^p)$, never enters, and
that is precisely why Step 5 must compute the Hessian of the population
criterion by hand: \eqref{eq:hessian} is the information identity, and a
theorem about arbitrary criterion functions cannot be expected to know
it.

The second proof below reverses that emphasis.
It works throughout with the local experiments indexed by the tangent
vectors $\bm{h} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$, that is with the points
$\text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}(\bm{h}/\sqrt{n})$ of $M$, and with their log-likelihood
ratio process; its analytic engine is Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}, that is
Proposition III.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, whose sole input is the
differentiability in quadratic mean supplied by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM};
and it quotes no asymptotic theorem of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}.
What replaces the $M$-estimation machinery is the geometry of $(M,g)$.
Two features of it are used, and they are used in different places.
The first is that the exponential map is affine in the horizontal lift,
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, so that
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} is an exact polynomial identity: every
expansion below terminates, and none of the curvature corrections that
Lemma A.1 and Lemma A.6 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} would supply on a
general manifold is needed.
The second is that the vertical and horizontal spaces meet trivially,
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle}, which is what makes $G$
positive definite in Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} and hence what produces the
quadratic separation \eqref{eq:alt_separation} of the Kullback--Leibler
divergence; that separation, and not a maximal inequality, is what
delivers the $\sqrt{n}$ rate.

Throughout the proof $G$, $\iota$ and $s(\bm{x})$ keep the meaning fixed
at the beginning of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}; the
orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$, the identification
$T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d$, the ball $B_{\rho}$, the
matrix $\bm{G}$ and the coordinate vector $\bm{s}(\bm{x})$ are those of
its Steps 1 and 6; the functions $m$, $\mathbb{M}_n$ and $M$ are those
of \eqref{eq:m_def} and \eqref{eq:M_def}; and $c_1$, $c_2$, $c_3$ are
the constants of its Steps 2 and 4.
We write $\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{G}) > 0$ for the smallest eigenvalue
of $\bm{G}$, positive by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, and we use freely that
all norms on $\text{Sym}(p)$ are equivalent.

\begin{proof}[Alternative proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}]
\textbf{Step 1: the local experiment, and the score as a linear
functional of $\bm{S}_n$.}
Abbreviate

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_Z_n_def}
  \bm{Z}_n := \sqrt{n}\bracket{\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0} \in \text{Sym}(p),
  \qquad
  \bm{\Delta}_n := \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{s}(\bm{X}_i) \in \mathbb{R}^d,
\end{equation}
and, for $\bm{h} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$ with
$\abs{\bm{h}} < \rho\sqrt{n}$, let

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_Lambda_def}
  \Lambda_n(\bm{h})
  := \sum_{i=1}^n \log
  \frac{p\bracket{\bm{X}_i; \text{Exp}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}}}{p(\bm{X}_i;[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])}
  = n\squared{\mathbb{M}_n\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}} - \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{0})}
\end{equation}
be the log-likelihood ratio process of the local experiments, which is
the object appearing in Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}.
Since $\bm{x}\bm{x}^T$ enters \eqref{eq:score} linearly and
$\bm{Z}_n = n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n
\bracket{\bm{X}_i\bm{X}_i^T - \bm{\Sigma}_0}$, formula \eqref{eq:score}
gives the exact identity

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_score_linear}
  \begin{gathered}
  \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n s(\bm{X}_i)(\bm{h})
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\curly{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{Z}_n\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])}
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{B}_{\bm{h}}\bm{Z}_n},
  \\
  \bm{B}_{\bm{h}} := \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  \in \text{Sym}(p) .
  \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The score is thus an exact linear image of the single random matrix
$\bm{Z}_n$; this is the sufficiency of $\bm{S}_n$, part 4 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties}, made quantitative, and it is what allows
the entire stochastic side of the argument to rest on the one weak limit
\eqref{eq:S_n_CLT}.
Two consequences are recorded at once.
First, by \eqref{eq:S_n_covariance} applied with
$\bm{B} = \bm{B}_{\bm{h}_1}$ and $\bm{C} = \bm{B}_{\bm{h}_2}$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_score_moments}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{\Delta}_n} = \bm{0},
  \qquad
  \text{Cov}\bracket{\bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}_1, \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}_2}
  = \frac{n}{4}\cdot\frac{2}{n}
  \tr\bracket{\bm{B}_{\bm{h}_1}\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{B}_{\bm{h}_2}\bm{\Sigma}_0}
  = G(\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2)
\end{equation}
for every $n$, the last equality being \eqref{eq:G_def}.
Second, applying the continuous mapping theorem to the linear map
$\bm{Z}\mapsto \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\tr(\bm{B}_{\bm{e}_j}\bm{Z})}_{j=1}^d$
and to \eqref{eq:S_n_CLT},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_score_CLT}
  \bm{\Delta}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}),
  \qquad \text{and in particular} \qquad
  \bm{\Delta}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
  \quad
  \|\bm{Z}_n\|_F = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1) .
\end{equation}
No separate central limit theorem for the score is needed.

\textbf{Step 2: the criterion is exactly affine in $\bm{S}_n$.}
By \eqref{eq:m_def} and
$\bm{x}^T\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{x}
= \tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{x}\bm{x}^T}$,

\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{u}) = -\frac{p}{2}\log(2\pi)
  - \frac{1}{2}\log\det\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}
  - \frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{S}_n},
  \qquad \bm{u} \in B_{\rho},
\end{equation*}
which differs from \eqref{eq:M_def} only in that $\bm{S}_n$ replaces
$\bm{\Sigma}_0$; hence
$\mathbb{M}_n(\bm{u}) - M(\bm{u})
= -\frac{1}{2}\tr\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}(\bm{S}_n-\bm{\Sigma}_0)}$
and, writing $K(\bm{u}) := M(\bm{0}) - M(\bm{u})$ for the
Kullback--Leibler divergence evaluated in \eqref{eq:KL},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_exact}
  \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{0}) - \mathbb{M}_n(\bm{u})
  = K(\bm{u})
  + \frac{1}{2}\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}}\bracket{\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}}
  \qquad \text{for every } \bm{u} \in B_{\rho} \text{ and every } n .
\end{equation}
This identity is exact: no remainder, no expectation, no limit.
Equivalently, in terms of \eqref{eq:alt_Lambda_def},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_Lambda}
  \Lambda_n(\bm{h}) = -\,nK\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  - \frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}}\bm{Z}_n},
  \qquad \abs{\bm{h}} < \rho\sqrt{n} .
\end{equation}
The log-likelihood ratio process is therefore a deterministic smooth
function of $\bm{h}$ plus a linear functional of $\bm{Z}_n$ whose
coefficient is again deterministic and smooth in $\bm{h}$.
This is the structural reason why no empirical process, no envelope
function and no maximal inequality will be required below.

\textbf{Step 3: elementary bounds on the chart.}
Let $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$.
Since $\iota$ is an isometry, $\|\bm{U}_{\bm{u}}\|_F \leqslant \abs{\bm{u}}$,
and $\|D_{\bm{u}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])\|_F
\leqslant c_3\abs{\bm{u}}$ by the bound of Step 4 of the previous proof
read at $\bm{u} = \bm{0}$ together with linearity in the direction, so
that \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_Sigma_lipschitz}
  \|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F
  \leqslant c_3\abs{\bm{u}} + \abs{\bm{u}}^2
  \leqslant (c_3+\rho)\abs{\bm{u}} .
\end{equation}
Since $\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1} - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
= \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0 - \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}}\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}$
and $\|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\|_2 \leqslant c_1^{-1}$,
$\|\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\|_2 \leqslant c_1^{-1}$ by \eqref{eq:spectral_bounds},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_inverse_lipschitz}
  \|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1} - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\|_F
  \leqslant c_1^{-2}\|\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}} - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F
  \leqslant c_4\abs{\bm{u}},
  \qquad c_4 := \frac{c_3+\rho}{c_1^{2}} .
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every $K > 0$, every $\bm{h}$ with
$\abs{\bm{h}} \leqslant K$ and every $n > K^2/\rho^2$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_inverse_expansion}
  \left\|\sqrt{n}\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}} + \bm{B}_{\bm{h}}\right\|_F
  \leqslant \frac{c_5(K)}{\sqrt{n}},
  \qquad
  c_5(K) := \bracket{c_4c_3c_1^{-1} + c_1^{-2}}K^2 .
\end{equation}
Indeed, taking $\bm{u} = \bm{h}/\sqrt{n}$ in the factorization above and
using \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic},

\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}} + \bm{B}_{\bm{h}}
  = -\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1} - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}}
  D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}([\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
  - \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1}
  \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1},
\end{equation*}
and \eqref{eq:alt_inverse_lipschitz} bounds the first term in Frobenius
norm by $c_4c_3c_1^{-1}K^2n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ and the second by
$c_1^{-2}K^2n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$.
Combining \eqref{eq:alt_inverse_expansion} with
\eqref{eq:alt_score_linear} and
$\abs{\tr(\bm{Y}\bm{Z})} \leqslant \|\bm{Y}\|_F\|\bm{Z}\|_F$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_linear_term}
  \sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant K}
  \abs{-\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2}\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}}\bm{Z}_n} - \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}}
  \leqslant \frac{c_5(K)}{2\sqrt{n}}\|\bm{Z}_n\|_F
  = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}} .
\end{equation}

\textbf{Step 4: the Hessian of the Kullback--Leibler divergence, read
off from local asymptotic normality.}
By \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} the map
$\bm{u}\mapsto\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}$ is polynomial and, by
\eqref{eq:spectral_bounds}, takes its values in the open cone of
positive definite matrices, on which $\log\det$ and inversion are real
analytic; hence $K$ is $C^{\infty}$ on $B_{\rho}$.
By \eqref{eq:KL} we have $K \geqslant 0$ and $K(\bm{0}) = 0$, so
$\bm{0}$ is an interior minimizer of $K$ and therefore

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_K_gradient}
  K(\bm{0}) = 0, \qquad \nabla K(\bm{0}) = \bm{0} ;
\end{equation}
this requires no computation, only the nonnegativity of the
Kullback--Leibler divergence.
Now fix $\bm{h} \in T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M$.
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}, applied at the true parameter
$[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$ and written in the coordinates of Step 1, states
that $\Lambda_n(\bm{h}) = \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}
- \frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h}) + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
Substituting this and \eqref{eq:alt_linear_term} into
\eqref{eq:alt_Lambda},

\begin{equation*}
  nK\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  = -\Lambda_n(\bm{h}) + \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  = \frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h}) + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1) .
\end{equation*}
The left hand side is a deterministic sequence of real numbers, and a
deterministic sequence which equals a constant up to $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$
converges to that constant; hence
$nK(\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}) \to \frac{1}{2}G(\bm{h},\bm{h})$ for every
$\bm{h}$.
On the other hand \eqref{eq:alt_K_gradient} and Taylor's theorem give
$nK(\bm{h}/\sqrt{n})
= \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\nabla^2K(\bm{\xi}_{n,\bm{h}})\bm{h}$
for some $\bm{\xi}_{n,\bm{h}}$ on the segment joining $\bm{0}$ to
$\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}$, and $\nabla^2K$ is continuous, so
$nK(\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}) \to \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\nabla^2K(\bm{0})\bm{h}$.
Comparing the two limits and polarizing,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_hessian}
  \nabla^2 K(\bm{0}) = \bm{G},
  \qquad \text{equivalently} \qquad
  -\nabla^2 M(\bm{0}) = \bm{G} .
\end{equation}
This is \eqref{eq:hessian} again, the manifold form of the identity
between the Fisher information and the Hessian of the Kullback--Leibler
divergence recorded as Remark III.4 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}.
It has not been computed here: it has been read off the local asymptotic
normality expansion, which is itself only a restatement of the
differentiability in quadratic mean of Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}.
Two corollaries follow.
First, since $\nabla^2K$ is continuous, the Taylor argument above is
uniform on compacta,

\begin{equation*}
  \sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant K}
  \abs{nK\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  - \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}}
  \leqslant \frac{K^2}{2}\sup_{\abs{\bm{\xi}}\leqslant \frac{K}{\sqrt{n}}}
  \left\|\nabla^2K(\bm{\xi}) - \nabla^2K(\bm{0})\right\|
  \longrightarrow 0,
\end{equation*}
so that \eqref{eq:alt_Lambda} and \eqref{eq:alt_linear_term} upgrade
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} to its locally uniform form: for every
$K > 0$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_ULAN}
  \sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant K}
  \abs{\Lambda_n(\bm{h}) - \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}
  + \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}}
  \longrightarrow 0
  \qquad \text{in probability}.
\end{equation}
The upgrade is free precisely because, by \eqref{eq:alt_Lambda}, all the
dependence of $\Lambda_n$ on $\bm{h}$ is deterministic and smooth, the
only randomness being the single matrix $\bm{Z}_n$.
Second, \eqref{eq:alt_K_gradient}, \eqref{eq:alt_hessian} and Taylor's
theorem give
$K(\bm{u}) = \frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^T\bm{G}\bm{u} + o(\abs{\bm{u}}^2)$,
so there is $\rho_1 \in (0,\rho]$ with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_separation}
  K(\bm{u}) \geqslant
  \frac{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{G})}{4}\abs{\bm{u}}^2
  \qquad \text{whenever } \abs{\bm{u}} \leqslant \rho_1 .
\end{equation}
This quadratic separation is where the positive definiteness of the
Fisher operator, that is, by the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, the
transversality
$V_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)} \cap H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)} = \{\bm{0}\}$
of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle}, is used quantitatively.

\textbf{Step 5: the $\sqrt{n}$ rate, by the basic inequality.}
On $\mathcal{A}_n$ the vector $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ maximizes $\mathbb{M}_n$
over $B_{\rho}$, by Step 1 of the previous proof, and
$\bm{0} \in B_{\rho}$; hence
$\mathbb{M}_n(\bm{0}) - \mathbb{M}_n(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \leqslant 0$, and
\eqref{eq:alt_exact} together with \eqref{eq:alt_inverse_lipschitz}
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_basic_inequality}
  K(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)
  \leqslant \frac{1}{2}
  \abs{\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^{-1}
  - \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}}\bracket{\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}}}
  \leqslant \frac{c_4}{2}\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  \,\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n .
\end{equation}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$
almost surely, so for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is
$n_{\varepsilon}$ with
$\mathbb{P}\bracket{\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} \leqslant \rho_1
\text{ for all } n \geqslant n_{\varepsilon}} \geqslant 1-\varepsilon$.
On the intersection of that event with $\mathcal{A}_n$ the separation
\eqref{eq:alt_separation} applies to $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ and, combined with
\eqref{eq:alt_basic_inequality}, yields
$\frac{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{G})}{4}\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^2
\leqslant \frac{c_4}{2}\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
\|\bm{S}_n-\bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F$, that is

\begin{equation*}
  \abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} \leqslant
  \frac{2c_4}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{G})}
  \|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F .
\end{equation*}
Since $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$, since $\varepsilon$ was
arbitrary, and since
$\|\bm{S}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0\|_F = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ by
part 5 of Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties}, we conclude that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_rate}
  \hat{\bm{h}}_n := \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1) .
\end{equation}
This is the classical basic inequality argument in its simplest possible
form.
It is available here because \eqref{eq:alt_exact} is exact and affine in
$\bm{S}_n$, so that the stochastic part of the criterion is controlled by
the single Lipschitz bound \eqref{eq:alt_inverse_lipschitz}, and because
its deterministic part is quadratically separated by
\eqref{eq:alt_separation}.
Beyond Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency}, only the geometry of the chart
has been used.

\textbf{Step 6: identification of the local maximizer.}
By \eqref{eq:alt_Lambda_def} and Step 1 of the previous proof, on
$\mathcal{A}_n$ the vector $\hat{\bm{h}}_n$ maximizes $\Lambda_n$ over
$\curly{\abs{\bm{h}} < \rho\sqrt{n}}$.
Since $\bm{G}$ is positive definite by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, the
quadratic

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_Q_n}
  Q_n(\bm{h}) := \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h}
  - \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}
  = \frac{1}{2}\bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n
  - \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{h} - \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n}^T\bm{G}
  \bracket{\bm{h} - \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n}
\end{equation}
has the unique maximizer $\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n$, which is
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ by \eqref{eq:alt_score_CLT}.
Let $\varepsilon > 0$.
By \eqref{eq:alt_rate}, \eqref{eq:alt_score_CLT} and
$\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_n)\to 1$ there are $K > 0$ and $n_0 > K^2/\rho^2$
such that the event

\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal{E}_n := \mathcal{A}_n
  \cap \curly{\abs{\hat{\bm{h}}_n} \leqslant K}
  \cap \curly{\abs{\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n}\leqslant K}
\end{equation*}
has $\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_n) \geqslant 1-\varepsilon$ for all
$n \geqslant n_0$; on $\mathcal{E}_n$ both $\hat{\bm{h}}_n$ and
$\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n$ then lie in the ball
$\curly{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant K}$, on which $\Lambda_n$ is defined.
Put $r_n := \sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant K}
\abs{\Lambda_n(\bm{h}) - Q_n(\bm{h})}$, which is $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ by
\eqref{eq:alt_ULAN}.
On $\mathcal{E}_n$, using the maximality of $\hat{\bm{h}}_n$ for
$\Lambda_n$ in the middle step,

\begin{equation*}
  Q_n(\hat{\bm{h}}_n)
  \geqslant \Lambda_n(\hat{\bm{h}}_n) - r_n
  \geqslant \Lambda_n\bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n} - r_n
  \geqslant Q_n\bracket{\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n} - 2r_n ,
\end{equation*}
which, by the completed square in \eqref{eq:alt_Q_n}, is exactly

\begin{equation*}
  \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\hat{\bm{h}}_n - \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n}^T\bm{G}
  \bracket{\hat{\bm{h}}_n - \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n} \leqslant 2r_n,
  \qquad \text{whence} \qquad
  \abs{\hat{\bm{h}}_n - \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n}^2
  \leqslant \frac{4r_n}{\lambda_{\text{min}}(\bm{G})} .
\end{equation*}
Therefore
$\limsup_n \mathbb{P}\bracket{\abs{\hat{\bm{h}}_n
- \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n} > \delta} \leqslant \varepsilon$
for every $\delta > 0$, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:alt_conclusion}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n
  + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{eq:vdV_conclusion}.

\textbf{Step 7: conclusion.}
The passage from \eqref{eq:alt_conclusion} to \eqref{eq:MLE_linear}, the
verification of \eqref{eq:IF_moments} --- for which
\eqref{eq:alt_score_moments} may be quoted in place of
Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} --- and the Procrustes form
\eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes} are word for word Steps 6 and 7 of the
previous proof and are not repeated.
Finally \eqref{eq:alt_conclusion}, \eqref{eq:alt_score_CLT} and
Slutsky's lemma give
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow
N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G}\bm{G}^{-1}}
= N\bracket{\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}}$, which is \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:no_geodesic_convexity}
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} has the shape of Corollary V.1 of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, but neither proof given above is an application
of it.
That corollary is deduced there from their Proposition V.1, whose
standing hypothesis is that the loss
$\bm{x}\mapsto -\log p(\bm{x};\, \cdot \,)$ be almost surely
\textit{geodesically convex} on the parameter manifold, in the sense of
their Definition V.1.
That hypothesis is not available here.
A function $f$ convex along a geodesic $\gamma$ with
$\gamma(0) = \theta$ and $\nabla f(\theta) = \bm{0}$ satisfies
$f(\gamma(t)) \geqslant f(\gamma(0))$ for all $t \in [0,1]$, so a
geodesically convex function has no stationary point that fails to
minimize it among the points that a geodesic joins it to.
But the stationarity equations (7) and (8) of
\cite{Tipping_Bishop_1999}, recalled in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{thm:TB}, exhibit a stationary point of
\eqref{eq:loglik} for every selection of $q$ eigenvectors of $\bm{S}$
whose eigenvalues exceed the resulting $\hat{\sigma}^2$, and only the
selection of the $q$ largest ones gives the maximizer.
As soon as $p - q \geqslant 2$ a second admissible selection exists for
an open set of $\bm{S} \in \mathcal{O}$ --- take
$\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_{q+1}$ nearly equal and much larger than
$\lambda_{q+2},\ldots,\lambda_p$, and retain
$\lambda_2,\ldots,\lambda_{q+1}$ --- and by part 2 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties} the law of $\bm{S}_n$ charges every such
open set once $n \geqslant p$.
So $-\mathbb{L}_n$ has, with positive probability, stationary points on
$M$ that are not global minimizers, and the geodesic convexity of
Definition V.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} could hold only in the vacuous
way permitted by the incompleteness of $M$, that is if no geodesic of
$(M,g)$ joined such a point to the maximizer.
Neither proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} needs it.
What the second proof does use of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} is their
Definition III.1 and Proposition III.1, verified for $\mathcal{P}$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} and Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}; their Remark III.4,
obtained here as \eqref{eq:alt_hessian} rather than assumed; and their
Assumption III.2, whose role is played by
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}\label{thm:influence_relation}
The influence operator \eqref{eq:IF_def} satisfies the influence relation
of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, Lemma III.3, namely
\begin{equation}\label{eq:influence_relation}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)\otimes s(\bm{X}_1;[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2])}
  = \text{id}_{T_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}M}.
\end{equation}
Moreover it is the only influence operator with values in the span of the
score for which \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} can hold.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the coordinates of Step 6 of the previous proof,
\eqref{eq:influence_relation} reads
\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{c}(\bm{X}_1)\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)^T}
  = \bm{G}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\squared{\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1)^T}
  = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G} = \bm{I}_d .
\end{equation*}
For the uniqueness, an influence operator of the stated form is
$\text{IF}'(\bm{x}) = \bm{C}\bm{s}(\bm{x})$ for some $d\times d$ matrix
$\bm{C}$, and \eqref{eq:influence_relation}, which
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} show to be forced for regular asymptotically
linear sequences, gives $\bm{C}\bm{G} = \bm{I}_d$, that is
$\bm{C} = \bm{G}^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:MLE_CLT_comments}
Three comments on the content of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}.

First, \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes} is the well posed replacement of the
false naive statement discussed at the beginning of this section:
it is not the raw error $\hat{\bm{W}}_n - \bm{W}_0$ that is asymptotically
Gaussian, but the error measured after the optimal rotational alignment
$\hat{\bm{W}}_n \mapsto \hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n$, and the limit lives on the
horizontal space $H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$ only.
Viewed inside $\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times\mathbb{R} \cong \mathbb{R}^{pq+1}$
the limiting Gaussian law is degenerate, carrying no mass in the
$\frac{q(q-1)}{2}$ vertical directions of
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle}.
This degeneracy is a feature and not a defect: those are exactly the
directions along which the likelihood \eqref{eq:loglik} is constant,
so no data can ever be informative about them.

Second, \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} contains as a by-product the rate of
convergence, since it gives
$\abs{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}([\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2])}
= O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$
and hence, on $\mathcal{A}_n$, that the Riemannian distance between
$[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$ and $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$ is
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$.
This is the quantitative refinement of
Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency}.

Third, no assumption whatsoever has been made on the multiplicities of the
singular values of $\bm{W}_0$.
This is worth emphasizing, because the classical route to such a theorem,
namely perturbation of the eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ followed by the delta
method, requires the leading $q$ eigenvalues of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ to be simple.
When they are not, individual principal directions are genuinely not
asymptotically normal, and yet the point $[\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2]$
of $M$ still is: the quotient level limit simply does not see the
multiplicities.
The reason is Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, where positive definiteness of
$G_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ was obtained from
$V_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}\cap H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)} = \{\bm{0}\}$ alone.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} is the input required by the optimality theory of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}.
Together with the local asymptotic normality expansion of
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} it yields, through Le Cam's third lemma, that the
maximum likelihood estimator is locally regular in the sense of their
Definition III.5, and then their convolution theorem, Theorem III.1, and
their local asymptotic minimax theorem, Theorem III.2, identify
$G^{-1}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$ as the efficiency bound over all regular estimator
sequences; compare their Theorem V.1.
Carrying this out requires the parallel transport of $(M,g)$ and a
comparison between the transported and the coordinate errors, which are
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} and
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} of Section~\ref{sec:regularity};
the conclusions are Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular},
Theorem~\ref{thm:convolution} and Theorem~\ref{thm:LAM} there.
\end{remark}

\section{Parallel Transport, Local Regularity and Optimality}\label{sec:regularity}

Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} is a statement made entirely under the single
law $\mathbb{P}_{[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]}$, and it concerns a single
estimator.
The present section adds the two things it does not contain: a statement
about the behaviour of the maximum likelihood estimator under
\textit{neighbouring} parameters, which is local regularity, and a
statement about \textit{all other} estimators, which is optimality.
Both require comparing tangent vectors based at two different points of
$M$, so both require the parallel transport of $(M,g)$, which we compute
first.

Throughout this section we abbreviate

\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta_notation}
  \theta_0 := [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in M,
  \qquad
  \hat{\theta}_n := [\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2] \in M,
  \qquad
  G := G_{\theta_0},
  \qquad
  \iota := \iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)} .
\end{equation}
The letter $\theta$, which in Section~\ref{sec:basic_setup} denoted a
point of $\Theta$, was retired at the beginning of Section~\ref{sec:MLE}
when the coordinates on $\Theta$ were renamed $(\bm{W},\sigma^2)$, so no
confusion can arise: from here on $\theta$ always denotes a point of $M$.
We keep the orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ of
$T_{\theta_0}M$ and the identification $T_{\theta_0}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d$
of Step 1 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, and we write
$\bm{G}$ for the matrix of $G$ and $\bm{s}(\bm{x}) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ for
the coordinate vector of the score in that basis, exactly as in
\eqref{eq:vdV_conclusion}.
Finally we abbreviate the normalized score

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Delta_n_def}
  \bm{\Delta}_n := \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{s}(\bm{X}_i)
  \in \mathbb{R}^d ,
\end{equation}
so that Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} reads
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$
and Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} reads, for $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ with
coordinate vector still written $\bm{h}$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:LAN_coordinates}
  \Lambda_n(\bm{h}) := \sum_{i=1}^n
  \log \frac{p\bracket{\bm{X}_i; \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}}}{p(\bm{X}_i; \theta_0)}
  = \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h} - \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
\end{equation}
because $s(\bm{x})(\bm{h}) = \bm{s}(\bm{x})^T\bm{h}$ by bilinearity and
$\sum_i s(\bm{X}_i)(\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}) = \bm{\Delta}_n^T \bm{h}$.

\subsection*{Parallel transport along the geodesics of $M$}

The metric $g$ was constructed in Section~\ref{sec:basic_setup} so that
$\pi$ is a Riemannian submersion from the \textit{flat} manifold
$\Theta \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{pq+1}$, and Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}
exploited this to make the exponential map affine in the horizontal lift.
The same structure makes the parallel transport an explicit linear
ordinary differential equation.

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:parallel_transport}
Let $[\bm{A},s] \in M$, let $\bm{v} \in \mathcal{E}_{[\bm{A},s]}$ with
horizontal lift $(\bm{V},c) := \iota_{(\bm{A},s)}(\bm{v})$, and let

\begin{equation}\label{eq:geodesic_gamma}
  \gamma(t) := \text{Exp}_{[\bm{A},s]}(t\bm{v}) = [\bm{A}_t, s+tc],
  \qquad \bm{A}_t := \bm{A}+t\bm{V}, \qquad t \in [0,1],
\end{equation}
be the geodesic of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\rank(\bm{A}_t) = q$ for every $t \in [0,1]$, and for every
  $t$ and every skew-symmetric $\bm{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{q\times q}$ the
  Lyapunov equation

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:lyapunov}
    \bracket{\bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t}\bm{\Omega}
    + \bm{\Omega}\bracket{\bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t} = \bm{K}
  \end{equation}
  has a unique solution $\bm{\Omega} =: \mathcal{L}_t(\bm{K})$;
  it is skew-symmetric, $\mathcal{L}_t$ is linear in $\bm{K}$, and
  $(t,\bm{K}) \mapsto \mathcal{L}_t(\bm{K})$ is smooth.
  \item Let $\bm{x}(\, \cdot \,)$ be a vector field along $\gamma$, that
  is $\bm{x}(t) \in T_{\gamma(t)}M$, and let
  $(\bm{X}(t),\beta(t)) := \iota_{(\bm{A}_t, s+tc)}(\bm{x}(t))
  \in H_{(\bm{A}_t,s+tc)}$
  be its horizontal lift.
  Then $\bm{x}$ is parallel along $\gamma$ if and only if

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_ODE}
    \dot{\bm{X}}(t)
    = \bm{A}_t\,\mathcal{L}_t\bracket{\bm{X}(t)^T\bm{V} - \bm{V}^T\bm{X}(t)},
    \qquad \dot{\beta}(t) = 0 .
  \end{equation}
  \item Equation \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} is a linear ordinary
  differential equation on $\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times\mathbb{R}$ whose
  coefficients are smooth in $(t,\bm{A},\bm{V},c)$;
  its flow preserves the horizontal subspaces, that is
  $(\bm{X}(0),\beta(0)) \in H_{(\bm{A},s)}$ implies
  $(\bm{X}(t),\beta(t)) \in H_{(\bm{A}_t,s+tc)}$ for all $t$, and it
  preserves the Euclidean norm.
  Consequently the parallel transport along $\gamma$ exists, is a linear
  isometry, and depends smoothly on all the data.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} Since $\iota_{(\bm{A},s)}$ is an isometry,
$\|\bm{V}\| \leqslant \|\bm{V}\|_F \leqslant \abs{\bm{v}}
< \min(\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s)$, so Weyl's perturbation inequality, as
quoted in the proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, gives
$\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}_t) \geqslant \sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}) - t\|\bm{V}\| > 0$
for $t \in [0,1]$; hence $\bm{A}_t$ has rank $q$ and
$\bm{P}_t := \bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t$ is positive definite.
The linear operator $\bm{\Omega} \mapsto \bm{P}_t\bm{\Omega} + \bm{\Omega}\bm{P}_t$
on $\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}$ is invertible: diagonalizing
$\bm{P}_t = \bm{O}\text{diag}(p_1,\ldots,p_q)\bm{O}^T$ with $\bm{O} \in O(q)$
and $p_i > 0$ and conjugating by $\bm{O}$ turns it into the entrywise
multiplication by $p_i + p_j > 0$.
This proves existence, uniqueness and linearity, and smoothness in
$(t,\bm{K})$ follows since the inverse of an invertible matrix depends
smoothly on it and $t \mapsto \bm{P}_t$ is polynomial.
For the skew-symmetry, transposing \eqref{eq:lyapunov} gives
$\bm{P}_t(-\bm{\Omega}^T) + (-\bm{\Omega}^T)\bm{P}_t = -\bm{K}^T = \bm{K}$,
so $-\bm{\Omega}^T$ solves the same equation and uniqueness forces
$\bm{\Omega}^T = -\bm{\Omega}$.

\textbf{2.} The curve $c(t) := (\bm{A}_t, s+tc)$ is a straight line in
$\Theta$ with horizontal initial velocity, hence, as recalled in the proof
of Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, a horizontal geodesic of
$\Theta$ projecting to $\gamma$.
Because $\pi$ is a Riemannian submersion, the horizontal lift of the
covariant derivative $D_t\bm{x}$ along $\gamma$ is the horizontal part of
the covariant derivative of the lift along $c$
(\citealp{O_neill_1967}, Section 2; \citealp{Valencia_2021}, Section 3).
The statement there is for basic vector fields, and it extends to fields
along a curve because both sides of the identity are $\mathbb{R}$-linear,
satisfy the Leibniz rule in the scalar factor, and agree on fields that
extend to basic fields, which are the three properties characterizing the
covariant derivative along a curve.
Since the metric of $\Theta$ is the Euclidean one \eqref{eq:euclidean_metric},
its covariant derivative along $c$ is the ordinary derivative
$(\dot{\bm{X}},\dot{\beta})$.
Therefore $\bm{x}$ is parallel if and only if
$(\dot{\bm{X}}(t),\dot{\beta}(t)) \in V_{(\bm{A}_t,s+tc)}$, which by
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} means

\begin{equation}\label{eq:parallel_vertical}
  \dot{\bm{X}}(t) = \bm{A}_t\bm{\Omega}(t) \text{ for some skew-symmetric }
  \bm{\Omega}(t), \qquad \dot{\beta}(t) = 0 .
\end{equation}
It remains to identify $\bm{\Omega}(t)$.
By Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} again, horizontality of the
lift at every $t$ says that
$\bm{S}(t) := \bm{A}_t^T\bm{X}(t) - \bm{X}(t)^T\bm{A}_t$ vanishes
identically, so $\dot{\bm{S}}(t) = \bm{0}$; substituting
\eqref{eq:parallel_vertical} and $\bm{\Omega}^T = -\bm{\Omega}$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:S_dot}
  \bm{0} = \dot{\bm{S}}(t)
  = \bm{V}^T\bm{X} + \bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t\bm{\Omega}
  - \bm{\Omega}^T\bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t - \bm{X}^T\bm{V}
  = \bracket{\bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t\bm{\Omega} + \bm{\Omega}\bm{A}_t^T\bm{A}_t}
  - \bracket{\bm{X}^T\bm{V} - \bm{V}^T\bm{X}},
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{eq:lyapunov} with
$\bm{K} = \bm{X}^T\bm{V}-\bm{V}^T\bm{X}$, a skew-symmetric matrix.
By part 1 this determines $\bm{\Omega}(t)$ uniquely and gives
\eqref{eq:transport_ODE}.
Conversely, \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} is of the form
\eqref{eq:parallel_vertical} with a skew-symmetric $\bm{\Omega}(t)$, so it
implies parallelism provided the lift stays horizontal, which is part 3.

\textbf{3.} The right hand side of \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} is linear in
$\bm{X}(t)$, with coefficients smooth in $t$ and in
$(\bm{A},\bm{V},c)$ by part 1; solutions therefore exist on all of $[0,1]$
and depend smoothly on the initial condition and on the parameters, by the
standard theory of linear systems and smooth dependence
(\citealp{Lee_2013}, Theorem D.1 and Theorem D.6).
For the preservation of horizontality, let $\bm{X}$ solve
\eqref{eq:transport_ODE} and put again
$\bm{S}(t) := \bm{A}_t^T\bm{X}(t) - \bm{X}(t)^T\bm{A}_t$.
The computation \eqref{eq:S_dot}, read in the reverse direction, gives
$\dot{\bm{S}}(t) = \bm{0}$ identically, and $\bm{S}(0) = \bm{0}$ because
$(\bm{X}(0),\beta(0))$ is horizontal; hence $\bm{S} \equiv \bm{0}$.
For the preservation of the norm, write $\bm{\Omega}(t)$ for the
skew-symmetric matrix appearing in \eqref{eq:transport_ODE} and note that
$\bm{X}^T\bm{A}_t = (\bm{A}_t^T\bm{X})^T = \bm{A}_t^T\bm{X}$ is symmetric
by the horizontality just proved, so

\begin{equation*}
  \frac{d}{dt}\squared{\tr\bracket{\bm{X}\bm{X}^T} + \beta^2}
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bm{X}^T\dot{\bm{X}}} + 0
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bm{X}^T\bm{A}_t\bm{\Omega}}
  = 2\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{A}_t^T\bm{X}}\bm{\Omega}} = 0,
\end{equation*}
the last equality because the trace of the product of a symmetric and a
skew-symmetric matrix vanishes.
Since $\iota_{(\bm{A}_t,s+tc)}$ is an isometry by \eqref{eq:lift_def},
this is exactly the statement that
$\abs{\bm{x}(t)}$ is constant.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{def:transport}
For $\bm{u} \in B_{\rho}$ we write

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gamma_def}
  \Gamma_{\bm{u}} :
  T_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}M \longrightarrow T_{\theta_0}M
\end{equation}
for the parallel transport from $t=1$ to $t=0$ along the radial geodesic
$t \mapsto \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{u})$ of the normal chart of
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart}, that is the inverse of the linear isometry
supplied by Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} applied with
$[\bm{A},s] = \theta_0$ and $\bm{v} = \bm{u}$.
This is the transport along the minimizing geodesic joining the two
points, which is the map denoted $\Gamma$ in Definition III.5 of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}.
By Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport}, $\Gamma_{\bm{u}}$ is a
linear isometry, $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$, and, after both tangent
spaces are identified with subspaces of
$\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times\mathbb{R}$ through the horizontal lifts
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$ and
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\, \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})}$, the map
$(\bm{u},\bm{z}) \mapsto \Gamma_{\bm{u}}(\bm{z})$ is smooth on
$B_{\rho}\times\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times\mathbb{R}$.
\end{definition}

\subsection*{The transport comparison}

Definition III.5 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} compares
$\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\hat{\theta}_n)$, transported back to
$T_{\theta_0}M$, with $\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\theta}_n)$.
On a flat space the two would differ by exactly the vector joining the
two base points; on $M$ they differ by that vector plus a curvature
correction, and the whole content of this subsection is that the
correction is quadratically small.
We first record that the logarithm may be taken at a moving base point.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:uniform_chart}
There exists $r \in \bracket{0,\frac{\rho}{2}}$ such that, writing
$\mathcal{V} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_r)$, the following hold.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item For every $x \in \mathcal{V}$ the map $\text{Exp}_x^{-1}$ is
  defined and smooth on $\mathcal{V}$, and
  $\text{Exp}_x^{-1}(x) = \bm{0}$.
  \item The map
  $(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \mapsto
  \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}\bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}$
  is smooth on $B_r \times B_r$, as a map into
  $\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}\times\mathbb{R}$ after the horizontal lift
  identification of Definition~\ref{def:transport}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{E} \subseteq TM$ be the set of pairs $(x,\bm{v})$ with
$\bm{v} \in \mathcal{E}_x$, the ball of
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}.
Its radius at $x = [\bm{A},s]$ is $\min(\sigma_{\min}(\bm{A}),s)$, which
is well defined on $M$ because the singular values of $\bm{A}$ are
unchanged by $\bm{A}\mapsto\bm{A}\bm{Q}$ with $\bm{Q} \in O(q)$, and is a
continuous function of $x$; hence $\mathcal{E}$ is open in $TM$ and
contains the zero section, and
$\text{Exp}:\mathcal{E}\to M$ is smooth by
Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}, being affine in the horizontal
lift.
Consider

\begin{equation*}
  E : \mathcal{E} \longrightarrow M \times M,
  \qquad E(x,\bm{v}) := \bracket{x, \text{Exp}_x(\bm{v})} .
\end{equation*}
Its differential at $(\theta_0,\bm{0})$ is invertible, because in the
splitting $T_{(\theta_0,\bm{0})}TM \cong T_{\theta_0}M\oplus T_{\theta_0}M$
it is $(\bm{a},\bm{b})\mapsto(\bm{a},\bm{a}+\bm{b})$, using
$d(\text{Exp}_{\theta_0})_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$.
The inverse function theorem therefore gives open sets
$\mathcal{N}\ni(\theta_0,\bm{0})$ in $\mathcal{E}$ and
$\mathcal{D}\ni(\theta_0,\theta_0)$ in $M\times M$ such that
$E:\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{D}$ is a diffeomorphism.
This is the standard construction of uniformly normal neighbourhoods
(\citealp{Lee_2018}, Theorem 5.14 and its proof); note that completeness of
$M$, which fails here, is nowhere used, only the openness of the domain
$\mathcal{E}$ of $\text{Exp}$.
Writing $E^{-1}(x,y) = (x, \Xi(x,y))$, the map $\Xi$ is smooth on
$\mathcal{D}$ and satisfies $\text{Exp}_x(\Xi(x,y)) = y$ and
$\Xi(x,x)=\bm{0}$; and $\Xi(x, \, \cdot \,)$ is a smooth inverse of
$\text{Exp}_x$ on a neighbourhood of $x$, so it deserves the name
$\text{Exp}_x^{-1}$.
Since $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is continuous and
$(\theta_0,\theta_0) \in \mathcal{D}$, we may choose
$r \in (0,\rho/2)$ so small that
$\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V} \subseteq \mathcal{D}$ with
$\mathcal{V}=\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(B_r)$, which is part 1.
Part 2 follows by composing $\Xi$ with the smooth map
$(\bm{u},\bm{w})\mapsto(\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}),\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w}))$
and with the horizontal lift, itself smooth because by
Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} the horizontal space at
$(\bm{A},s)$ is the kernel of a matrix depending polynomially on
$\bm{A}$, of constant rank.
\end{proof}

We may and do shrink $r$ further so that
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}^{-1}$ agrees on $\mathcal{V}$ with the map of
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart}, which is automatic since both invert
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ on $B_r \subseteq B_{\rho}$ and the latter is
injective there.
Define

\begin{equation}\label{eq:F_def}
  F : B_r \times B_r \longrightarrow T_{\theta_0}M,
  \qquad
  F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) := \Gamma_{\bm{u}}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})}} .
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Transport comparison]\label{thm:transport_comparison}
After a further shrinking of $r$ there is a constant $C < \infty$,
depending only on $[\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2]$ and $r$, such that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport_comparison}
  \abs{F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) - (\bm{w}-\bm{u})}
  \leqslant C\,\abs{\bm{u}}\,\abs{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_r .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart} and Definition~\ref{def:transport}, $F$
is smooth on $B_r\times B_r$; put

\begin{equation*}
  R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) := F(\bm{u},\bm{w}) - (\bm{w}-\bm{u}),
\end{equation*}
a smooth $T_{\theta_0}M$-valued function on $B_r\times B_r$.
It vanishes on two slices:

\begin{itemize}
  \item $R(\bm{u},\bm{u}) = \bm{0}$ for every $\bm{u} \in B_r$, because
  $\text{Exp}^{-1}_x(x) = \bm{0}$ and $\Gamma_{\bm{u}}$ is linear, so
  $F(\bm{u},\bm{u}) = \bm{0} = \bm{u}-\bm{u}$;
  \item $R(\bm{0},\bm{w}) = \bm{0}$ for every $\bm{w} \in B_r$, because
  $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$ and
  $\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{w})) = \bm{w}$,
  so $F(\bm{0},\bm{w}) = \bm{w} = \bm{w}-\bm{0}$.
\end{itemize}
Replace $r$ by $r/2$, so that the closed balls
$\overline{B_r}\times\overline{B_r}$ are compact subsets of the open set
on which $R$ is smooth, and set

\begin{equation*}
  C := \sup_{(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \in \overline{B_r}\times\overline{B_r}}
  \left\| \partial_1\partial_2 R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) \right\| < \infty,
\end{equation*}
the norm being the one induced on bilinear maps
$T_{\theta_0}M\times T_{\theta_0}M \to T_{\theta_0}M$ by $\abs{\, \cdot \,}$;
the supremum is finite because $\partial_1\partial_2R$ is continuous on a
compact set.
Let $\bm{u},\bm{w} \in B_r$.
The ball $B_r$ being convex, the segment from $\bm{u}$ to $\bm{w}$ lies
in it, and the fundamental theorem of calculus in the second variable,
together with $R(\bm{u},\bm{u})=\bm{0}$, gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:R_first_taylor}
  R(\bm{u},\bm{w}) = \int_0^1
  \partial_2R\bracket{\bm{u}, \bm{u}+\tau(\bm{w}-\bm{u})}
  \squared{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau .
\end{equation}
Fix $\bm{z} \in B_r$.
The segment from $\bm{0}$ to $\bm{u}$ also lies in $B_r$, and
$\partial_2R(\bm{0},\bm{z}) = \bm{0}$ because $R(\bm{0},\, \cdot \,)$
vanishes identically on $B_r$; so the same argument in the first variable
gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:R_second_taylor}
  \partial_2R(\bm{u},\bm{z})
  = \int_0^1 \partial_1\partial_2R(\varsigma\bm{u},\bm{z})[\bm{u}]\,\mathrm{d}\varsigma,
  \qquad \text{whence} \qquad
  \left\|\partial_2R(\bm{u},\bm{z})\right\| \leqslant C\abs{\bm{u}} .
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:R_second_taylor} into \eqref{eq:R_first_taylor}
yields \eqref{eq:transport_comparison}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:transport_scale}
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} is the only place where the
curvature of $(M,g)$ enters the asymptotic theory, and
\eqref{eq:transport_comparison} says exactly how much it costs.
In the applications below $\bm{u}$ is a local perturbation of size
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$ and $\bm{w}-\bm{u}$ is an estimation error of size
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$, so the right hand side is
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})$; after the multiplication by $\sqrt{n}$ that all
these statements carry, it is $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ and
disappears.
The two vanishing slices used in the proof are the two trivial cases of
the comparison --- no displacement of the base point, and no error to
compare --- and it is their combination, not either alone, that produces
the product $\abs{\bm{u}}\abs{\bm{w}-\bm{u}}$.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Local regularity}

We can now state the definition.
For $\bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M$ and $n$ large enough that
$\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}} \in B_r$ we write

\begin{equation}\label{eq:local_alternative}
  \theta_{n,\bm{h}} := \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}} \in \mathcal{V},
\end{equation}
the \textit{geodesic local alternative} in the direction $\bm{h}$, and
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta}$ for the law of $(\bm{X}_1,\ldots,\bm{X}_n)$ when
the true parameter is $\theta$, that is the $n$-fold product of
$p(\, \cdot \,;\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\bm{x}$.
Given an $M$-valued estimator sequence $T_n$ we put, in the notation of
Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:errors_def}
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] := \begin{cases}
    \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_0}(T_n) & \text{on } \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}},\\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
  \qquad
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n] := \begin{cases}
    \Gamma_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
    \bracket{\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(T_n)} & \text{on } \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}},\\
    \bm{0} & \text{otherwise},
  \end{cases}
\end{equation}
both taking values in the single vector space $T_{\theta_0}M$; the second
is defined as soon as $\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}} \in B_r$, by part 1 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}.
For the maximum likelihood estimator, $\hat{\bm{u}}_n[\hat{\theta}_n]$
coincides with the vector $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ of \eqref{eq:e_n_def} on the
event $\curly{\hat{\theta}_n \in \mathcal{V}}$, which by the last part of
the proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, applied with
$\epsilon = r$, has probability tending to one; we therefore keep the
notation $\hat{\bm{u}}_n$ for it, the two conventions differing only on
events of vanishing probability, which by the portmanteau argument of
Step 7 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} changes nothing below.

\begin{definition}\label{def:regular}
An $M$-valued estimator sequence $T_n$ is \textit{locally regular at
$\theta_0$ with limit law $L$}, where $L$ is a Borel probability measure
on $T_{\theta_0}M$, if for every $\bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M$

\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n] \rightsquigarrow L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} ,
\end{equation}
the limit $L$ being the same for every $\bm{h}$.
This is Definition III.5 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} with
$\psi = \text{id}$ and $\Psi = M$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Contiguity]\label{thm:contiguity}
For every $\bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M$ the sequences
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ and $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ are
mutually contiguous.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:LAN_weak_convergence} of Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} the
log-likelihood ratio $\Lambda_n(\bm{h})$ of
\eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} converges in distribution under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ to $N(-\frac{1}{2}\varsigma^2,\varsigma^2)$ with
$\varsigma^2 := G(\bm{h},\bm{h})$.
If $Z \sim N(-\frac{1}{2}\varsigma^2,\varsigma^2)$ then
$\mathbb{E}[e^{Z}] = e^{-\frac{1}{2}\varsigma^2+\frac{1}{2}\varsigma^2} = 1$,
so Le Cam's first lemma in the form of Example 6.5 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998} gives mutual contiguity.
\end{proof}

The next lemma is the bridge: it says that on this manifold, regularity
in the intrinsic sense of Definition~\ref{def:regular} is the same thing
as regularity of the ordinary Euclidean kind read in the normal chart of
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart}.
It is what allows the classical theory to be applied verbatim below.

\begin{lemma}[Chart form of regularity]\label{thm:chart_regularity}
Let $T_n$ be an $M$-valued estimator sequence such that
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}(T_n \in \mathcal{V}) \to 1$ and
$\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$.
Then, for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:chart_form}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  = \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \bm{h} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} .
\end{equation}
Consequently $T_n$ is locally regular at $\theta_0$ with limit law $L$ if
and only if

\begin{equation}\label{eq:chart_regularity}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \bm{h} \rightsquigarrow L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
  \text{ for every } \bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M .
\end{equation}
Moreover the two standing hypotheses are automatically satisfied by any
locally regular sequence.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and abbreviate
$\hat{\bm{u}}_n := \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$,
$\bm{u}_n := \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}$ and
$\mathcal{B}_n := \curly{T_n \in \mathcal{V}}$.
By hypothesis $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}(\mathcal{B}_n)\to 1$, hence
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{B}_n)\to 1$ by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}; and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well.
The latter is the only property of contiguity used beyond the transfer of
$o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ statements, and it follows from the definition:
were it false, there would be $\varepsilon>0$, a subsequence $n_k$ and
constants $M_k \to \infty$ with
$\mathbb{P}^{n_k}_{\theta_{n_k,\bm{h}}}
\bracket{\sqrt{n_k}\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_{n_k}}>M_k} > \varepsilon$
for all $k$, whereas
$\mathbb{P}^{n_k}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\sqrt{n_k}\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_{n_k}}>M_k}\to 0$
by boundedness in probability under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, and these
two are incompatible with contiguity.
On $\mathcal{B}_n$, and for $n$ large enough that
$\bm{u}_n \in B_r$, we have $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \in B_r$ and, by
\eqref{eq:F_def} and \eqref{eq:errors_def},

\begin{equation*}
  \hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  = \Gamma_{\bm{u}_n}\bracket{
  \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u}_n)}
  \bracket{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)}}
  = F(\bm{u}_n, \hat{\bm{u}}_n),
\end{equation*}
because $T_n = \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ there.
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} therefore gives, on $\mathcal{B}_n$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparison_applied}
  \abs{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[T_n]
  - \bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}}
  = \sqrt{n}\,\abs{F(\bm{u}_n,\hat{\bm{u}}_n) - (\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{u}_n)}
  \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,C\,\frac{\abs{\bm{h}}}{\sqrt{n}}\,
  \abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  = \frac{C\abs{\bm{h}}}{\sqrt{n}}\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}} .
\end{equation}
The right hand side is $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}) = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, and
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{B}_n^c)\to 0$, which proves
\eqref{eq:chart_form}.
The equivalence of \eqref{eq:regularity} and
\eqref{eq:chart_regularity} is then Slutsky's lemma.

For the last assertion, suppose $T_n$ is locally regular with limit law
$L$ and take $\bm{h}=\bm{0}$ in \eqref{eq:regularity}: since
$\theta_{n,\bm{0}} = \theta_0$ and $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$, this
reads $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]\rightsquigarrow L$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, which is boundedness in probability by
Prohorov's theorem; and \eqref{eq:errors_def} is only meaningful under
the convention that $T_n\in\mathcal{V}$ with probability tending to one,
which we have built into the definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Local regularity of the maximum likelihood estimator]\label{thm:MLE_regular}
Let $1\leqslant q<p$ and let $\theta_0 = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in M$.
Then the maximum likelihood estimator $\hat{\theta}_n$ of
Definition~\ref{def:MLE} is locally regular at $\theta_0$ in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:regular}, with limit law

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_regular_limit}
  L = N\bracket{\bm{0}, G^{-1}_{\theta_0}} \qquad \text{on } T_{\theta_0}M,
\end{equation}
which does not depend on $\bm{h}$.
Explicitly, for every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:MLE_regular_statement}
  \Gamma_{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
  \bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}\bracket{\hat{\theta}_n}}
  \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} .
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We work in the coordinates of \eqref{eq:theta_notation}.
Fix $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$.

\textbf{Step 1: a single linear statistic drives everything.}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, in the form \eqref{eq:vdV_conclusion},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:AL_recalled}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_n + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation}
and by \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates},
$\Lambda_n(\bm{h}) = \bm{\Delta}_n^T\bm{h} - \frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}
+ o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$.
Both expansions are linear in the \textit{same} vector $\bm{\Delta}_n$ of
\eqref{eq:Delta_n_def}, so no separate joint central limit theorem is
required: the vectors $\bm{s}(\bm{X}_1),\bm{s}(\bm{X}_2),\ldots$ are
independent, identically distributed, centred and square integrable with
covariance matrix $\bm{G}$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, so the multivariate
central limit theorem gives
$\bm{\Delta}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G})$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$, and the continuous mapping theorem applied to
the linear map
$\bm{\delta}\mapsto(\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\delta},\ \bm{\delta}^T\bm{h}
-\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h})$
together with Slutsky's lemma yields

\begin{equation}\label{eq:joint_convergence}
  \bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n,\ \Lambda_n(\bm{h})}
  \rightsquigarrow
  N\bracket{
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{0}\\ -\frac{1}{2}\bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix},
  \begin{pmatrix} \bm{G}^{-1} & \bm{h} \\
  \bm{h}^T & \bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h}\end{pmatrix}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0},
\end{equation}
the off-diagonal block being
$\text{Cov}(\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{\Delta},\bm{\Delta}^T\bm{h})
= \bm{G}^{-1}\bm{G}\bm{h} = \bm{h}$ for
$\bm{\Delta}\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{G})$.

\textbf{Step 2: Le Cam's third lemma.}
The limit in \eqref{eq:joint_convergence} has the form required by
Example 6.7 of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, the Gaussian case of Le Cam's
third lemma (Theorem 6.6 there): the last coordinate is the
log-likelihood ratio and is $N(-\frac{1}{2}\varsigma^2,\varsigma^2)$ with
$\varsigma^2 = \bm{h}^T\bm{G}\bm{h} = G(\bm{h},\bm{h})$.
The cited result therefore gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{h},\bm{G}^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} :
\end{equation}
the limit under the contiguous alternative is the limit under the null
shifted by the covariance $\bm{h}$ between the statistic and the
log-likelihood ratio.
Equivalently
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h} \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, a law free of $\bm{h}$.

\textbf{Step 3: from the chart to the transported error.}
The hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} hold for
$T_n = \hat{\theta}_n$: the event $\curly{\hat{\theta}_n\in\mathcal{V}}$
has probability tending to one, as recorded after
\eqref{eq:errors_def}, and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ by \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}.
The equivalence \eqref{eq:chart_regularity} of that lemma turns
\eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion} into \eqref{eq:MLE_regular_statement},
with limit law $N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$, which in the
$g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ represents
$N(\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0})$ as in Step 6 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:hodges}
It is worth being explicit about what \eqref{eq:MLE_regular_statement}
excludes, since Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} alone excludes nothing of the
kind.
Fix a point $\theta_* \in M$, known to the statistician, and let
$\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$ be the Hodges-type modification of
$\hat{\theta}_n$ that replaces it by $\theta_*$ whenever
$\hat{\theta}_n \in \text{Exp}_{\theta_*}(B)$ with $B$ the ball of radius
$n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$ in $T_{\theta_*}M$, and leaves it unchanged otherwise.
For any fixed true parameter $\theta_0 \neq \theta_*$ the modification
occurs on an event of probability tending to zero, by
Proposition~\ref{thm:consistency}, so $\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$
satisfies the exact analogue of \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} there.
For $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ it does better: since
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}) \ll n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$
by \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}, we have $\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}} = \theta_*$
with probability tending to one, so that
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}]
\rightsquigarrow \delta_{\bm{0}}$, a limit strictly more concentrated
than $N(\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0})$; this is the superefficiency
phenomenon, and it shows that no bound whatsoever can be extracted from
pointwise statements of the type of \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} alone.
And yet, at $\theta_0 = \theta_*$ and $\bm{h}\neq\bm{0}$, the same
computation gives $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n[\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}]
\rightsquigarrow \delta_{\bm{0}}$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ as well --- the estimator is pinned to
$\theta_*$ on an event of probability tending to one, since
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})
\ll n^{-\frac{1}{4}}$ under the alternative too, by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} --- so by \eqref{eq:chart_form} its
transported error converges to $\delta_{-\bm{h}}$, which does depend on
$\bm{h}$.
Hence $\hat{\theta}_n^{\text{H}}$ is superefficient at $\theta_*$ and is
not locally regular there.
Local regularity is precisely the statement that the asymptotics of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} do not degrade as the truth is moved at the
scale $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$, and it is a strictly stronger property.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Optimality}

Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} concerns one estimator.
We now turn to the statements that concern \textit{all} estimators, and
which are therefore lower bounds rather than limit theorems.
The key point is Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity}: because on this
manifold intrinsic regularity is equivalent to ordinary regularity read
in the normal chart of Lemma~\ref{thm:chart}, and because by
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} the local experiments in that chart are
asymptotically normal in the sense of Chapter 7 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, with nonsingular information matrix $\bm{G}$
by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, the classical bounds apply verbatim.
No curvature enters: it has already been absorbed by
Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison}.

Throughout this subsection the \textit{local experiments} are

\begin{equation}\label{eq:local_experiments}
  \mathcal{E}_n := \bracket{\mathbb{P}^n_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}\bracket{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}}
  \ : \ \bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M \cong \mathbb{R}^d},
\end{equation}
and \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} says exactly that $\mathcal{E}_n$ is
asymptotically normal at $\bm{h}=\bm{0}$ with score $\bm{\Delta}_n$ and
information $\bm{G}$.

\begin{theorem}[Convolution bound]\label{thm:convolution}
Let $T_n$ be any estimator sequence that is locally regular at $\theta_0$
in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:regular}, with limit law $L$.
Then there exists a Borel probability measure $\Lambda$ on
$T_{\theta_0}M$ such that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:convolution}
  L = N\bracket{\bm{0}, G^{-1}_{\theta_0}} * \Lambda,
\end{equation}
the convolution of the Gaussian law with covariance operator the inverse
Fisher information and an independent factor.
In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item if $L$ has a finite second moment then so does $\Lambda$ and the
  covariance operators satisfy
  $\text{cov}(L) = G^{-1}_{\theta_0} + \text{cov}(\Lambda)
  \succeq G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$;
  \item the maximum likelihood estimator attains
  $\Lambda = \delta_{\bm{0}}$, by Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}, and is
  therefore \textit{best regular}: no locally regular estimator sequence
  for $[\bm{W},\sigma^2]$ has a limit law less dispersed than that of
  $\hat{\theta}_n$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the last assertion of Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} the sequence
$T_n$ satisfies the two standing hypotheses of that lemma, and by
\eqref{eq:chart_regularity}

\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \bm{h} \rightsquigarrow L
  \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
  \text{ for every } \bm{h} \in \mathbb{R}^d .
\end{equation*}
In the chart, $\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$ is an estimator of the local
parameter $\bm{u}$, the true value is $\bm{u}=\bm{0}$, the local
alternative $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ is the parameter value
$\bm{u} = \bm{h}/\sqrt{n}$, and the display reads
$\sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n] - \frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}}}
\rightsquigarrow L$ under $\bm{h}$;
this is precisely the definition of a regular estimator sequence of
Section 8.5 of \cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, with $\psi$ the identity map.
The experiments $\mathcal{E}_n$ being asymptotically normal with
nonsingular $\bm{G}$, the convolution theorem, Theorem 8.8 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, gives a probability measure $\Lambda$ on
$\mathbb{R}^d$ with $L = N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}) * \Lambda$, which is
\eqref{eq:convolution} once $\mathbb{R}^d$ is identified with
$T_{\theta_0}M$ through the $g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal basis
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$, in which $\bm{G}^{-1}$ represents
$G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$.

Part 1 is the elementary fact that if $Z$ and $Y$ are independent with
$Z\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$ and $Z+Y\sim L$ square integrable then $Y$
is square integrable, its law being the image of $L$ under a measurable
map determined by the deconvolution, and covariances add on independent
summands; concretely,
$\text{cov}(L) = \text{cov}(Z)+\text{cov}(Y)
= \bm{G}^{-1}+\text{cov}(\Lambda)$.
Part 2 is Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}, which gives
$L = N(\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0})$, whence $\Lambda = \delta_{\bm{0}}$ by
uniqueness of the deconvolution of a Gaussian, or directly by comparing
characteristic functions:
$\hat{L}(\xi) = \hat{N}(\xi)\hat{\Lambda}(\xi)$ with
$\hat{N}$ nowhere vanishing forces $\hat{\Lambda}\equiv 1$.
\end{proof}

The convolution theorem restricts attention to regular estimators.
The local asymptotic minimax theorem removes that restriction, at the
price of replacing the limit law by a maximal risk over a shrinking
neighbourhood.
Recall that a function $\ell : T_{\theta_0}M\to[0,\infty)$ is called
\textit{bowl-shaped} if its sublevel sets
$\curly{\ell \leqslant c}$ are convex and symmetric about the origin for
every $c \geqslant 0$; the squared norm $\ell(\bm{z}) = \abs{\bm{z}}^2$
and its truncations $\abs{\bm{z}}^2\wedge m$ are the examples to keep in
mind.

\begin{theorem}[Local asymptotic minimax bound and its attainment]\label{thm:LAM}
Let $\ell$ be bowl-shaped.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textnormal{(Lower bound.)}
  For \textit{every} sequence $\hat{\bm{v}}_n$ of measurable maps
  $(\mathbb{R}^p)^n \to T_{\theta_0}M$ --- in particular for
  $\hat{\bm{v}}_n = \hat{\bm{u}}_n[T_n]$ arising from an arbitrary
  $M$-valued estimator sequence $T_n$, regular or not ---

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:LAM}
    \sup_{I} \ \liminf_{n\to\infty} \ \sup_{\bm{h}\in I}\
    \mathbb{E}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
    \squared{\ell\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{v}}_n - \bm{h}}}
    \ \geqslant \ \int \ell \, \mathrm{d}N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}},
  \end{equation}
  the first supremum being over all finite subsets
  $I \subset T_{\theta_0}M$.
  \item \textnormal{(Attainment.)}
  If $\ell$ is in addition bounded and continuous, then the maximum
  likelihood estimator turns \eqref{eq:LAM} into an equality:

  \begin{equation}\label{eq:LAM_attained}
    \sup_{I}\ \liminf_{n\to\infty}\ \sup_{\bm{h}\in I}\
    \mathbb{E}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
    \squared{\ell\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}}
    = \int \ell \, \mathrm{d}N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}},
  \end{equation}
  and the same holds with $\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}$ replaced by
  the transported intrinsic error
  $\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[\hat{\theta}_n]$ of
  \eqref{eq:errors_def}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.} As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:convolution}, in the
normal chart the family $\mathcal{E}_n$ is a sequence of asymptotically
normal experiments indexed by $\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^d$ with nonsingular
information $\bm{G}$, and $\hat{\bm{v}}_n$ is an arbitrary estimator
sequence for the local parameter.
Inequality \eqref{eq:LAM} is then Theorem 8.11 of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, the local asymptotic minimax theorem, whose
only hypotheses are the asymptotic normality of the experiments and the
bowl-shapedness of $\ell$; no property of the estimator sequence is
required, which is why the bound is estimator-agnostic and closes the
superefficiency loophole illustrated in Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges}.

\textbf{2.} Fix a finite $I \subset T_{\theta_0}M$.
By Step 2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular},
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}\rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$ for each $\bm{h}$.
Since $\ell$ is bounded and continuous, the portmanteau theorem gives
$\mathbb{E}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}\squared{\ell(\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n-\bm{h})}
\to \int\ell\,\mathrm{d}N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
for each $\bm{h}\in I$, a limit not depending on $\bm{h}$.
As $I$ is finite, the maximum over $\bm{h}\in I$ of finitely many
sequences with a common limit converges to that limit, so

\begin{equation*}
  \liminf_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\bm{h}\in I}
  \mathbb{E}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}\squared{\ell\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n-\bm{h}}}
  = \int \ell\,\mathrm{d}N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})
\end{equation*}
for every finite $I$, and taking the supremum over $I$ leaves the value
unchanged.
This is \eqref{eq:LAM_attained}, and it matches the lower bound of part 1
exactly.
For the last claim, \eqref{eq:chart_form} gives
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n^{\bm{h}}[\hat{\theta}_n]
= \sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, so the two have the same limit in
distribution and, $\ell$ being bounded and continuous, the same limiting
risk.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:LAM_unbounded}
Part 2 is stated for bounded $\ell$ because weak convergence alone
controls the risk only for bounded losses; for
$\ell(\bm{z}) = \abs{\bm{z}}^2$ the identity \eqref{eq:LAM_attained}
requires in addition the uniform integrability of
$\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}^2$ under
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, which we have not established and
which does not follow from anything proved above.
The lower bound of part 1 is unaffected: it holds for every bowl-shaped
$\ell$, bounded or not.
Applying part 2 to the truncations $\ell\wedge m$, which are again
bounded, continuous and bowl-shaped, and letting $m\to\infty$ recovers
the bound but not the equality, monotone convergence acting on the wrong
side of the inequality.
\end{remark}

\subsection*{Consequences}

The first consequence closes the small gap left in
Corollary~\ref{thm:influence_relation}, whose uniqueness clause was
proved by invoking the influence relation, which
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} establish in their Lemma III.3 for sequences that
are \textit{both} asymptotically linear \textit{and} regular.
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} supplies the missing half of that
hypothesis.
It is worth recording that the uniqueness in fact needs neither.

\begin{proposition}\label{thm:IF_unique}
Let $T_n$ be an estimator sequence that is asymptotically linear at
$\theta_0$ in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:asymptotic_linearity} with
influence operator $\text{IF}$, and suppose $\text{IF}'$ is another
influence operator for which \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} holds.
Then $\text{IF}'(\bm{X}_1) = \text{IF}(\bm{X}_1)$ almost surely.
In particular the influence operator \eqref{eq:IF_def} of the maximum
likelihood estimator is the unique one, with no restriction to the span
of the score, and $\hat{\theta}_n$ is a regular asymptotically linear
sequence, so that the influence relation
