\eqref{eq:influence_relation} is now available with all the hypotheses of
\cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026}, Lemma III.3, verified.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Subtracting the two expansions \eqref{eq:asymptotic_linearity} gives
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n \bm{D}(\bm{X}_i) = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ with
$\bm{D} := \text{IF}-\text{IF}'$, which is centred and square integrable
by \eqref{eq:IF_moments}.
The multivariate central limit theorem gives
$n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_i\bm{D}(\bm{X}_i)\rightsquigarrow
N(\bm{0},\text{cov}(\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1)))$, and a sequence converging in
distribution to a limit and also to $\bm{0}$ in probability forces that
limit to be $\delta_{\bm{0}}$, so
$\text{cov}(\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1)) = \bm{0}$; being centred,
$\bm{D}(\bm{X}_1) = \bm{0}$ almost surely.
The remaining assertions are Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular}.
\end{proof}

The second consequence is the inferential payoff, and it is the one that
distinguishes local regularity from Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} in
practice.
We first record that the information operator may be evaluated at the
estimator.

\begin{lemma}\label{thm:information_smooth}
For $\bm{u}\in B_r$ let $\bm{G}(\bm{u})$ be the $d\times d$ matrix

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_transported}
  \bm{G}(\bm{u})_{jk} := G_{\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})}
  \bracket{\Gamma_{\bm{u}}^{-1}(\bm{e}_j), \Gamma_{\bm{u}}^{-1}(\bm{e}_k)},
  \qquad j,k = 1,\ldots,d,
\end{equation}
the matrix of the Fisher information operator at
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$ in the frame obtained by transporting
$\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ along the radial geodesic.
Then $\bm{u}\mapsto\bm{G}(\bm{u})$ is smooth on $B_r$, with
$\bm{G}(\bm{0}) = \bm{G}$, and it is positive definite everywhere.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})
:= (\bm{W}_0+\bm{U}_{\bm{u}},\ \sigma_0^2+b_{\bm{u}})$ for the
representative of $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$ used in
Definition~\ref{def:transport}, which is affine in $\bm{u}$, and let
$(\tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u}), \tilde{b}_j(\bm{u}))
:= \iota_{(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})}
\bracket{\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}}(\bm{e}_j)}$
be the horizontal lift there.
By \eqref{eq:G_def} and \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional} applied at the point
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$,

\begin{equation*}
  \bm{G}(\bm{u})_{jk} = \frac{1}{2}\tr\curly{
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_j(\bm{u})
  \bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}\bm{\Delta}_k(\bm{u})},
  \qquad
  \bm{\Delta}_j(\bm{u}) := \tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u})\bm{W}_{\bm{u}}^T
  + \bm{W}_{\bm{u}}\tilde{\bm{U}}_j(\bm{u})^T + \tilde{b}_j(\bm{u})\bm{I}_p .
\end{equation*}
Each ingredient is smooth in $\bm{u}$: the matrix
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}$ is a polynomial by
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} and is bounded below by $c_1\bm{I}_p$ by
\eqref{eq:spectral_bounds}, so $\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}^{-1}$ is smooth;
$\Gamma^{-1}_{\bm{u}}$ is smooth by
Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} and
Definition~\ref{def:transport}; and
$\iota_{(\bm{W}_{\bm{u}},\sigma^2_{\bm{u}})}$ is smooth in $\bm{u}$, as
noted at the end of the proof of Lemma~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}.
That $\bm{G}(\bm{0}) = \bm{G}$ is $\Gamma_{\bm{0}} = \text{id}$, and
positive definiteness is Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} at the point
$\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\bm{u})$, the change of frame being invertible.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Locally uniform Wald confidence sets]\label{thm:confidence}
Let $\alpha\in(0,1)$, let $\chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}$ be the $(1-\alpha)$
quantile of the chi-squared law with $d$ degrees of freedom, and define
the random subset of $M$

\begin{equation}\label{eq:confidence_set}
  \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha} := \text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}
  \bracket{\curly{\bm{v} \in T_{\hat{\theta}_n}M \ : \
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{v},\bm{v}) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}}},
\end{equation}
which is well defined on an event of probability tending to one.
Then, for every $\bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M$,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:coverage}
  \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
  \bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}} \in \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}}
  \longrightarrow 1-\alpha .
\end{equation}
In particular the coverage is asymptotically correct not only at
$\theta_0$, which is the case $\bm{h}=\bm{0}$ and already follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, but along every sequence of local alternatives
at the estimation scale $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and work under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, which by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} is contiguous to
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$; all the $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ statements of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} and
Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} therefore remain valid.
In particular $\hat{\bm{u}}_n \to \bm{0}$ in probability and
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.

By Lemma~\ref{thm:information_smooth} and the continuous mapping theorem,
$\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) \to \bm{G}$ in probability; in particular
$\lambda_{\min}(\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n))$ is bounded away from zero in
probability, so the ellipsoid in \eqref{eq:confidence_set} is contained in
a ball of radius $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ and hence, with
probability tending to one, in the domain
$\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ of $\text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ supplied
by Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}; this is the well definedness.

Since $\text{Exp}_{\hat{\theta}_n}$ is injective on that ball for large
$n$, membership $\theta_{n,\bm{h}} \in \mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}$ is
equivalent, on an event of probability tending to one, to

\begin{equation}\label{eq:coverage_equivalent}
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{v}_n,\bm{v}_n) \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha},
  \qquad
  \bm{v}_n := \text{Exp}^{-1}_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\theta_{n,\bm{h}}) .
\end{equation}
Put $\bm{z}_n := \Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}(\bm{v}_n) \in T_{\theta_0}M$.
Because $\Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^{-1}\bm{e}_1,\ldots,
\Gamma_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^{-1}\bm{e}_d$ is a basis of
$T_{\hat{\theta}_n}M$ whose coordinates for $\bm{v}_n$ are exactly the
coordinates of $\bm{z}_n$ in $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$, the definition
\eqref{eq:G_transported} gives

\begin{equation}\label{eq:quadratic_form_identity}
  G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{v}_n,\bm{v}_n)
  = \bm{z}_n^T\,\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)\,\bm{z}_n .
\end{equation}
Now apply Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} with
$\bm{u} = \hat{\bm{u}}_n$ and $\bm{w} = \bm{h}/\sqrt{n}$, both in $B_r$
with probability tending to one, noting that
$F(\hat{\bm{u}}_n, \bm{h}/\sqrt{n}) = \bm{z}_n$ by \eqref{eq:F_def}:

\begin{equation*}
  \abs{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n - \bracket{\bm{h} - \sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}}
  \leqslant \sqrt{n}\,C\,\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\,
  \abs{\frac{\bm{h}}{\sqrt{n}} - \hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  = \frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\,\abs{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\,
  \abs{\bm{h}-\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n}
  = O_{\mathbb{P}}\bracket{n^{-\frac{1}{2}}},
\end{equation*}
so that
$\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n = -\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}
+ o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
By Step 2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular},
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}\rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, and the Gaussian law is
symmetric, so
$\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$.
Combining this with $\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)\to\bm{G}$ in probability,
Slutsky's lemma and the continuous mapping theorem applied to
$(\bm{z},\bm{A})\mapsto\bm{z}^T\bm{A}\bm{z}$ give, through
\eqref{eq:quadratic_form_identity},

\begin{equation*}
  n\,G_{\hat{\theta}_n}(\bm{v}_n,\bm{v}_n)
  = \bracket{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n}^T\bm{G}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)\bracket{\sqrt{n}\bm{z}_n}
  \rightsquigarrow \bm{Z}^T\bm{G}\bm{Z},
  \qquad \bm{Z}\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1}),
\end{equation*}
and $\bm{Z}^T\bm{G}\bm{Z} = \|\bm{G}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z}\|^2 \sim \chi^2_d$
because $\bm{G}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{Z}\sim N(\bm{0},\bm{I}_d)$.
The distribution function of $\chi^2_d$ being continuous, the probability
of \eqref{eq:coverage_equivalent} converges to
$\mathbb{P}(\chi^2_d \leqslant \chi^2_{d,1-\alpha}) = 1-\alpha$, which is
\eqref{eq:coverage}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:coverage_uniform}
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence} is stated along each fixed sequence of
local alternatives, which is the exact strength of
Definition~\ref{def:regular} and is already enough to rule out the
failure exhibited in Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges}: for the Hodges-type
estimator the analogous set is a single point with probability tending to
one, and its coverage of $\theta_{n,\bm{h}}$ tends to $0$ for every
$\bm{h}\neq\bm{0}$.
The genuinely uniform statement,
$\sup_{\abs{\bm{h}}\leqslant c}\abs{\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}
(\theta_{n,\bm{h}}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,1-\alpha}) - (1-\alpha)}\to 0$,
requires the expansion \eqref{eq:LAN_coordinates} along arbitrary bounded
sequences $\bm{h}_n$ rather than along fixed $\bm{h}$, which
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN} as stated does not provide; we do not need it
anywhere.
\end{remark}

The last consequence records what all of this says about the object the
model is really about, namely the fitted covariance matrix.
It is the analogue of Corollary 5.5 of the companion draft.
Recall from \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:D_0_def}
  \mathcal{D}_0 : T_{\theta_0}M \longrightarrow \text{Sym}(p),
  \qquad
  \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) := D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)
  = \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T
  + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p
\end{equation}
is linear; it is the differential $d\bm{\Sigma}_{\theta_0}$ of the map
$\bm{\Sigma}$ of \eqref{eq:Sigma_Phi_def}, read through the exponential
chart.
We write $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n := \bm{\Sigma}(\hat{\theta}_n)
= \hat{\bm{W}}_n\hat{\bm{W}}_n^T + \hat{\sigma}_n^2\bm{I}_p$ for the
fitted covariance matrix, which unlike $\hat{\theta}_n$ itself is an
honest element of the fixed vector space $\text{Sym}(p)$.

\begin{corollary}[Covariance level central limit theorem]\label{thm:Sigma_CLT}
Let $1\leqslant q<p$ and $\theta_0 \in M$.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ the fitted covariance matrix is
  asymptotically linear in $\text{Sym}(p)$ with influence function
  $\mathcal{D}_0\circ\text{IF}$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_linear}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
    = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^n
    \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\text{IF}(\bm{X}_i)} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),
  \end{equation}
  and consequently
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_CLT}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
    \rightsquigarrow \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z}),
    \qquad \bm{Z} \sim N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}}
    \text{ on } T_{\theta_0}M .
  \end{equation}
  The limit is a centred Gaussian law on $\text{Sym}(p)$ carried by the
  $d$-dimensional subspace $\mathcal{D}_0(T_{\theta_0}M)$, the tangent
  space at $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ to the immersed submanifold
  $\bm{\Sigma}(M)$ of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle}; it is
  degenerate in $\text{Sym}(p)$ exactly when $p - q \geqslant 2$, the
  codimension being
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_codim}
    \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - d = \frac{(p-q+2)(p-q-1)}{2} .
  \end{equation}
  \item The convergence \eqref{eq:Sigma_CLT} is locally regular: for
  every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$,
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_regular}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n
    - \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}}
    \rightsquigarrow \mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z})
    \qquad \text{under } \mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}},
  \end{equation}
  with the same limit law, free of $\bm{h}$.
\end{enumerate}
Explicitly, $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n$ retains the $q$ leading eigenpairs of
$\bm{S}_n$ and replaces the trailing $p-q$ eigenvalues by their average,
by \eqref{eq:fitted_covariance}, and the blocks of $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z})$
in the singular basis of $\bm{W}_0$ are computed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates} and
Remark~\ref{rmk:IF_vs_S_n}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{1.}
On $\mathcal{A}_n$ we have $\hat{\theta}_n
= \text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$, so
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} applied at $\bm{u} = \hat{\bm{u}}_n$ is the
\textit{exact} identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_hat_exact}
  \hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \mathcal{D}_0(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)
  + \bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n ,
\end{equation}
with no remainder, because $\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}$ is affine in the
horizontal lift by Proposition~\ref{thm:exponential_map}.
Since $\iota$ is a linear isometry,
$\|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T\|_F
\leqslant \|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\|_F^2
\leqslant \abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^2$, and
$\abs{\hat{\bm{u}}_n} = O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ by
\eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}; hence
$\sqrt{n}\|\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T\|_F
= O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}}) = o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
Multiplying \eqref{eq:Sigma_hat_exact} by $\sqrt{n}$ therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_delta}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}_0}
  = \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\sqrt{n}\,\hat{\bm{u}}_n} + o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n ,
\end{equation}
and the restriction to $\mathcal{A}_n$ is harmless: the two sides of
\eqref{eq:Sigma_delta} differ only on $\mathcal{A}_n^c$, whose
probability tends to zero by Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined}, so
their difference is $o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and they have the same weak
limits, exactly as in Step 7 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}.
Now \eqref{eq:Sigma_linear} follows from \eqref{eq:MLE_linear} and the
linearity of $\mathcal{D}_0$, and \eqref{eq:Sigma_CLT} from
\eqref{eq:MLE_CLT}, Slutsky's lemma and the continuous mapping theorem
applied to the linear, hence continuous, map $\mathcal{D}_0$.

The map $\mathcal{D}_0$ is injective: if $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = \bm{0}$
then $G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h},\bm{h}) = 0$ by \eqref{eq:G_def}, whence
$\bm{h} = \bm{0}$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}.
Its image is therefore a $d$-dimensional subspace of $\text{Sym}(p)$, and
it is the image of $d\bm{\Sigma}_{\theta_0}$ because
$\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h}) = \frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}
\bm{\Sigma}(\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{h}))$ by
\eqref{eq:Sigma_directional} and $t\mapsto\text{Exp}_{\theta_0}(t\bm{h})$
has velocity $\bm{h}$ at $t=0$.
Finally, by \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_def},
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - d
  = \frac{p(p+1)}{2} - pq - 1 + \frac{q(q-1)}{2}
  = \frac{(p-q)^2 + (p-q) - 2}{2}
  = \frac{(p-q+2)(p-q-1)}{2},
\end{equation*}
which vanishes if and only if $p-q = 1$, since $p-q+2 \geqslant 3$.

\textbf{2.}
Fix $\bm{h}$ and work under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$, which is
contiguous to $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_0}$ by
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}; in particular
$\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\mathcal{A}_n) \to 1$ and the
$O_{\mathbb{P}}$ statements above persist, as in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{thm:confidence}.
Applying \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} at
$\bm{u} = \bm{h}/\sqrt{n}$ gives the second exact identity
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{\Sigma}\bracket{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}} - \bm{\Sigma}_0
  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{h})
  + \frac{1}{n}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T ,
\end{equation*}
so subtracting it from \eqref{eq:Sigma_hat_exact} and multiplying by
$\sqrt{n}$,
\begin{equation*}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}(\theta_{n,\bm{h}})}
  = \mathcal{D}_0\bracket{\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}}
  + \sqrt{n}\,\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{U}_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}^T
  - \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T
  \qquad \text{on } \mathcal{A}_n .
\end{equation*}
The last term is deterministic and $O(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$.
By \eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion} in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular},
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n - \bm{h}\rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$
under $\mathbb{P}^n_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}$; in particular
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n = O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ there, so the middle term is
again $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\frac{1}{2}})$ by the bound of part 1.
The continuous mapping theorem applied to $\mathcal{D}_0$ now gives
\eqref{eq:Sigma_regular}, the limit being the image of
$N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$, that is $\mathcal{D}_0(\bm{Z})$, and free of
$\bm{h}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What part 1 does and does not use]\label{rmk:Sigma_CLT_regularity}
Part 1 of Corollary~\ref{thm:Sigma_CLT} uses nothing from this section.
Its proof invokes Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, the exact expansion
\eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic}, Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} and
the continuous mapping theorem, and no more; neither the parallel
transport of Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport}, nor the transport
comparison of Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison}, nor the contiguity of
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity}, nor the local regularity of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} appears anywhere in it.
It could have been stated at the end of Section~\ref{sec:MLE}; it is
placed here only because part 2 belongs here, and because it is the
covariance level reading of the whole chapter.

The reason no transport is needed is worth isolating, because it is the
converse of the phenomenon that made this section necessary in the first
place.
The intrinsic error $\text{Exp}^{-1}_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}(\hat{\theta}_n)$
of Definition~\ref{def:regular} lives in
$T_{\theta_{n,\bm{h}}}M$, a vector space that \textit{moves with $n$},
which is exactly why $\Gamma_{\bm{h}/\sqrt{n}}$ has to be inserted before
any limit can be taken.
The covariance level error
$\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n - \bm{\Sigma}(\theta)$, by contrast, is a difference
of two elements of the single fixed vector space $\text{Sym}(p)$, for
every $\theta$ at once; there is nothing to transport, and the delta
method applies verbatim.
Curvature is invisible at the covariance level because
$\bm{\Sigma}(M) \subseteq \text{Sym}(p)$ is being viewed extrinsically.

What part 2 costs is correspondingly small: given
\eqref{eq:third_lemma_conclusion}, which is the substance of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} and does use
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} and Le Cam's third lemma, the passage to
\eqref{eq:Sigma_regular} is three lines of exact algebra.
One should resist reading more into part 2 than it says: it asserts that
the \textit{maximum likelihood} fitted covariance is locally regular as
an estimator of $\bm{\Sigma}(\theta)$, and nothing about other
estimators.
The corresponding lower bound would require the convolution theorem for
the differentiable functional $\theta\mapsto\bm{\Sigma}(\theta)$, which
is Theorem IV.1 of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026} rather than
Theorem~\ref{thm:convolution}, and which we do not develop.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rmk:what_is_added}
It may be useful to summarize what Section~\ref{sec:regularity} adds to
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, since the three groups of results answer three
different questions.

Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} is about \textit{other parameters}: it says
that the conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} is stable under
perturbations of the truth at the scale $n^{-\frac{1}{2}}$.
Nothing in \eqref{eq:MLE_linear}--\eqref{eq:MLE_CLT_procrustes} implies
this, as Remark~\ref{rmk:hodges} shows by exhibiting an estimator that
satisfies the analogue of \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} at every point of $M$ and is
not regular anywhere.

Theorems~\ref{thm:convolution} and \ref{thm:LAM} are about \textit{other
estimators}: they are lower bounds over a class of procedures, and are
therefore statements of a different kind from anything in
Section~\ref{sec:MLE}, which concerns one estimator only.
The two are complementary.
The convolution bound is sharp and distributional but restricted to
regular sequences; the local asymptotic minimax bound holds for every
estimator sequence whatsoever but only in the weaker, maximal-risk form.
It is worth noting where each hypothesis is used: the lower bound
\eqref{eq:LAM} needs nothing beyond Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} and
Proposition~\ref{thm:LAN}, so it was already within reach at the end of
Section~\ref{sec:MLE}; what needs Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} is the
\textit{attainment} \eqref{eq:LAM_attained}, because the supremum there
is taken over a neighbourhood that shrinks at the estimation scale and
therefore probes exactly the local alternatives.

Finally, Proposition~\ref{thm:parallel_transport} and
Lemmas~\ref{thm:uniform_chart}--\ref{thm:transport_comparison} are geometric
results about $(M,g)$ in their own right, independent of any statistics;
Lemma~\ref{thm:contiguity} is a probabilistic one.
They are what the two-base-point comparison costs, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, which lives entirely inside the single vector
space $T_{\theta_0}M$, needed none of them.

In the terminology of \cite{Sun_Lin_Liu_2026},
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_regular} verifies their Definition III.5 for the
probabilistic principal component analysis model,
Theorem~\ref{thm:convolution} is the instance of their Theorem III.1,
Theorem~\ref{thm:LAM} of their Theorem III.2, and the two together are
their Theorem V.1 for this model.
We have proved them here through the chart reduction of
Lemma~\ref{thm:chart_regularity} and the classical Euclidean theorems of
\cite{van_der_Vaart_1998}, rather than by citing the manifold versions,
because Lemma~\ref{thm:transport_comparison} makes the reduction exact
and because it isolates precisely where the curvature of $M$ is felt:
in the $O(\abs{\bm{u}}\abs{\bm{w}-\bm{u}})$ term of
\eqref{eq:transport_comparison}, and nowhere else.
\end{remark}


\section{The Fisher Information Operator in Closed Form}\label{sec:fisher_explicit}

Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} identifies the limiting covariance of the
maximum likelihood estimator as $G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$, and
Theorems~\ref{thm:convolution} and \ref{thm:LAM} identify the same
operator as the efficiency bound; but nothing so far says what
$G^{-1}_{\theta_0}$ \textit{is}.
This section computes it.
The only inputs are \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional}, \eqref{eq:score} and
\eqref{eq:G_def} of Section~\ref{sec:PPCA_properties}, the spectral
decomposition \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} of
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}, and the description
\eqref{eq:lift_def} of the horizontal lift; no asymptotics are used until
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates}, which merely reads
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} in the coordinates constructed here.
The computation follows Section 4 of the companion draft.

Two features of the answer deserve to be announced.
First, the whole calculation is a $2\times 2$ block computation in the
singular basis of $\bm{W}_0$, and it produces a set of coordinates in
which $G_{\theta_0}$ is block diagonal with blocks of size $1$, $1$ and
$q+1$; the only genuinely coupled block is the one that mixes the
$q$ leading directions with the noise variance, and it is inverted by a
one-line Schur complement.
Second, no assumption whatsoever is made on the multiplicities of the
singular values of $\bm{W}_0$: the formulas below are valid when
$\sigma_1(\bm{W}_0) = \cdots = \sigma_q(\bm{W}_0)$, a case in which
individual eigenvectors of $\bm{S}_n$ are not asymptotically normal at
all.
This is the quantitative form of the third comment of
Remark~\ref{rmk:MLE_CLT_comments}.

\subsection*{Block coordinates at the true parameter}

Throughout this section we keep the notation \eqref{eq:theta_notation},
we fix the singular value decomposition
$\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$ of
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum}, and we complete $\bm{U}_0$ to an
orthogonal matrix: we let
$\bm{U}_0^{\perp} \in \mathbb{R}^{p\times(p-q)}$ have orthonormal columns
spanning the orthogonal complement of the column space of $\bm{W}_0$, so
that

\begin{equation}\label{eq:J_0_def}
  \bm{J}_0 := \squared{\bm{U}_0 \ \ \bm{U}_0^{\perp}} \in O(p),
  \qquad
  \bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T .
\end{equation}
We abbreviate the eigenvalues of $\bm{\Sigma}_0$ supplied by
Lemma~\ref{thm:Sigma_0_spectrum} as

\begin{equation}\label{eq:tau_def}
  \tau_j := \lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0) = \sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_0^2
  > \sigma_0^2 \qquad (j = 1,\ldots,q),
  \qquad
  \bm{T}_0 := \bm{D}_0^2 + \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_q
  = \text{diag}(\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_q).
\end{equation}
The letters $\bm{B}$, $\bm{C}$, $\bm{\Psi}$, $\bm{y}$, $\bm{z}$,
$\bm{J}_0$, $\bm{T}_0$, $\tau_j$, $\kappa$ and $\mathcal{G}$ are local to
this section.

\begin{lemma}[Block form of the differential]\label{thm:block_form}
Let $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$ have horizontal lift
$\iota(\bm{h}) = (\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}, b_{\bm{h}})$ as in
\eqref{eq:lift_def}, and set

\begin{equation}\label{eq:B_C_Psi_def}
  \bm{B} := \bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{q\times q},
  \qquad
  \bm{C} := \bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0
  \in \mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q},
  \qquad
  \bm{\Psi} := \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 + \bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T}
  \in \text{Sym}(q).
\end{equation}
Then:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item $\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix} \bm{T}_0 & \bm{0}\\ \bm{0} & \sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q}\end{pmatrix}$
  and
  $\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix} \bm{T}_0^{-1} & \bm{0}\\ \bm{0} & \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{I}_{p-q}\end{pmatrix}$.
  \item $\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}
  = \bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T$
  and
  $\abs{\bm{h}}^2
  = \|\bm{B}\|_F^2 + \|\bm{C}\|_F^2 + b_{\bm{h}}^2$.
  \item $\bm{D}_0\bm{B} \in \text{Sym}(q)$; conversely every triple
  $(\bm{B},\bm{C},b)$ with $\bm{D}_0\bm{B}$ symmetric arises from exactly
  one $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$.
  \item In the basis \eqref{eq:J_0_def},
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:Dh_blocks}
    \bm{J}_0^T\, D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)\, \bm{J}_0
    = \begin{pmatrix}
      2\bm{\Psi} + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q & \bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T \\
      \bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_{p-q}
    \end{pmatrix}.
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
1. Substitute $\bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{U}_0^T$ and
$\bm{I}_p - \bm{P}_0 = \bm{U}_0^{\perp}(\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T$ into
\eqref{eq:Sigma_0_diagonalization} and use
$\bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_0 = \bm{I}_q$,
$(\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T\bm{U}_0^{\perp} = \bm{I}_{p-q}$,
$\bm{U}_0^T\bm{U}_0^{\perp} = \bm{0}$.

2. Since $\bm{J}_0 \in O(p)$ and $\bm{V}_0 \in O(q)$, the matrix
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{V}_0$ has blocks $\bm{B}$ and $\bm{C}$
stacked, which is the first identity; the Frobenius norm is invariant
under multiplication by orthogonal matrices on either side, so
$\|\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\|_F^2 = \|\bm{B}\|_F^2 + \|\bm{C}\|_F^2$, and the
second identity is \eqref{eq:lift_def}.

3. By part 3 of Proposition~\ref{thm:horizontal_verticle} the pair
$(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b_{\bm{h}})$ is horizontal at $(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)$
if and only if $\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T\bm{W}_0 = \bm{W}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}$.
Using part 2 and $\bm{W}_0 = \bm{U}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{V}_0^T$,
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{W}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}
  = \bm{V}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  \bracket{\bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T}
  = \bm{V}_0\bracket{\bm{D}_0\bm{B}}\bm{V}_0^T,
\end{equation*}
which is symmetric if and only if $\bm{D}_0\bm{B}$ is.
Conversely, given such a triple, part 2 defines $\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}$, the
displayed identity makes $(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b)$ horizontal, and
$\bm{h} := d\pi_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}(\bm{U}_{\bm{h}},b)$ is the unique
preimage because $\iota$ is a bijection onto
$H_{(\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2)}$.

4. By \eqref{eq:Sigma_directional},
$D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)
= \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T + \bm{W}_0\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}^T
+ b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p$.
By part 2,
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T
  = \bracket{\bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{V}_0^T + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{V}_0^T}
  \bm{V}_0\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  = \bm{U}_0\bm{B}\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T
  + \bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{U}_0^T,
\end{equation*}
so that
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\bm{W}_0^T\bm{J}_0
= \begin{pmatrix} \bm{B}\bm{D}_0 & \bm{0}\\
\bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & \bm{0}\end{pmatrix}$.
Adding the transpose of this matrix and $b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_p$, and using
$\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 + \bracket{\bm{B}\bm{D}_0}^T = 2\bm{\Psi}$, gives
\eqref{eq:Dh_blocks}.
\end{proof}

The three blocks have direct meanings, and it is worth recording them
before any computation.
By \eqref{eq:Dh_blocks} the matrix $2\bm{\Psi} + b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q$ is
the first order change of $\bm{\Sigma}$ inside the signal block, the
matrix $\bm{C}\bm{D}_0$ is its change in the signal--noise block, and
$b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_{p-q}$ is its change in the noise block --- where the
model forces the change to be a \textit{multiple of the identity},
which is the whole source of the efficiency gain computed below.
Note also that $\bm{\Psi}$, not $\bm{B}$, is the natural unknown: the
map $\bm{B}\mapsto\bm{\Psi}$ is exactly the operation that quotients out
the $\frac{q(q-1)}{2}$ directions along which $\bm{\Sigma}$ does not
move.

\begin{lemma}[Linear coordinates on $T_{\theta_0}M$]\label{thm:block_coords}
The map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kappa_def}
  \kappa : T_{\theta_0}M \longrightarrow
  \text{Sym}(q) \times \mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R},
  \qquad
  \kappa(\bm{h}) := \bracket{\bm{\Psi}, \bm{C}, b_{\bm{h}}}
\end{equation}
of \eqref{eq:B_C_Psi_def} is a linear isomorphism.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Linearity is clear: $\iota$ is linear by \eqref{eq:lift_def}, and
$\bm{U}_{\bm{h}}\mapsto(\bm{B},\bm{C})\mapsto\bm{\Psi}$ is a composition
of linear maps.

For injectivity, suppose $\kappa(\bm{h}) = \bm{0}$.
Then $b_{\bm{h}} = 0$ and $\bm{C} = \bm{0}$, and $\bm{\Psi} = \bm{0}$
reads $\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T$.
Since $\rank(\bm{W}_0) = q$, all $\sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)$ are strictly
positive and $\bm{D}_0$ is invertible, so part 3 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form}, that is $\bm{D}_0\bm{B} = \bm{B}^T\bm{D}_0$,
gives $\bm{B}^T = \bm{D}_0\bm{B}\bm{D}_0^{-1}$.
Substituting this into $\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0\bm{B}^T$ yields
$\bm{B}\bm{D}_0 = -\bm{D}_0^2\bm{B}\bm{D}_0^{-1}$, and multiplying on the
right by $\bm{D}_0$,
\begin{equation*}
  \bm{B}\bm{D}_0^2 + \bm{D}_0^2\bm{B} = \bm{0}.
\end{equation*}
Both $\bm{D}_0^2$ factors are diagonal, so the $(i,j)$ entry of the left
hand side is
$\bracket{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2 + \sigma_j(\bm{W}_0)^2}\bm{B}_{ij}$,
and the bracket is strictly positive; hence $\bm{B} = \bm{0}$.
By part 2 of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form} this forces
$\bm{U}_{\bm{h}} = \bm{0}$, so $\iota(\bm{h}) = (\bm{0},0)$ and
$\bm{h} = \bm{0}$.

For surjectivity it suffices to compare dimensions, since $\kappa$ is
injective and linear:
\begin{equation*}
  \dim\bracket{\text{Sym}(q)\times\mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R}}
  = \frac{q(q+1)}{2} + (p-q)q + 1
  = pq + 1 - \frac{q(q-1)}{2}
  = d
\end{equation*}
by \eqref{eq:Sigma_0_def}.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{thm:block_coords} lets us treat
$(\bm{\Psi},\bm{C},b)$ as coordinates on $T_{\theta_0}M$.
We use throughout the \textit{scalar} coordinates
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar_coords}
  \bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}_{1\leqslant i \leqslant j \leqslant q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\bm{C}_{ki}}_{1\leqslant k \leqslant p-q,\ 1\leqslant i \leqslant q},
  \qquad
  b ,
\end{equation}
which identify the target of $\kappa$ with $\mathbb{R}^d$, and we let
$\mathcal{G}$ denote the $d\times d$ symmetric matrix representing the
Fisher form in them, that is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gcal_def}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2)
  = \kappa(\bm{h}_1)^T\,\mathcal{G}\,\kappa(\bm{h}_2)
  \qquad \text{for all } \bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2 \in T_{\theta_0}M .
\end{equation}
Note that $\kappa$ is \textit{not} a $g_{\theta_0}$-isometry, so
$\mathcal{G}$ is not the matrix $\bm{G}$ of Step 1 of the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}; the relation between the two is recorded in
the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates} and is the only place
where it is needed.

\subsection*{The Fisher form and its inverse}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit Fisher form]\label{thm:G_explicit}
For every $\bm{h} \in T_{\theta_0}M$, in the notation
\eqref{eq:B_C_Psi_def},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_blocks}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h},\bm{h})
  = \frac{1}{2}\tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}
  \bracket{2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q}}^2}
  + \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}\tr\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}
  + \frac{(p-q)\,b_{\bm{h}}^2}{2\sigma_0^4},
\end{equation}
and, in the scalar coordinates \eqref{eq:scalar_coords},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:G_scalar}
  G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h},\bm{h})
  = \underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^q \frac{\bracket{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b_{\bm{h}}}^2}{2\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{(p-q)b_{\bm{h}}^2}{2\sigma_0^4}}_{\text{coupled block}}
  \ + \underbrace{\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant q}\frac{4\bm{\Psi}_{ij}^2}{\tau_i\tau_j}}_{\bm{\Psi}\text{ off-diagonal}}
  \ + \underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^q\bracket{\frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}}
  \|\bm{C}\bm{e}_i\|^2}_{\bm{C}\text{-block}} .
\end{equation}
In particular $\mathcal{G}$ is block diagonal with respect to the
partition of \eqref{eq:scalar_coords} into the three groups
$\curly{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}_{i<j}$, $\curly{\bm{C}_{ki}}$ and
$\bracket{\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b}$, with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gcal_blocks}
  \mathcal{G}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}\bm{\Psi}_{ij}} = \frac{4}{\tau_i\tau_j}
  \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \mathcal{G}_{\bm{C}_{ki}\bm{C}_{ki}} = \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}
  = \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i},
  \qquad
  \mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}
  = \begin{pmatrix}
  \text{diag}\bracket{2\tau_i^{-2}} & \bm{v} \\
  \bm{v}^T & \gamma
  \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
the last being the coupled block in the ordering
$(\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b)$, where

\begin{equation}\label{eq:v_gamma_def}
  \bm{v} := \bracket{\tau_1^{-2},\ldots,\tau_q^{-2}}^T \in \mathbb{R}^q,
  \qquad
  \gamma := \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4} .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\bm{M} := 2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q$ and $b := b_{\bm{h}}$.
Conjugating by $\bm{J}_0$ does not change traces, so by
\eqref{eq:G_def} with $\bm{h}_1 = \bm{h}_2 = \bm{h}$ it suffices to
compute the trace of the square of
$\bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)\bm{J}_0$.
Multiplying the two matrices of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigma_inv_Dh}
  \bm{J}_0^T\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix}
    \bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M} & \bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T \\
    \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0 & \sigma_0^{-2}b\,\bm{I}_{p-q}
  \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
The two diagonal blocks of the square of \eqref{eq:Sigma_inv_Dh} are
\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2
  + \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T\bm{C}\bm{D}_0
  \qquad \text{and} \qquad
  \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T
  + \sigma_0^{-4}b^2\bm{I}_{p-q},
\end{equation*}
so, using the cyclic property of the trace and the fact that the
diagonal matrices $\bm{T}_0$ and $\bm{D}_0$ commute,
\begin{equation*}
  \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)}^2}
  = \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2}
  + \frac{2}{\sigma_0^2}\tr\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}
  + \frac{(p-q)b^2}{\sigma_0^4} .
\end{equation*}
Halving gives \eqref{eq:G_blocks}.

For \eqref{eq:G_scalar}, both $\bm{T}_0^{-1}$ and $\bm{D}_0^2$ are
diagonal.
Hence
$\tr(\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{D}_0^2\bm{C}^T\bm{C})
= \sum_{i=1}^q \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\tau_i}
\bracket{\bm{C}^T\bm{C}}_{ii}
= \sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\tau_i}\|\bm{C}\bm{e}_i\|^2$,
and $\frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}
= \frac{\tau_i - \sigma_0^2}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}
= \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}$ by \eqref{eq:tau_def}.
For the first term, $\bm{M}$ is symmetric, so
\begin{equation*}
  \tr\squared{\bracket{\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{M}}^2}
  = \sum_{i,j=1}^q\frac{\bm{M}_{ij}\bm{M}_{ji}}{\tau_i\tau_j}
  = \sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{M}_{ii}^2}{\tau_i^2}
  + 2\sum_{i<j}\frac{\bm{M}_{ij}^2}{\tau_i\tau_j},
\end{equation*}
and $\bm{M}_{ii} = 2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b$, $\bm{M}_{ij} = 2\bm{\Psi}_{ij}$
for $i\neq j$; halving gives the first two groups of terms in
\eqref{eq:G_scalar}.

Finally, \eqref{eq:G_scalar} is a sum of three quadratic forms in
\textit{disjoint} sets of the scalar coordinates
\eqref{eq:scalar_coords}, which is exactly the assertion of block
diagonality; reading off the coefficients gives the first two entries of
\eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}.
For the third, expanding the coupled part of \eqref{eq:G_scalar},
\begin{equation*}
  \sum_{i}\frac{\bracket{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}+b}^2}{2\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{(p-q)b^2}{2\sigma_0^4}
  = \sum_i \frac{2\bm{\Psi}_{ii}^2}{\tau_i^2}
  + 2b\sum_i\frac{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}{\tau_i^2}
  + b^2\bracket{\frac{1}{2}\sum_i\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4}},
\end{equation*}
whose matrix in the ordering $(\bm{\Psi}_{11},\ldots,\bm{\Psi}_{qq},b)$
is $\mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}$ of \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}: the coefficient of
$\bm{\Psi}_{ii}^2$ is $2\tau_i^{-2}$, that of $b\,\bm{\Psi}_{ii}$ is
$2\tau_i^{-2} = 2\bm{v}_i$, and that of $b^2$ is $\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit inverse]\label{thm:G_inverse_explicit}
The matrix $\mathcal{G}$ of \eqref{eq:Gcal_def} is invertible, and
$\mathcal{G}^{-1}$ is block diagonal with respect to the same partition,
with

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ginv_diagonal}
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}
  = \frac{\tau_i\tau_j}{4} \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{C}_{ki}\bm{C}_{ki}}
  = \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}
  = \bracket{\frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}-\frac{1}{\tau_i}}^{-1},
\end{equation}
these two groups being diagonal, and, on the coupled block,

\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ginv_coupled}
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{bb} = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}b} = -\frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \bracket{\mathcal{G}^{-1}}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}\bm{\Psi}_{jj}}
  = \delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2} + \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)} .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$\mathcal{G}$ is positive definite because $G_{\theta_0}$ is, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM}, and $\kappa$ is a bijection; so it is invertible,
and the inverse of a block diagonal matrix is block diagonal with the
inverted blocks.
The first two groups are diagonal by \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}, and their
inverses are the reciprocals, which is \eqref{eq:Ginv_diagonal}.

For the coupled block write
$\mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}
= \begin{pmatrix}\bm{P} & \bm{v}\\ \bm{v}^T & \gamma\end{pmatrix}$
with $\bm{P} := \text{diag}(2\tau_i^{-2})$ as in
\eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}.
Then $\bm{P}^{-1} = \text{diag}(\tau_i^2/2)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Pinv_v}
  \bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}
  = \bracket{\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}}_{i=1}^q
  = \frac{1}{2}\bm{1}_q ,
\end{equation}
so the Schur complement of $\bm{P}$ in $\mathcal{G}_{\text{c}}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur}
  \varsigma := \gamma - \bm{v}^T\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}
  = \gamma - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{1}{\tau_i^2}
  = \frac{p-q}{2\sigma_0^4}
\end{equation}
by \eqref{eq:v_gamma_def}; it is strictly positive because $q<p$, which
is where the standing assumption $q<p$ enters the inversion.
The standard block inversion formula
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{pmatrix}\bm{P} & \bm{v}\\ \bm{v}^T & \gamma\end{pmatrix}^{-1}
  = \begin{pmatrix}
  \bm{P}^{-1} + \varsigma^{-1}\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}\bm{v}^T\bm{P}^{-1}
  & -\varsigma^{-1}\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v} \\
  -\varsigma^{-1}\bm{v}^T\bm{P}^{-1} & \varsigma^{-1}
  \end{pmatrix}
\end{equation*}
together with \eqref{eq:Pinv_v} and \eqref{eq:schur} gives
$\varsigma^{-1} = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$, then
$-\varsigma^{-1}\bracket{\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}}_i
= -\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\cdot\frac{1}{2}
= -\frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$, and finally
\begin{equation*}
  \bracket{\bm{P}^{-1}}_{ij}
  + \varsigma^{-1}\bracket{\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}}_i\bracket{\bm{P}^{-1}\bm{v}}_j
  = \delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}
  + \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}
  = \delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2} + \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)},
\end{equation*}
which is \eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} in block coordinates]\label{thm:MLE_CLT_coordinates}
Let $1\leqslant q<p$ and $\theta_0 = [\bm{W}_0,\sigma_0^2] \in M$.
Define, on the event $\mathcal{A}_n$ of \eqref{eq:e_n_def}, the aligned
error blocks

\begin{equation}\label{eq:error_blocks}
  \begin{gathered}
  \bm{B}_n := \bm{U}_0^T\bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0}\bm{V}_0,
  \qquad
  \bm{C}_n := \bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T
  \bracket{\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n - \bm{W}_0}\bm{V}_0,
  \\
  \bm{\Psi}_n := \frac{1}{2}\bracket{\bm{B}_n\bm{D}_0+\bm{D}_0\bm{B}_n^T},
  \qquad
  b_n := \hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \sigma_0^2,
  \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
and $(\bm{\Psi}_n,\bm{C}_n,b_n) := (\bm{0},\bm{0},0)$ off
$\mathcal{A}_n$.
Then

\begin{equation}\label{eq:CLT_coordinates}
  \sqrt{n}\bracket{\bm{\Psi}_n, \bm{C}_n, b_n}
  \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{\bm{0}, \mathcal{G}^{-1}}
  \qquad \text{in } \text{Sym}(q)\times\mathbb{R}^{(p-q)\times q}\times\mathbb{R},
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal{G}^{-1}$ given by Proposition~\ref{thm:G_inverse_explicit}.
Explicitly, writing
$\bm{Z} = (\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}},\bm{Z}_{\bm{C}},Z_b)$ for the limit:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item the $(p-q)q$ entries of $\bm{Z}_{\bm{C}}$ are mutually
  independent, independent of $(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}},Z_b)$, and
  $\text{Var}\bracket{(\bm{Z}_{\bm{C}})_{ki}}
  = \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}$;
  \item the $\frac{q(q-1)}{2}$ strictly upper triangular entries of
  $\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}}$ are mutually independent, independent of
  everything else, and
  $\text{Var}\bracket{(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}})_{ij}} = \frac{\tau_i\tau_j}{4}$
  for $i<j$;
  \item the remaining $q+1$ coordinates
  $\bracket{(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}})_{11},\ldots,(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}})_{qq},Z_b}$
  are jointly Gaussian with the covariances \eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled}; in
  particular
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:sigma2_CLT}
    \sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\sigma}_n^2 - \sigma_0^2}
    \rightsquigarrow N\bracket{0, \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}} .
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:error_welldefined} we have
$\iota(\hat{\bm{u}}_n) = \bm{e}_n
= (\hat{\bm{W}}_n\bm{Q}_n-\bm{W}_0,\ \hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2)$ on
$\mathcal{A}_n$, so by \eqref{eq:B_C_Psi_def} the triple
$(\bm{\Psi}_n,\bm{C}_n,b_n)$ of \eqref{eq:error_blocks} is exactly
$\kappa(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ there, and both vanish off $\mathcal{A}_n$
because $\hat{\bm{u}}_n = \bm{0}$ there by \eqref{eq:e_n_def}.
Hence $(\bm{\Psi}_n,\bm{C}_n,b_n) = \kappa(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ identically.

Let $\bm{K}$ denote the matrix of $\kappa$ with respect to the
$g_{\theta_0}$-orthonormal basis $\bm{e}_1,\ldots,\bm{e}_d$ of Step 1 of
the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT} on the source and the scalar
coordinates \eqref{eq:scalar_coords} on the target.
Comparing \eqref{eq:Gcal_def} with $\bm{G}_{jk} = G_{\theta_0}(\bm{e}_j,\bm{e}_k)$
gives $\bm{G} = \bm{K}^T\mathcal{G}\bm{K}$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pushforward}
  \bm{K}\bm{G}^{-1}\bm{K}^T
  = \bm{K}\bracket{\bm{K}^T\mathcal{G}\bm{K}}^{-1}\bm{K}^T
  = \bm{K}\bm{K}^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{-1}\bm{K}^{-T}\bm{K}^T
  = \mathcal{G}^{-1} .
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:MLE_CLT} we have
$\sqrt{n}\hat{\bm{u}}_n \rightsquigarrow N(\bm{0},\bm{G}^{-1})$ in the
coordinates of that basis, so the continuous mapping theorem applied to
the linear map $\bm{K}$ and \eqref{eq:pushforward} give
\eqref{eq:CLT_coordinates}.

The three assertions are then read off
Proposition~\ref{thm:G_inverse_explicit}: a Gaussian vector whose
covariance matrix is block diagonal has independent blocks, and within
the first two groups the blocks are diagonal, so the coordinates are
mutually independent.
Statement \eqref{eq:sigma2_CLT} is the $(b,b)$ entry of
\eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled} together with $b_n = \hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2$.
\end{proof}

\subsection*{The score and the influence function in coordinates}

The same block decomposition makes the score \eqref{eq:score} and the
influence operator \eqref{eq:IF_def} completely explicit.
Throughout, for $\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^p$ we write

\begin{equation}\label{eq:y_z_def}
  \bm{y} := \bm{U}_0^T\bm{x} \in \mathbb{R}^q,
  \qquad
  \bm{z} := \bracket{\bm{U}_0^{\perp}}^T\bm{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{p-q},
\end{equation}
so that, by part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form}, when
$\bm{X}\sim N_p(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_0)$ the vectors
$\bm{y} = \bm{U}_0^T\bm{X}$ and $\bm{z} = (\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T\bm{X}$ are
independent, $\bm{y}\sim N_q(\bm{0},\bm{T}_0)$ and
$\bm{z}\sim N_{p-q}(\bm{0},\sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q})$.

\begin{proposition}[Score and influence function in coordinates]\label{thm:score_coords}
Let $\bm{s}(\bm{x}) \in \mathbb{R}^d$ be the coordinate vector of the
score, defined by
$s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{h}) = \bm{s}(\bm{x})^T\kappa(\bm{h})$ for all
$\bm{h}\in T_{\theta_0}M$.
Then, in the scalar coordinates \eqref{eq:scalar_coords},

\begin{equation}\label{eq:score_coords}
  \bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}} = \frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{\tau_i^2},
  \qquad
  \bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}} = \frac{2\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j}{\tau_i\tau_j}
  \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \bm{s}_{\bm{C}_{ki}} = \frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)\,\bm{y}_i\bm{z}_k}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:score_b}
  \bm{s}_b = \frac{1}{2}\squared{
  \sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{\|\bm{z}\|^2-(p-q)\sigma_0^2}{\sigma_0^4}} .
\end{equation}
Moreover $\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}(\bm{X})] = \bm{0}$ and
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}(\bm{X})\bm{s}(\bm{X})^T] = \mathcal{G}$, the influence
operator \eqref{eq:IF_def} has coordinate vector
$\kappa(\text{IF}(\bm{x})) = \mathcal{G}^{-1}\bm{s}(\bm{x})$, and
explicitly

\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_coords}
  \text{IF}_b(\bm{x})
  = \frac{\bm{x}^T\bracket{\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0}\bm{x} - (p-q)\sigma_0^2}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \text{IF}_{\bm{C}_{ki}}(\bm{x}) = \frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{z}_k}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)},
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_Psi}
  \text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}(\bm{x}) = \frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j}{2}
  \quad (i<j),
  \qquad
  \text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}(\bm{x})
  = \frac{1}{2}\squared{\bm{y}_i^2 - \tau_i - \text{IF}_b(\bm{x})} .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Abbreviate $\bm{E} := \bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}
\squared{\bm{x}\bm{x}^T-\bm{\Sigma}_0}\bm{\Sigma}_0^{-1}$, so that
\eqref{eq:score} reads
$s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{h}) = \frac{1}{2}\tr\curly{\bm{E}
D_{\bm{h}}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)}$.
Since $\bm{J}_0^T\bm{x} = (\bm{y}^T,\bm{z}^T)^T$ by
\eqref{eq:y_z_def}, part 1 of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:E_blocks}
  \bm{J}_0^T\bm{E}\bm{J}_0
  = \begin{pmatrix}
    \bm{T}_0^{-1}\bracket{\bm{y}\bm{y}^T-\bm{T}_0}\bm{T}_0^{-1}
    & \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{y}\bm{z}^T \\
    \sigma_0^{-2}\bm{z}\bm{y}^T\bm{T}_0^{-1}
    & \sigma_0^{-4}\bracket{\bm{z}\bm{z}^T-\sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q}}
  \end{pmatrix}
  =: \begin{pmatrix}\bm{E}_{11} & \bm{E}_{12}\\
  \bm{E}_{12}^T & \bm{E}_{22}\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Pairing \eqref{eq:E_blocks} with \eqref{eq:Dh_blocks}, the two
off-diagonal contributions to the trace are equal, because
$\tr(\bm{E}_{12}^T\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T)
= \tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{E}_{12}^T\bm{D}_0\bm{C}^T}^T}
= \tr\bracket{\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{E}_{12}}
= \tr\bracket{\bm{E}_{12}\bm{C}\bm{D}_0}$, and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:score_expanded}
  2\,s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{h})
  = \tr\bracket{\bm{E}_{11}\squared{2\bm{\Psi}+b_{\bm{h}}\bm{I}_q}}
  + \frac{2}{\sigma_0^2}\,\bm{z}^T\bm{C}\bm{D}_0\bm{T}_0^{-1}\bm{y}
  + b_{\bm{h}}\tr\bm{E}_{22} .
\end{equation}
Now read off the coefficient of each scalar coordinate.
The matrix $\bm{\Psi}$ is symmetric, so
$\tr(\bm{E}_{11}\bm{\Psi}) = \sum_i (\bm{E}_{11})_{ii}\bm{\Psi}_{ii}
+ 2\sum_{i<j}(\bm{E}_{11})_{ij}\bm{\Psi}_{ij}$, while
$(\bm{E}_{11})_{ii} = \frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{\tau_i^2}$ and
$(\bm{E}_{11})_{ij} = \frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j}{\tau_i\tau_j}$ for
$i\neq j$; dividing the resulting $2\tr(\bm{E}_{11}\bm{\Psi})$ by the
factor $2$ in \eqref{eq:score_expanded} gives the first two formulas of
\eqref{eq:score_coords}.
The second term of \eqref{eq:score_expanded} equals
$\frac{2}{\sigma_0^2}\sum_{k,i}\bm{C}_{ki}\bm{z}_k
\frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)\bm{y}_i}{\tau_i}$, which after division by $2$
gives the third formula of \eqref{eq:score_coords}.
The coefficient of $b_{\bm{h}}$ is
$\frac{1}{2}\squared{\tr\bm{E}_{11}+\tr\bm{E}_{22}}$, which is
\eqref{eq:score_b}.

The identities $\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}]=\bm{0}$ and
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}\bm{s}^T]=\mathcal{G}$ are Theorem~\ref{thm:DQM} read
through \eqref{eq:Gcal_def}, since for all
$\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2 \in T_{\theta_0}M$
\begin{equation*}
  \mathbb{E}\squared{s(\bm{X};\theta_0)(\bm{h}_1)\,s(\bm{X};\theta_0)(\bm{h}_2)}
  = G_{\theta_0}(\bm{h}_1,\bm{h}_2)
  = \kappa(\bm{h}_1)^T\mathcal{G}\kappa(\bm{h}_2)
\end{equation*}
and $\kappa$ is onto.
(They can also be checked directly from \eqref{eq:score_coords}: with
$\bm{y}$ and $\bm{z}$ independent centred Gaussians,
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}^2] = \text{Var}(\bm{y}_i^2)/\tau_i^4
= 2/\tau_i^2$,
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}^2] = 4/(\tau_i\tau_j)$,
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}_{\bm{C}_{ki}}^2]
= \sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2\tau_i\sigma_0^2/(\sigma_0^4\tau_i^2)
= \sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2/(\sigma_0^2\tau_i)$,
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}\bm{s}_b] = 1/\tau_i^2$ and
$\mathbb{E}[\bm{s}_b^2] = \gamma$, every product containing an odd power
of a coordinate of $\bm{z}$ having mean zero; these are exactly the
entries \eqref{eq:Gcal_blocks}.)

By \eqref{eq:G_inverse_def} the vector $\text{IF}(\bm{x})$ is
characterized by
$G_{\theta_0}(\text{IF}(\bm{x}),\bm{h}) = s(\bm{x};\theta_0)(\bm{h})$
for all $\bm{h}$, that is, by \eqref{eq:Gcal_def},
$\kappa(\text{IF}(\bm{x}))^T\mathcal{G}\kappa(\bm{h})
= \bm{s}(\bm{x})^T\kappa(\bm{h})$ for all $\bm{h}$; since $\kappa$ is
onto, $\mathcal{G}\kappa(\text{IF}(\bm{x})) = \bm{s}(\bm{x})$.

It remains to compute $\mathcal{G}^{-1}\bm{s}$ block by block.
For $i<j$, by \eqref{eq:Ginv_diagonal},
$\text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ij}}
= \frac{\tau_i\tau_j}{4}\cdot\frac{2\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j}{\tau_i\tau_j}
= \frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j}{2}$, and
$\text{IF}_{\bm{C}_{ki}}
= \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}
\cdot\frac{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)\bm{y}_i\bm{z}_k}{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}
= \frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{z}_k}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)}$, which are the second
formula of \eqref{eq:IF_coords} and the first of \eqref{eq:IF_Psi}.
On the coupled block, \eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled} and \eqref{eq:score_b}
give
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{split}
  \text{IF}_b
  & = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\,\bm{s}_b
  - \frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\sum_{i=1}^q \bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}
  \\ & = \frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{\tau_i^2}
  + \frac{\|\bm{z}\|^2-(p-q)\sigma_0^2}{p-q}
  - \frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{\tau_i^2}
  = \frac{\|\bm{z}\|^2-(p-q)\sigma_0^2}{p-q},
  \end{split}
\end{equation*}
the $\bm{y}$ terms cancelling identically; since
$\|\bm{z}\|^2 = \bm{x}^T\bm{U}_0^{\perp}(\bm{U}_0^{\perp})^T\bm{x}
= \bm{x}^T(\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0)\bm{x}$ by \eqref{eq:J_0_def}, this is the
first formula of \eqref{eq:IF_coords}.
Similarly
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{split}
  \text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}_{ii}}
  & = \sum_{j=1}^q\squared{\delta_{ij}\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}
  + \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)}}\bm{s}_{\bm{\Psi}_{jj}}
  - \frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}\,\bm{s}_b
  \\ & = \frac{\bm{y}_i^2-\tau_i}{2}
  + \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)}\sum_{j=1}^q\frac{\bm{y}_j^2-\tau_j}{\tau_j^2}
  - \frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)}\sum_{j=1}^q\frac{\bm{y}_j^2-\tau_j}{\tau_j^2}
  - \frac{\|\bm{z}\|^2-(p-q)\sigma_0^2}{2(p-q)},
  \end{split}
\end{equation*}
which is the second formula of \eqref{eq:IF_Psi}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The noise variance]\label{thm:sigma2_summary}
The maximum likelihood estimator of the noise variance satisfies
\eqref{eq:sigma2_CLT}, and it is asymptotically linear with influence
function

\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_sigma2}
  \text{IF}_{\sigma^2}(\bm{x})
  = \frac{\bm{x}^T\bracket{\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0}\bm{x} - (p-q)\sigma_0^2}{p-q},
  \qquad
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\text{IF}_{\sigma^2}(\bm{X})} = 0,
  \qquad
  \mathbb{E}\squared{\text{IF}_{\sigma^2}(\bm{X})^2}
  = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}.
\end{equation}
Consequently $\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$ cannot be improved: by
Theorem~\ref{thm:convolution} the limit law of any locally regular
estimator sequence of $\theta_0$ is
$N\bracket{\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0}}*\Lambda$, so the noise variance
component of such a sequence has limit law
$N\bracket{0,\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}}*\Lambda_b$ for some probability
measure $\Lambda_b$ on $\mathbb{R}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The weak limit is \eqref{eq:sigma2_CLT}.
The influence function is the first formula of \eqref{eq:IF_coords},
because $b_n = \hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2$ is the $b$ coordinate of
$\kappa(\hat{\bm{u}}_n)$ by \eqref{eq:error_blocks}, so that the
$b$ coordinate of \eqref{eq:MLE_linear} reads
$\sqrt{n}(\hat{\sigma}_n^2-\sigma_0^2)
= n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^n \text{IF}_b(\bm{X}_i)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$.
For the moments, $\bm{z}\sim N_{p-q}(\bm{0},\sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q})$ gives
$\mathbb{E}\|\bm{z}\|^2 = (p-q)\sigma_0^2$ and
$\text{Var}(\|\bm{z}\|^2) = 2(p-q)\sigma_0^4$, whence
$\mathbb{E}[\text{IF}_{\sigma^2}^2]
= \frac{2(p-q)\sigma_0^4}{(p-q)^2} = \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$,
consistent with \eqref{eq:sigma2_CLT} as it must be, since the asymptotic
covariance of an asymptotically linear sequence is the covariance of its
influence function.
For the last assertion, a linear map carries a convolution of two laws
to the convolution of their images, so it suffices to push
$L = N(\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0})*\Lambda$ forward by the linear map
$\bm{h}\mapsto b_{\bm{h}}$, which by \eqref{eq:pushforward} sends
$N(\bm{0},G^{-1}_{\theta_0})$ to
$N\bracket{0,(\mathcal{G}^{-1})_{bb}}
= N\bracket{0,\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}}$ by \eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Where the model helps, and where it does not]\label{rmk:IF_vs_S_n}
Formulas \eqref{eq:IF_coords}--\eqref{eq:IF_Psi} answer a question the
asymptotic theory alone does not: in which directions is the constrained
estimator $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n
:= \bm{\Sigma}([\hat{\bm{W}}_n,\hat{\sigma}_n^2])$ actually better than
the sample covariance matrix $\bm{S}_n$?
The answer is: in exactly one block, and the reason is
Proposition~\ref{thm:fitted_covariance}.

By \eqref{eq:Sigma_u_quadratic} the quadratic term of
$\bm{\Sigma}_{\bm{u}}-\bm{\Sigma}_0$ is $O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})$ under
Theorem~\ref{thm:MLE_CLT}, so
$\sqrt{n}\bracket{\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n-\bm{\Sigma}_0}
= \sqrt{n}D_{\hat{\bm{u}}_n}\bm{\Sigma}(\theta_0)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)$ and
the influence function of $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}_n$ is, by
\eqref{eq:Dh_blocks}, the matrix with blocks
$2\text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}}+\text{IF}_b\bm{I}_q$,
$\text{IF}_{\bm{C}}\bm{D}_0$ and $\text{IF}_b\bm{I}_{p-q}$.
By \eqref{eq:IF_coords}--\eqref{eq:IF_Psi} these are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:IF_Sigma_blocks}
  \bracket{2\text{IF}_{\bm{\Psi}}+\text{IF}_b\bm{I}_q}_{ij}
  = \bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j - \delta_{ij}\tau_i,
  \qquad
  \bracket{\text{IF}_{\bm{C}}\bm{D}_0}_{ki}
  = \sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)\frac{\bm{y}_i\bm{z}_k}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)}
  = \bm{z}_k\bm{y}_i,
  \qquad
  \text{IF}_b\bm{I}_{p-q},
\end{equation}
the diagonal case of the first identity being exactly the cancellation
of $\text{IF}_b$ in the second formula of \eqref{eq:IF_Psi}.
Now the influence function of $\bm{S}_n$, read in the same basis
\eqref{eq:J_0_def}, is $\bm{J}_0^T(\bm{x}\bm{x}^T-\bm{\Sigma}_0)\bm{J}_0$,
whose blocks are
$\bm{y}\bm{y}^T-\bm{T}_0$, $\bm{z}\bm{y}^T$ and
$\bm{z}\bm{z}^T-\sigma_0^2\bm{I}_{p-q}$.
The first two coincide with \eqref{eq:IF_Sigma_blocks}: \textit{to first
order the maximum likelihood fit reproduces the sample covariance matrix
in the signal block and in the signal--noise block, and gains nothing
there}.
Only the trailing block differs, where $\bm{z}\bm{z}^T-\sigma_0^2\bm{I}$
is replaced by its average
$\frac{\tr(\bm{z}\bm{z}^T-\sigma_0^2\bm{I})}{p-q}\bm{I}_{p-q}
= \text{IF}_b\bm{I}_{p-q}$: the isotropy constraint of the model averages
the $p-q$ noise directions, dividing the variance by $p-q$.
This is the infinitesimal shadow of the exact identity
\eqref{eq:fitted_covariance} of
Proposition~\ref{thm:fitted_covariance}, which says the same thing
non-asymptotically: $\hat{\bm{\Sigma}}$ differs from $\bm{S}$ only
through the trailing eigenvalues, which it replaces by their mean.
It also explains why the leading block cannot be improved: by
Proposition~\ref{thm:least_squares} the model imposes no constraint
there at all.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Cross-checks against classical principal component
analysis]\label{rmk:pca_crosschecks}
Three consistency checks, each of which recovers a classical formula and
none of which is used elsewhere.

\textbf{1. Pooled noise eigenvalues.}
The variance $\frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}$ of \eqref{eq:sigma2_CLT} is the
classical asymptotic variance of the average of the $p-q$ sample
eigenvalues attached to a population eigenvalue of multiplicity $p-q$;
indeed $\hat{\sigma}_n^2 = \frac{1}{p-q}\sum_{j>q}\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)$
exactly, by \eqref{eq:TB_formula}, and
$\sum_{j>q}\lambda_j(\bm{S}_n)
= \tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n)}\bm{S}_n}$,
in which $\bm{P}_q(\bm{S}_n)$ may be replaced by $\bm{P}_0$ at a cost of
$O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})$, by Lemma~\ref{thm:riesz} and part 5 of
Lemma~\ref{thm:S_n_properties}; the statistic that remains is
$\frac{1}{p-q}\tr\bracket{\bracket{\bm{I}_p-\bm{P}_0}\bm{S}_n}$, whose
centred and $\sqrt{n}$-scaled version is exactly the average of the
influence functions \eqref{eq:IF_sigma2}.

\textbf{2. The leading block is not improved.}
By \eqref{eq:Ginv_coupled},
\begin{equation*}
  \text{Var}\bracket{2(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}})_{ii}+Z_b}
  = 4\squared{\frac{\tau_i^2}{2}+\frac{\sigma_0^4}{2(p-q)}}
  + 4\squared{-\frac{\sigma_0^4}{p-q}} + \frac{2\sigma_0^4}{p-q}
  = 2\tau_i^2
  = \text{Var}\bracket{\bracket{\bm{U}_0^T\bm{X}}_i^2},
\end{equation*}
and likewise
$\text{Var}(2(\bm{Z}_{\bm{\Psi}})_{ij}) = \tau_i\tau_j
= \text{Var}(\bm{y}_i\bm{y}_j)$ for $i<j$ --- the sample covariance
variances for those coordinates, as Remark~\ref{rmk:IF_vs_S_n} requires.

\textbf{3. Anderson's eigenvector variances.}
Suppose the $\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)$ are simple.
By part 2 of Lemma~\ref{thm:block_form}, the component of the aligned
loading error orthogonal to the column space of $\bm{W}_0$ is
$\bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}_n\bm{V}_0^T$, so the tilt of the estimated
$i$-th direction out of the signal subspace is
$\bm{U}_0^{\perp}\bm{C}_n\bm{e}_i/\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)$, with limiting
per-coordinate variance
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{1}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}\cdot
  \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)^2}
  = \frac{\sigma_0^2\tau_i}{\bracket{\tau_i-\sigma_0^2}^2}
  = \frac{\lambda_i(\bm{\Sigma}_0)\lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0)}
  {\bracket{\lambda_i(\bm{\Sigma}_0)-\lambda_j(\bm{\Sigma}_0)}^2}
  \quad \text{for } j>q,
\end{equation*}
which is Anderson's formula for the noise directions.
When the $\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)$ are repeated this eigenvector-by-eigenvector
reading breaks down --- individual eigenvectors are then not
asymptotically normal --- but nothing in
Propositions~\ref{thm:G_explicit} and \ref{thm:G_inverse_explicit} is
affected: no formula in this section divides by a difference of singular
values, and the only inverted quantities are $\tau_i$, $\sigma_0^2$,
$\sigma_i(\bm{W}_0)$ and $p-q$, all strictly positive under the standing
assumptions $\rank(\bm{W}_0)=q$, $\sigma_0^2>0$ and $q<p$.
This is the promised quantitative form of the third comment of
Remark~\ref{rmk:MLE_CLT_comments}.
\end{remark}
